     2-36.  Advantages for the SBCT in this formation include but are not limited to:
            z More firepower forward in the SBCT formation.
            z The ability to identify and engage armored enemy formations early reducing the time from sensor

                  to shooter to neutralize the enemy.
            z The ability to break contact (range and firepower) if the Cavalry squadron becomes decisively

                  engaged.
            z Allows the scout element to remain stealthy and the SBCT weapons troop element to engage to

                  target at long range.
            z Provides the Cavalry squadron overmatch with firepower in the counter reconnaissance fight.

2-8         ATP 3-21.91                                                                         11 May 2017


---

                          Reconnaissance and Security

       z The ability to fight for information if needed.
       z The ability to seize and retain key terrain METT-TC dependent.
       z Allows the weapons troop to establish conditions for follow on forces such as the Infantry

            battalion. Sets the conditions far forward in advance preventing the enemy’s ability to disrupt the
            main effort.

2-37.  Disadvantages for the SBCT in this formation include but are not limited to:
       z Compromising the main firepower of the SBCT potentially leaving the Infantry Battalions with

             less capability at the SBCT’s decisive point.
       z The additional firepower may influence the Cavalry squadron commander to issue different

             engagement criteria encouraging the Cavalry formation for engage more enemy.
       z The logistics package for the assigned forward support company (FSC) may not support the SBCT

             weapons troop with specialty skills such as ammunition handling, TOW and 105mm main gun
             repair and maintenance.
       z The task organization change may compromised due to METT-TC factors.

PREPARE

2-38. Preparation is simultaneous with planning for the SBCT weapons troop during the reconnaissance and
security phase. Every Soldier must be gainfully employed in either conducting analysis, coordination with
other elements in support of planning; or they must conduct pre-combat checks, acquire necessary classes of
supply, or maintenance. Priorities of work and standard operating procedures must be efficient during this
phase in order to gain and maintain initiative.

2-39. The final actions of preparation include rehearsals. At a minimum, combined arms rehearsal, medical
evacuation and vehicle recovery should be rehearsed by the SBCT weapons troop and its elements. Focus,
tempo, engagement and displacement criteria must be stated, refined, and confirmed by participating
members. Intelligence updates will be continuous throughout the preparation phase and final incorporation
into the plan can occur during the rehearsal.

EXECUTE

2-40. Execution phase begins with movement to the line of departure for the operation. It culminates upon
either a transition to another phase of the SBCT operation, task organization change, changes in the enemy
situation or operating area, achievement of the reconnaissance objectives, or directed guidance by the SBCT
commander. During the reconnaissance the SBCT weapons troop will follow and support the Cavalry
squadron with long range direct fire weapon systems capable of acquiring and engaging targets. The SBCT
weapons troop organizes itself according to the higher echelons reconnaissance technique.

2-41. There are two reconnaissance techniques commanders employ to answer information requirements:
reconnaissance push and reconnaissance pull. Commanders employ these techniques based on their level of
understanding of the operational environment combined with the time available to refine their understanding.
In selecting one technique over the other, the commander considers the following:

        z Degree of the situational understanding of the enemy.
        z Time available to collect the information.
        z Leadership ability of subordinate commanders.
        z Proficiency of subordinate units to plan and rapidly react for uncertain situations.

2-42. Reconnaissance-push is reconnaissance that refines the common operational picture, enabling the
commander to finalize the plan and support shaping and decisive operations. It is normally used once the
commander commits to a scheme of maneuver or course of action (FM 3-90-2). Reconnaissance push is used
when commanders have a relatively thorough understanding of the operational environment. In these cases
commanders ‘push’ reconnaissance assets into specific portions of their areas of operation to confirm, deny,
and validate planning assumptions impacting operations. Reconnaissance push emphasizes detailed, well-
rehearsed planning.

2-43. Reconnaissance-pull is reconnaissance that determines which routes are suitable for maneuver, where
the enemy is strong and weak, and where gaps exist, thus pulling the main body toward and along the path

11 May 2017  ATP 3-21.91  2-9


---

Chapter 2

     of least resistance. This facilitates the commander’s initiative and agility (FM 3-90-2). Reconnaissance pull
     is used when commanders are uncertain of the composition and disposition of threat forces in their areas of
     operation, information concerning terrain is vague, and time is limited. In these cases, reconnaissance assets
     initially work over a broad area to develop the enemy situation. As they gain an understanding of enemy
     weaknesses, they then ‘pull’ the main body to positions of tactical advantage. Reconnaissance pull knowingly
     emphasizes opportunity at the expense of a detailed, well-rehearsed plan, and unity of effort. Plans are based
     on several viable branches or COAs triggered by decision points that reconnaissance assets operate to answer
     associated commander’s critical information requirement. Leaders at all levels must understand and rehearse
     branches and sequels (see FM 3-98 for more information).

ASSESS

     2-44. Assessment is constant during a reconnaissance. It occurs at the same time as execution and does not
     end until a change of mission is executed. As part of the assessment the information gained from the
     reconnaissance is analyzed and shared to feed the SBCT’s intelligence process to generate SBCT commander
     decisions.

  SECTION II – SECURITY

     2-45. Security operations are those operations undertaken by the commander to provide early and accurate
     warning of enemy operations, to provide the force being protected with time and maneuver space within
     which to react to the enemy, and to develop the situation to allow the commander to effectively use the
     protected force (ADRP 3-90). Security operations enable the commander to detect enemy operations, protect
     another unit, and develop the situation. Security operations include reconnaissance aimed at reducing terrain
     and enemy unknowns; gaining and maintaining contact with the enemy to ensure continuous information
     collection; and providing early and accurate reporting of combat information to the protected force. Security
     forces orient in any direction from a stationary or moving force.

FUNDAMENTALS OF SECURITY OPERATIONS

     2-46. The five fundamentals of security operations are—
             z Provide early and accurate warning.
             z Provide reaction time and maneuver space.
             z Orient on the force, area, or facility to be protected.
             z Perform continuous reconnaissance.
             z Maintain enemy contact.

PROVIDE EARLY AND ACCURATE WARNING

     2-47. The security force provides early and accurate warning by detecting the threat force quickly and
     reporting combat information accurately to the commander. Early warning of threat activity provides the
     commander with the time, space, and information he needs to retain the tactical initiative and to choose the
     time and place to concentrate against the enemy. Placement of communication specifically retransmission
     stations is vital to establishing long-range communication with higher and adjacent units. At a minimum, the
     security force should operate far enough from the main body to prevent enemy ground forces from observing
     or engaging the main body with direct fire. The use of ATGM and MGS optics particularly with the thermal
     images can enhance long range observation.

PROVIDE REACTION TIME AND MANEUVER SPACE

     2-48. The security force operates as far from the protected force as possible within supporting range of the
     protected force, consistent with mission variables. Increased distance, coupled with effective
     communications, will yield greater reaction time and maneuver space for the protected force commander.
     The security force fights as needed to gain and retain adequate time and space for the protected force
     commander, allowing him to maneuver and concentrate forces to counter the threat.

2-10  ATP 3-21.91  11 May 2017


---

                                                                                                            Reconnaissance and Security

ORIENT ON THE FORCE, AREA, OR FACILITY TO BE PROTECTED

     2-49. The security force focuses all its actions on protecting the secured force or facility and providing
     maximum early warning of threat activity. It operates between the main body and known or suspected enemy
     units. The security force moves as the main body moves and orients on its movement. The security force
     commander has to know the main body’s scheme of maneuver and maneuver his force so it remains between
     the main body and the enemy. The value of terrain occupied by the security force depends on the operational
     area security it provides to the main body commander.

PERFORM CONTINUOUS RECONNAISSANCE

     2-50. Security comes in large part from knowing as much as possible about the threat and terrain within the
     assigned AO. This detailed knowledge results from ongoing, focused reconnaissance that aggressively and
     continuously reconnoiters key terrain; seeks the location, composition, and disposition of the threat; and
     attempts to determine the threat’s COA early. Stationary security forces use combinations of observation
     posts (OPs), patrols, and other information collection assets to perform continuous reconnaissance. Moving
     security forces accomplish this fundamental by performing area-, zone-, or route-reconnaissance in
     conjunction with temporary OPs and battle positions (BPs).

MAINTAIN ENEMY CONTACT

     2-51. Once gained, contact is not broken unless otherwise directed. The individual or sensor that first makes
     contact does not have to maintain it. However, the security force, collectively, maintains contact. The security
     force continuously conducts information collection on the threat’s activities, and prevents the threat from
     surprising the main body or endangering adjacent friendly forces.

     2-52. The fundamentals of maintaining enemy contact require the following:
             z Continuous contact (visual, electronic, sensor, or a combination).
             z Capability to use direct and indirect fires.
             z Freedom to maneuver.
             z Depth (of observers in time and space).

FORMS OF SECURITY

     2-53. Leaders categorize security operations in terms of the degree of security provided and the amount of
     combat power required. (Refer to FM 3-90-2 for more information.) There are five primary forms of security
     operations :

             z Screen is a security task that primarily provides early warning to the protected forces.
             z Guard is a security task whose primary task is to protect the main body by fighting to gain time

                   while also observing and reporting combat information and preventing enemy ground observation
                   of and direct fire against the main body. Units conducting a guard mission cannot operate
                   independently because they rely upon fires and close combat forces assets of the main body.
             z Cover is a security task to protect the main body by fighting to gain time while also observing and
                   reporting information and preventing enemy ground observation of and direct fire against the
                   main body. Unlike a screening or guard force, the covering force is a self-contained force capable
                   of operating independently of the main body.
             z Area security is a security task conducted to protect friendly forces, installations, routes, and
                   actions within a specific area.
             z Local security is a security task that includes close in security activities conducted near a unit to
                   prevent surprise by the enemy (ADRP 3-90).

     2-54. The SBCT weapons troops supports screens and guards with its task organized forces and participates
     in area security and cover as part of a larger force. The screen, guard, and cover, respectively contain
     increasing levels of combat power and provide increasing levels of security for the main body. However, the
     more combat power in the security force means less for the main body.

11 May 2017  ATP 3-21.91  2-11


---

Chapter 2

SCREEN

2-55. A screen primarily provides early warning by observing, identifying, and reporting enemy actions. It
provides the least amount of security of any security mission. Generally, a screening force engages and
destroys enemy reconnaissance elements within its capabilities, but otherwise fights to prevent from being
decisively engaged or fixed.

2-56. A screen is appropriate to cover gaps between forces, the exposed flanks or rear of stationary and
moving forces, or the front of a stationary formation. Units use screens when the likelihood of enemy contact
is remote, the expected enemy force is small, or the friendly main body needs only a minimum amount of
time, once it is warned, to react effectively. A screening force primarily uses indirect fires or close air support
(CAS) to destroy enemy reconnaissance elements and slow the movement of other enemy forces. Units
usually accomplish screening by establishing a series of OPs and conducting patrols to ensure adequate
surveillance of the assigned AO.

2-57.      Screen tasks are to—
           z Allow no enemy ground element to pass through the screen undetected and unreported.
           z Maintain continuous surveillance of all avenues of approach into the designated area under all

                 visibility conditions.
           z Destroy or repel all enemy reconnaissance within its capabilities.
           z Locate the lead elements of each enemy advance guard and determine its direction of movement

                 in a defensive screen.
           z Maintain contact with enemy forces and report any activity in the AO.
           z Maintain contact with the main body and any security forces operating on its flanks.
           z Impede and harass the enemy within its capabilities while displacing.

2-58. The SBCT weapons troop normally conducts a screen mission pure or split with the Cavalry squadron.
Normally, the Cavalry squadron and weapons troop will task organize scout sections with ATGM and MGS
sections. They will use a technique known as “hunter killer teams.” Scouts sections will establish OP’s in
depth along a screen line and position ATGM and MGS in positions of tactical advantage. Upon identification
of enemy elements meeting engagement criteria; the scouts will alert the ATGM and MGS elements to
engage from specific positions in the security area. The information passed to ATGM and MGS elements
will be assessed by their leaders as to the best means to engage the target limiting their exposure and ensuring
its destruction.

2-59. Counterreconnaissance is an essential component of security operations. The security force should
deny the enemy information and intelligence concerning the friendly force. The security force accomplishes
this by destroying, defeating, or deceiving enemy reconnaissance units or sensors according to engagement
criteria and the rules of engagement (ROE).

GUARD

2-60. A guard differs from a screen in that a guard force contains sufficient combat power to defeat, cause
the withdrawal of, or fix the lead elements of an enemy ground force before it can engage the main body with
direct fire. A guard force uses all means at its disposal, to include decisive engagement, to prevent the enemy
from penetrating to a position where it could observe and engage the main body. It operates within the range
of the main body’s fire support weapons, deploying over a narrower front than a comparable-size screening
force to permit concentrating combat power.

2-61. The three types of guard operations are as follows: advance, flank, and rear guard. The commander
can assign a guard mission to protect either a stationary or a moving force.

        z The advance guard is responsible for clearing the axis of advance or designated portions of the
             AO of enemy elements. This allows the main body to move unimpeded, prevents the unnecessary
             delay of the main body, and defers the deployment of the main body for as long as possible. An
             advance guard for a stationary force is defensive in nature. It defends or delays according to the
             main body commander’s intent. An advance guard for a moving Stryker force is offensive in

2-12       ATP 3-21.91  11 May 2017


---

                                                                                                      Reconnaissance and Security

             nature and normally conducts a movement to contact and tries to initiate contact with dismounted
             elements.
        z A flank guard protects against an exposed flank of the main body. The commander of the main
             body designates the general location of the flank guard’s positions. AOs assigned to the flank
             guard should be sufficiently deep to provide early warning and reaction time. However, flank
             guards remain within supporting range of the main body.
        z The rear guard protects the exposed rear of the main body. This occurs during offensive tasks
             when the main body breaks contact with flanking forces or during a retrograde. The commander
             may deploy a rear guard behind both moving and stationary main bodies. The rear guard for a
             moving force displaces to successive BPs along phase lines or delay lines in depth as the main
             body moves. The nature of enemy contact determines the exact movement method or combination
             of methods used in the displacement (successive bounds, alternate bounds, and continuous
             marching).

2-62. A unit conducting a guard performs certain tasks within its capabilities unless ordered otherwise. If a
unit does not have the time or other resources to complete all of these tasks, it informs the commander
assigning the mission of the shortfall and requests guidance on which tasks to complete or the priority of
tasks. After starting the guard, if the unit determines that it cannot complete an assigned task, such as cause
deployment of the enemy advance guard, it reports this to the commander and awaits further instructions.

2-63. The SBCT weapons troop normally conducts a guard mission pure with the Cavalry squadron. (See
figure 2-3, page 2-14.) Normally, the Cavalry squadron and troop will organize in depth. They will establish
a series of OP’s to identify enemy moving within the security area. The will pass the information to the
weapons troop to mass at point of tactical advantage to engage the enemy. The weapons troop can also expect
to respond to scout elements that have been engaged and are endanger of becoming fixed.

2-64. The Cavalry squadron is most vulnerable when collapsing the guard. The weapons troop will be used
to provide overwatch. It is recommended that weapons troop elements use bounding overwatch to cover
elements during this task.

Legend: BP = battle position, RTE = route, N = north
                           Figure 2-3. SBCT weapons troop pure in a guard

2-14       ATP 3-21.91                                                     11 May 2017


---

                                                                                                            Reconnaissance and Security

COVER

     2-65. The SBCT weapons troop is not structured to conducting a cover on its own. A covering force is a self-
     contained force capable of operating independently of the main body, unlike a screening or guard force to
     conduct the cover task (FM 3-90-1). A covering force, or portions of it, often becomes decisively engaged
     with enemy forces. Therefore, the covering force has to have substantial combat power to engage the enemy
     and accomplish its mission. A covering force develops the situation earlier than a screen or a guard force. It
     fights longer and more often and defeats larger enemy forces.

     2-66. While a covering force provides more security than a screen or guard force, it requires more resources.
     A covering force accomplishes all the tasks of screening and guard forces. A covering force for a stationary
     force performs a defensive mission, while a covering force for a moving force generally conducts offensive
     actions. A covering force normally operates forward of the main body in the offense or defense, or to the rear
     for a retrograde.

AREA SECURITY

     2-67. Area security operations may be offensive or defensive in nature. They focus on the protected force,
     installation, route, or area. Forces to protect range from echelon headquarters through artillery and echelon
     reserves to the sustaining base. Protected installations can be part of the sustaining base or they can constitute
     part of the area’s infrastructure. Areas to secure range from specific points (bridges and defiles) and terrain
     features (ridge lines and hills) to large population centers and their adjacent areas.

     2-68. Area security operations can require the execution of a wide variety of supporting operations and tasks.
     SBCT weapons troop conducts area security as part of a larger force. They can be employed as part of the
     reserve, in support of areas, or in areas that are suited for long range engagements.

     2-69. When conducting an area security mission, the SBCT weapons troop prevents threat ground
     reconnaissance elements from directly observing friendly activities within the area being secured. It prevents
     threat ground maneuver forces from penetrating the defensive perimeters established by the commander. The
     SBCT weapons troop can execute security tasks through ambushes and preemptive strikes proactively and
     with greater precision.

     2-70. An analysis of mission variables enables the commander to determine the augmentation for the SBCT
     weapons troop, with particular consideration given to the need for aviation, engineers, and artillery. Early
     warning of threat activity is paramount when conducting area security missions, and provides the commander
     with time and space to react to threats. Proper intelligence analysis and information collection planning,
     coupled with dismounted/mounted patrols and aerial reconnaissance, is essential to successful operations,
     especially when securing fixed sites. Failure to conduct continuous reconnaissance can create a vulnerable
     seam through which the enemy can execute an infiltration or attack.

     2-71. During area security operations civilians will be present. Therefore, leaders ensure Soldiers understand
     current ROE. However, leaders are always responsible for protecting their forces and consider this
     responsibility when applying the ROE. Restrictions on conducting operations and using force need to be
     explained clearly and understood by everyone. Soldiers need to understand their actions, no matter how
     minor, may have far-reaching positive or negative effects. They need to realize both friendly or hostile media
     and adversary information activities can quickly exploit their actions, especially the manner in which they
     treat the civilian population.

     2-72. When a perimeter is not feasible, the SBCT weapons troop secures the area by establishing a presence
     and conducting operations throughout the area. The SBCT weapons troop establishes perimeters around base
     camps, critical infrastructure, and high-value assets, while other units conduct operations to establish
     presence, provide security, and conduct stability tasks. The troop can position a reaction force between
     several secured perimeters. Other missions or tasks in support of area security may include the following:

             z Screens along zones of separation or other designated areas.
             z Route or convoy security of critical lines of communication.
             z Checkpoints to monitor or control movement.

11 May 2017  ATP 3-21.91  2-15


---

Chapter 2

LOCAL SECURITY

     2-73. Local security includes any local measure(s) taken by units to prevent surprise by the enemy. It
     involves avoiding detection by the enemy or deceiving the enemy about friendly positions and intentions.
     Local security is an important part of maintaining the initiative. The requirement for maintaining local
     security is an inherent part of all operations. Units use both active and passive measures to provide local
     security. Active measures include OPs, patrols, and conducting stand-to. Passive measures include
     camouflage, noise and light discipline, and sensors to maintain surveillance over the area immediately around
     the unit.

     2-74. The SBCT weapons troop is responsible for maintaining its own security at all times. ATGM and MGS
     vehicles are vulnerable from attacks on its rear and sides from Infantry. The weapons on these vehicles have
     arming distances on their main weapon systems, as well as limitations on the angles that their weapons
     depress or elevate. Platoons and sections should establish mutually supporting positions for Stryker vehicles
     for 360 degree local security. In addition to maintaining security for its own elements, the SBCT weapons
     troop may implement local security for other units as directed by the SBCT task force commander. Examples
     of such situations include, but are not limited to, the following:

             z Provide security for engineers as they emplace/clear obstacles or construct survivability positions.
             z Establish mounted OPs to maintain surveillance of enemy infiltration and reconnaissance routes.
             z Conduct patrols to cover gaps in observation and to clear possible enemy OP from surrounding

                   areas.
             z Supply and logistical operations.

  SECTION III – RECONAISSANCE HANDOVER AND TRANSITION

     2-75. Reconnaissance and security handover is an event that occurs in the SBCT’s operation that causes a
     shift in the main effort. If the reconnaissance and security phase is successful it will set the condition for
     follow forces to conduct offensive or defensive tasks. The SBCT weapons troop and its subordinate platoons
     should prepare to reorganize under control of a follow on task force as SBCT transitions phases of the
     operation.

     2-76. To initiate the reconnaissance and security handover; the Cavalry squadron contacts the lead element
     from the task force behind it and begins the linkup process and information sharing. Normally the scout
     platoon of the SBCT Infantry battalion is the first element to conduct the linkup with the Cavalry squadron.
     During this process an assessment is conducted between the SBCT commander and staff, the Cavalry
     squadron, and the follow on SBCT task force to determine if operational variables and criteria are suitable
     for the transition. If so then guidance and operational control SBCT weapons troop and its ATGM and MGS
     platoons are given over to the SBCT task force. This element oversees the task organization change and
     handover until complete.

     2-77. A decision point and a conditions check to assess should be made by the SBCT commander supported
     by information from both the Squadron and SBCT staff to determine if it is prudent. When they transition to
     the main part of an offense or defense coordination must occur between the SBCT weapons troop and the
     SBCT Infantry battalion. A linkup point, phase line, or coordination point graphic control measure should
     depict where the handover process occurs. Additionally a refuel and rearm on the move before the ATGM
     and MGS transfer units is ideal.

TRANSITION TO OFFENSE

     2-78. The SBCT weapons troop transitions to the offense after the Cavalry squadron and the Infantry
     battalion have completed reconnaissance handover. Key to the handover is the coordination of their resupply,
     security, and acknowledgement between the losing element (Cavalry squadron), gaining element (Infantry
     battalion), and the weapons troop.

     2-79. The SBCT weapons troop can participate in the transition via two techniques. The first is to remain in
     place preferably overwatching a named area of interest or target area of interest or facilitating the Infantry
     elements maneuver to the objective. The second may require a physical linkup. This is likely during an SBCT
     movement to contact attack when friendly forces have not made contact with the enemy force or a deliberate

2-16       ATP 3-21.91  11 May 2017


---

                                                                                                            Reconnaissance and Security

     attack that requires planning and coordination. Using this technique it is best to linkup in secured areas and
     the having the SBCT weapons troop move to a position that the SBCT Infantry battalion or one of its
     subordinate elements secure. From this position it is easier to task organize into smaller elements.

TRANSITION TO DEFENSE

     2-80. The SBCT weapons troop transitions to the defense after the Cavalry squadron collapses the screen or
     guard, identifies an enemy force of considerable strength, or the SBCT conducts a retrograde. Keys to their
     transition are the same as in offense.

     2-81. The SBCT weapons troop can participate in the transition via two techniques. The first is to remain in
     place and Infantry elements move to their position and establish a defense. The second is for the SBCT
     weapons troop to move to the SBCT Infantry battalion. Using this technique the SBCT weapons troop is
     vulnerable during their movement from fratricide as well from enemy contact. It is recommended that an
     element from the gaining unit links up and guides them to their positions in the defense.

11 May 2017  ATP 3-21.91  2-17

                                                 Chapter 3

                                     Offense

         Offensive tasks lets the commander seize the initiative (choose when and where to
         fight), retain the initiative, and effectively exploit his troop's strengths while exploiting
         the enemy’s weakness. The commander may conduct offensive tasks to deprive the
         enemy of resources, seize decisive terrain, deceive or divert the enemy, collect
         information, or fix an enemy in position. The SBCT weapons troop and its subordinate
         elements conduct offensive tasks as part of a larger force often task organized to
         company/team and below size units. The SBCT weapons troops and its subordinate
         platoons assist the Infantry element’s engagement in close combat. This chapter covers
         basics of the offense, common offensive planning considerations, movement to
         contact, attack, and transitions.

  SECTION I – BASICS OF THE OFFENSE

     3-1. The main purpose of the offense is to defeat, destroy, or neutralize the enemy force. Additionally,
     commanders conduct offensive tasks to secure decisive terrain, to deprive the enemy of resources, to gain
     information, to deceive and divert the enemy, to hold the enemy in position, to disrupt the enemy’s attack,
     and to set up the conditions for future successful operations. (Refer to ADRP 3-90 for more information.)

CHARACTERISTICS OF THE OFFENSE

     3-2. The main feature of offensive tasks is taking and maintaining the initiative. The aim of the commander
     in the offense is to defeat the enemy. Audacity, concentration, surprise, and tempo characterize the conduct
     of offensive tasks and are components of initiative. Initiative—within the higher commander’s intent,
     combined with maneuver and fires, makes possible the conduct of decisive offensive tasks. (Refer to
     ADRP 3-90 for more information.)

AUDACITY

     3-3. Audacity is achieved by boldly executing a simple plan of action. Commanders must exercise audacity
     by developing bold, inventive plans that produce decisive results while violently applying combat power.
     Commanders understand when and where to take risks, avoid hesitation when executing their plan in order
     to seize, retain, or exploit the initiative.

CONCENTRATION

     3-4. Concentration is the massing of overwhelming effects of the combined arms team to achieve a single
     purpose. The troop commander must avoid set patterns or obvious movements that would indicate the timing
     or direction of the attack. He designates, sustains, and shifts the main effort as necessary. Subsequent volleys
     beyond the initial engagement may be against an enemy that has identified your location. Each vehicle may
     only get a single shot before displacing, and so maximizing the effects of an engagement is critical. However,
     this does not mean all fires must be initiated simultaneously. Alternating fires by position and weapons
     platform type can be effective in exploiting enemy reaction time.

SURPRISE

     3-5. Commanders achieve surprise by striking the enemy at a time or place, or in a manner, for which he is
     unprepared, by assessing the enemy commander’s intent and denying that commander the ability to gain
     situational understanding. Firing from concealed position simultaneously allows the weapons troop to

11 May 2017  ATP 3-21.91  3-1


---

Chapter 3

     capitalize on achieving surprise. Displacing immediately after the engagement keeps the enemy confused
     and disoriented allowing for multiple limited engagements.

     3-6. The enhanced digital communications and information systems found at the troop level facilitate the
     ability concentration of synchronized combat power against the enemy by quickly converging on identified
     targets. The SBCT weapons troop provides the most effective concentration when it engages targets from
     multiple positions surrounding a single engagement area.

RAPID TEMPO

     3-7. Tempo is a combination of speed and mass that creates pressure on the enemy. Commanders build the
     appropriate tempo to provide the necessary momentum for attacks to achieve their objectives.

     3-8. Controlling or altering tempo, deliberate or rapid, is necessary to retain the initiative. At the tactical
     level, a rapid tempo focuses on key pieces of information, terrain, entails a small number of tasks, and allows
     attackers to quickly penetrate barriers and defenses and destroy enemy forces in depth before they can react.

     3-9. Commanders adjust the tempo to achieve synchronization. Speed is preferred in order to keep the
     enemy off balance, but establishing the conditions for decisive actions may require the tempo to be slowed
     as the pieces are set in place. Once ready, the tempo is increased, and the action takes place rapidly.

OFFENSIVE TASKS

     3-10. The four primary offensive tasks are movement to contact, attack, exploitation, and pursuit. The SBCT
     weapons troop can participate in an exploitation or pursuit as part of a larger force by conducting movement
     to contact or attack as part brigade and higher echelon formations. (Refer to FM 3-90-1 for more information.)

SEQUENCE OF THE OFFENSE

     3-11. This chapter discusses executing movement to contact and attack offensive tasks using the following
     methodology:

             z Gain and maintain enemy contact.
             z Disrupt the enemy.
             z Fix the enemy.
             z Maneuver.
             z Follow through.

     3-12. This sequence provides a framework to discuss the execution of an offensive task and is not the only
     method a commander may use to achieve success. The steps normally overlap one another during the conduct
     of offense and the first three are typically supporting efforts, while maneuver is the main effort. Follow
     through is a sequel or a branch to the plan based on the current situation.

FORMS OF CONTACT

     3-13. In both offensive and defensive tasks, contact occurs when a member of the SBCT weapons troop
     encounters a situation that requires a lethal or nonlethal response. These situations may entail one or more of
     the following forms of contact: (Refer to ADRP 3-90 for more information.)

             z Visual.
             z Direct fire.
             z Indirect fire.
             z Obstacles.
             z Aerial.
             z CBRN.
             z Electronic warfare.
             z Nonhostile.

                   Adversary.
                   Supporters.
                   Neutrals.

3-2        ATP 3-21.91  11 May 2017


---

                                                                                                                                            Offense

ACTIONS ON CONTACT

     3-14. The SBCT weapons troop should execute actions on contact using a logical, well-organized process of
     decision making and action that entails the following four steps: (Refer to FM 3-90-1 for more information.)

             z Deploy and report.
             z Evaluate and develop the situation and COA.
             z Choose a COA.
             z Execute the selected COA.

     3-15. This four-step process, with some steps often conducted simultaneously, is not intended to generate a
     rigid, lockstep response to the enemy. Rather, the goal is to provide an orderly framework that enables the
     SBCT weapons troop and its platoons to survive the initial contact, and then apply sound decision making
     and timely actions to complete the operation. Ideally, the company sees the enemy (visual contact) before
     being seen by the enemy; it then can initiate physical contact on its own terms by executing the designated
     COA.

     3-16. Once the lead elements of a force encounter the enemy, they conduct actions on contact. The unit treats
     obstacles like enemy contact, and assumes that the obstacles are covered by fire. The unit’s security force
     gains tactical advantage over an enemy force by using tempo and initiative to conduct actions on contact.
     Doing so allows the unit to gain and maintain contact without becoming decisively engaged. How quickly
     the unit develops the situation is directly related to its security. This tempo is directly related to the unit's use
     of well-rehearsed SOPs and drills.

     3-17. Commanders understand that properly executed actions on contact require time at both platoon and
     company levels. To develop the situation fully, a platoon or company may have to execute flanking
     movement; conduct reconnaissance; or call for and adjust indirect fires. Each of these activities requires time.
     The commander balances the time required for subordinate elements to conduct actions on contact with the
     need of the company or battalion to maintain momentum.

Deploy and Report

     3-18. Events that occur during initial contact depend on whether the contact is expected or unexpected. The
     first step of actions on contact concludes with the unit deployed, the enemy suppressed or destroyed, and the
     commander sending a contact report to battalion headquarters. The following paragraphs examine some
     variables the company commander faces in both contact situations.

Expected Contact

     3-19. If the commander expects contact (based on reports or through his own reconnaissance or by other
     means), he will already have deployed the company by transitioning to the bounding overwatch movement
     technique. For the weapons troops and its platoons this likely occurs when supporting Infantry and Cavalry
     formation and responding to contact those units have already made. If the troop is alert to the likely presence
     of the enemy, it has a better chance of establishing first visual contact and then physical contact on its own
     terms. Contact, either visual or physical, usually is made by an overwatching or bounding platoon, which
     initiates the actions on contact. In a worst-case scenario, a previously undetected (but expected) enemy
     element may engage the platoon. The platoon in contact would conduct a battle drill for its own survival and
     then initiate actions on contact.

     3-20. Deployment in expected contact requires final coordination with the friendly element if responding to
     Infantry or Cavalry elements to ensure they are clear of surface danger zones (SDZs) and friendly fires. This
     normally occurs en route. Accurate spot reports of enemy direction and description as well as fire control
     measures alleviate the confusion (see chapter 9 of this publication for more information).

Unexpected Contact

     3-21. In some cases, the SBCT weapons troop makes unexpected contact with the enemy while using
     traveling or traveling overwatch. The element in contact or, if necessary, the entire troop may deploy using
     battle drills to survive the initial contact. When making unexpected contact, the platoon in contact
     immediately sends a contact report. The most efficient way to provide situational understanding and update
     the COP to the task force is through digital reports sent by platoons in contact. The SBCT weapons troop and

11 May 2017  ATP 3-21.91  3-3


---

Chapter 3

     platoons develop SOPs that harness the capabilities of JCR/BFT while destroying the enemy force and
     protecting the rest of the force.

     3-22. The SBCT weapons troop is most vulnerable to unexpected contact with enemy forces at close range.
     To avoid the likelihood of occurrence elements of the weapons troop should follow intermix or follow
     Infantry and Cavalry formations. The same fire control coordination and considerations should be taken into
     account as expected contact. Vehicle crews do have the inherent right to self-defense and must do so in such
     matters. Infantry and Cavalry units must be aware of this when working with one another.

Evaluate and Develop the Situation

     3-23. The commander evaluates the situation and continues to develop it while the SBCT weapons troop
     deploys. The commander quickly gathers as much combat information as possible, either visually or more
     often, through reports of the platoon(s) in contact. He analyzes the combat information to determine critical
     operational considerations, to include the following:

             z The size of the enemy element.
             z Location, composition, activity, and orientation of the enemy force.
             z The impact of obstacles and terrain.
             z Enemy capabilities.
             z Probable enemy intentions.
             z How to gain positional advantage over the enemy.
             z The friendly situation (location, strength, and capabilities).
             z Possible friendly COA to achieve the specified end state.

     3-24. The SBCT weapons troop commander sends a report to task force once he determines the size of the
     enemy force his unit encountered. However, after evaluating the situation, the commander may discover that
     he does not have enough information to identify the necessary operational considerations. To make this
     determination, he further develops the situation according to the task force commander’s intent, using a
     combination of the following capabilities:

             z Reconnaissance; employing platoons or unmanned aircraft system (UAS).
             z Maneuver (this includes flanking maneuver to gain additional information by viewing the enemy

                   from another perspective).
             z Indirect fire.
             z Reconnaissance by fire.

Choose a Course of Action

     3-25. After developing the situation and determining that he has enough information to make a decision, the
     troop commander selects a COA that meets the requirements of the task force commander’s intent and is
     within the troop’s capabilities.

Execute the Selected Course of Action

     3-26. The troop transitions to maneuver when executing a COA. It continues to maneuver throughout
     execution, either as part of a tactical mission task or to advance while in contact to reach the point in the AO
     from which it executes its tactical mission task. The weapons troop can employ a number of tactical mission
     tasks as COAs, any of which may be preceded (or followed) by additional maneuver. Some of these tasks are
     as follows:

             z Attack by fire.
             z Support by fire.
             z Create a breach point or Reduce obstacle.
             z Bypass.
             z Disengagement.
             z Exfiltrate.
             z Follow and assume.
             z Follow and support.

3-4  ATP 3-21.91  11 May 2017


---

                                                                                                                                            Offense

     3-27. More information becomes available to the troop commander as execution continues. The commander
     may have to alter his COA during execution based on the emerging details of the enemy situation. (For
     example, as the SBCT weapons troop maneuvers to destroy what appears to be a motorized Infantry platoon,
     it discovers an additional mechanized Infantry platoon and tank platoon.) The commander analyzes and
     develops the new situation. He selects an alternate COA, such as establishing a support by fire (SBF) position
     to support another company’s maneuver against the newly discovered enemy force.

Recommend a Course of Action to the Higher Commander

     3-28. Once the troop commander selects a COA, keeping in mind the commander’s intent, he informs the
     task force commander, who has the option of disapproving it based on its impact on the overall mission. Unit
     standard operating procedure (SOP) may provide automatic approval of certain actions to avoid delay.

FORMS OF MANEUVER

     3-29. The commander selects the form of maneuver based on his analysis of METT-TC. The commander
     then synchronizes the contributions of all warfighting functions to the selected form of maneuver. An
     operation may contain several forms of offensive maneuver. (Refer to FM 3-90-1 for more information.) The
     six forms of maneuver are as follows:

             z Envelopment.
             z Flank attack.
             z Turning movement.
             z Frontal attack.
             z Penetration.
             z Infiltration.

ENVELOPMENT

     3-30. Envelopment is a form of maneuver in which an attacking force seeks to avoid the principal enemy
     defenses by seizing objectives behind those defenses that allow the targeted enemy force to be destroyed in
     their established positions (FM 3-90-1). At the tactical level, envelopments reemphasize on seizing terrain,
     destroying specific enemy forces, and interdicting enemy withdrawal routes. The commander’s decisive
     operation focuses on attacking an assailable flank. Envelopment also produces great psychological shock on
     the enemy. The attacking force creates an assailable flank if none is available. The four varieties of
     envelopment are single envelopment, double envelopment, encirclement, and vertical envelopment.

     3-31. When operating pure of with the Cavalry squadron the weapons troop and its platoons conduct single
     envelopment and encirclement. This normally occurs for targets of opportunity or against enemy
     reconnaissance forces. They destroy enemy forces on or beyond the objective but do occupy for a short
     amount of time. Occupation of the objective is normally passed off to follow on forces.

     3-32. In support of Infantry ATGM and MGS platoons fix an enemy element with support by fires to allow
     Stryker Infantry to maneuver to a position of advantage at the flank or rear of the enemy. The Infantry
     dismount and assault the objective from a position of advantage. During the assault the ATGM and MGS
     must coordinate with the frontline trace of the Infantry element through visual means to prevent fratricide
     (See figure 3-1, page 3-6).

11 May 2017  ATP 3-21.91  3-5


---

Chapter 3

     Legend: MGS = mobile gun system

                                                  Figure 3-1. Envelopment

FLANK ATTACK

     3-33. A flank attack is a form of offensive maneuver directed at the flank of an enemy force (FM 3-90-1). A
     flank is the right or left side of a military formation and is not oriented toward the enemy. It is usually not as
     strong in terms of forces or fires as is the front of a military formation. A flank may be created by the attacker
     through the use of fires or by a successful penetration. A flanking attack is similar to an envelopment but
     generally conducted on a shallower axis. It is designed to defeat the enemy force while minimizing the effect
     of the enemy‘s frontally-oriented combat power.

     3-34. The primary difference between a flank attack and an envelopment is one of depth. A flank attack is
     an envelopment delivered squarely on the enemy’s flank. Conversely, an envelopment is a flank attack
     delivered beyond the enemy’s flank and into their rear area, but short of the depth associated with a turning
     movement. Smaller-size tactical units, such as maneuver battalions, companies, and platoons, are more likely
     to conduct flank attacks than larger tactical units. This is largely a result of troop-to-space ratios and
     sustainment and mission command.

     3-35. The SBCT weapons troop conducts flank attacks normally as the decisive operation directed at the
     flank of the enemy. (See figure 3-2.) They use mission command systems, mobility of their Stryker vehicles,
     to initiate the flank attack unaware to the enemy normally at their center or rear of their formation. They

3-6        ATP 3-21.91  11 May 2017


---

                                                                                                                                      Offense

capitalize on situations where speed and simplicity are the main factors and, ultimately, keeping the initiative.
The main effort normally comprised of MGS initiates the assault on the enemy’s flank. This attack is further
sustained when followed and supported by Infantry who move to the center and then expand to the far, front
and rear of enemy formation. A supporting effort comprised of ATGMs engages the enemy’s front allowing
the signature of the ATGM fires to be masked during the confusion of battle. Indirect fires using high
explosive and smoke mix add to further confusion allowing the Infantry, MGS and ATGM platoons to
withdraw respectively.

     Legend: ENY = enemy, MGS = mobile gun system

                                                   Figure 3-2. Flank attack

TURNING MOVEMENT

     3-36. A turning movement is a form of maneuver in which the attacking force seeks to avoid the enemy’s
     principle defensive positions by seizing objectives behind the enemy’s current positions thereby causing the
     enemy force to move out of their current positions or divert major forces to meet the threat (FM 3-90-1).

     3-37. The SBCT weapons troop uses information sharing through mission command systems with the
     mobility of the Stryker platform to maneuver in order to seize vital areas in the enemy’s support area before
     the main enemy force can withdraw or receive support or reinforcements. A turning movement differs from
     envelopment because the force conducting a turning movement seeks to make the enemy force displace from

11 May 2017  ATP 3-21.91  3-7


---

Chapter 3

     their current locations. An enveloping force seeks to engage the enemy in their established location from an
     unexpected direction. This is normally conducted with Infantry and Cavalry units who help secure the
     objective in a turning movement. (See figure 3-3.)

     Legend: MGS = mobile gun system
                                            Figure 3-3. Turning movement

3-8  ATP 3-21.91                                                          11 May 2017


---

                                                                                                                                            Offense

FRONTAL ATTACK

     3-38. In a frontal attack the attacking force destroys a weaker enemy or fixes a larger enemy force in place
     over a broad front (FM 3-90-1). An attacking force can use a frontal attack to rapidly overrun a weak enemy
     force. The SBCT weapons troop uses a frontal attack with MGS and ATGM, supported by fires and CAS.
     Most common is an attack by fire independently or its platoons may conduct a support by fire in support of
     Infantry. (See figure 3-4.)

Legend: MGS = mobile gun system
                                           Figure 3-4. Frontal attack

11 May 2017  ATP 3-21.91                                               3-9


---

Chapter 3

PENETRATION

     3-39. A penetration is a form of maneuver in which an attacking force seeks to rupture enemy defenses on
     a narrow front to disrupt the defensive system (FM 3-90-1).The penetration extends from the enemy’s main
     defensive positions through the security area into the enemy support area. The SBCT weapons troop
     commander employs a penetration when there is no assailable flank, enemy defenses are overextended and
     weak spots are detected in the enemy’s positions, or time pressures do not permit envelopment. They use
     fires and effects to support maneuvering units. Most common is an attack by fire independently or its platoons
     may conduct a support by fire in support of Infantry. (See figure 3-5.)

Legend: MGS = mobile gun system

                                 Figure 3-5. Penetration

3-10                             ATP 3-21.91              11 May 2017


---

                                                                                                                                            Offense

INFILTRATION

     3-40. An infiltration is a form of maneuver in which an attacking force conducts undetected movement
     through or into an area occupied by enemy forces to occupy a position of advantage behind those enemy
     positions while exposing only small elements to enemy defensive fires (FM 3-90-1). Infiltration occurs by
     land, water, air, or a combination of means. Moving and assembling forces covertly through enemy positions
     is time consuming. To infiltrate successfully, the force avoids detection and engagement. Since this
     requirement limits the size and strength of the infiltrating force—and infiltrated forces alone can rarely defeat
     an enemy—infiltration is normally used in conjunction with and in support of other forms of maneuver.

COMMON OFFENSIVE PLANNING CONSIDERATIONS

     3-41. The warfighting functions are critical tactical activities the commander can use to review, prepare, and
     execute planning. Synchronization and coordination between the warfighting functions are critical for
     success. The following paragraphs discuss selected warfighting functions and other additional planning
     considerations.

MISSION COMMAND

     3-42. The commander’s mission and intent determine the scheme of maneuver and the allocation of available
     resources. All planning for offensive tasks address the mission variables of METT-TC. The mission
     command function of offensive tasks for the SBCT weapons troop include:

             z Commander’s intent.
             z Mission objectives, to include task and purpose, for each subordinate element.
             z Scheme of maneuver.
             z Location of key leaders.
             z Suspected enemy locations and courses of action.
             z Courses of action.
             z Required control measures/graphics.
             z Priorities of fire.
             z Bypass criteria.
             z Reporting requirements.
             z Primary, alternate, contingency, and emergency communications.

     3-43. The weapons troop commander will have multiple considerations for mission command. The weapons
     troop and its platoons can potentially be supporting four task forces simultaneously under normal situations
     in the offense. It can also count on multiple task organization changes and transitions from operation to
     operation. Coordination is key for this unit.

     3-44. The weapons troop commander must build relationships with supported task forces. Together, they
     exchange of ideas to develop creative solutions to shared challenges. Successful mission command in the
     offense fosters a greater understanding of the operational environment and solution development (See
     ADRP 6-0 for more information). This results in teams that—

             z Are adaptive and anticipate transitions.
             z Accept risks to create opportunities.
             z Influence friendly, neutrals, adversaries, enemies, and unified action partners.

MOVEMENT AND MANEUVER

     3-45. The commander conducts maneuver to avoid enemy strengths and to create opportunities to increase
     the effects of friendly fires. The commander achieves surprise by making unexpected maneuvers, rapidly
     changing the tempo of ongoing missions, avoiding observation, and using deceptive techniques and
     procedures. The commander seeks to overwhelm the enemy with one or more unexpected actions before the
     enemy has time to react in an organized fashion. This happens when the troop conducting the attack is able
     to attack the enemy from a position of strength or from an unexpected location or direction.

11 May 2017  ATP 3-21.91  3-11


---

Chapter 3

Formations

     3-46. The weapons troop commander selects what formation and order of units to deploy his forces based
     on the factors of METT-TC. Each formation provides the commander with flexibility to achieve a specific
     outcome. Commanders can shift formations as needed as factors of METT-TC change to maintain the
     initiative.

     3-47. The weapons troop commanders’ role is to coordinate the maneuver of its subordinate forces to
     synchronize their maneuver and formation to accomplish to overall mission. METT-TC will impact the
     decision making of subordinate leaders to select formations that optimally support their maneuver. The
     weapons troop rapidly shifts to a combination of elements working to complement each other’s maneuver.
     This includes adjacent units maneuvering close to the weapons troop.

Column

     3-48. The column formation allows the troop to make contact with one platoon and maneuver with the other
     trail platoons. It is a flexible formation, allowing easy transition to other formations. It provides good
     all-around security and allows fast movement. It provides good dispersion and aids maneuver and control,
     especially in limited visibility. The troop can deliver a limited volume of fire to the front and to the rear, and
     a high volume to the flanks. (See figure 3-6.)

3-12  ATP 3-21.91  11 May 2017

Chapter 3

Troop Wedge
     3-49. The troop wedge formation allows the commander to make contact with a small element and still
     maneuver the remaining platoons. If the troop is engaged from the flank, one platoon is free to maneuver.
     This formation is hard to control, but it allows faster movement than the troop Vee formation. (See
     figure 3-7.)

tactical vehicles, MEV = medical evacuation vehicle, MGS = mobile gun system

                         Figure 3-7. SBCT weapons troop in a wedge formation

3-14  ATP 3-21.91  11 May 2017


---

                                                                                                                                            Offense

Troop and Platoon Vee
     3-50. The troop Vee formation has two platoons forward to provide immediate fire on contact or to flank the
     enemy. It has one platoon centered trailing the two forward platoons. If the troop is engaged from either
     flank, two platoons can provide fire, and at least one platoon is free to maneuver. This formation is hard to
     control and slows movement. The troop commander designates one of the forward platoons as the base
     platoon. (See figure 3-8.)

Legend: CDR = commander, FSV = fire support vehicle.
                           Figure 3-8. SBCT weapons troop in a Vee formation

11 May 2017  ATP 3-21.91                                                      3-15


---

Chapter 3

Troop Line
     3-51. The troop line formation puts all platoons forward along the same direction of movement, and it
     provides for the delivery of maximum fire to the front, but less to the flanks. It is the most difficult formation
     to control. The troop commander designates a base platoon, normally the center one, for the others to guide
     on. Flank and rear security is generally poor, but is improved when the flank platoons use echelon formations.
     (See figure 3-9.)

    Legend: CDR = commander, FSV = fire support vehicle

                               Figure 3-9. SBCT weapons troop in a line formation

Echelon Right or Left

     3-52. The echelon right or echelon left formation is used if the situation is vague and the troop commander
     anticipates enemy contact to the front or on one of the flanks. Normally, an obstacle or another friendly unit
     exists on the flank of the troop opposite the echeloned flank, preventing enemy contact on that side. This
     formation provides a good volume of fire and protection to the echeloned flank, but less to the opposite flank.
     (See figure 3-10.) ATGM should not perform echelon right or left formation while moving. The ATGM
     vehicle must be at a stop to fire its missile. This negates all the benefits from moving in echelon.

Box and Diamond

     3-53. The box information is useful when general information about the enemy is known, and the SBCT
     weapons troop requires flexibility and depth in its attack. The diamond formation is a variation of the box
     formation. The SBCT weapons troop uses box and diamond formations (see figure 3-11, page 3-18) when it
     has four maneuver forces. Both the box and the diamond formations—

                z Provide the best flexibility for maneuver.
                z Enable easy transition into all other formations.
                z Distribute firepower forward and to the flanks.
                z Are easy to control.
                z Provide all-around security.
                z Facilitate rapid movement.

                     in the center of the formation.

     3-55. Combat formations allow a unit to move in the AO in a posture suited to the senior commander’s intent
     and mission. A unit may employ a series of combat formations during the course of an attack; each has its
     advantages and disadvantages. Subordinate units within a combat formation can employ their own combat
     formations consistent with their particular situation. The commander considers the advantages and
     disadvantages of each formation with both mounted and dismounted elements in the areas of leading,
     influencing, maintenance, firepower orientation, ability to mass fires, and flexibility when determining the
     appropriate formation for a given situation.

Movement Techniques

     3-56. All combat formations use one or more of the three movement techniques: traveling, traveling
     overwatch, or bounding overwatch (FM 3-90-1). The weapons troop should generally be behind some
     friendly element such as Infantry or Cavalry, and as such, speed is the critical factor in getting to a designated
     location. When supporting other formations, platoons need to be in a tactical formation suited to their specific
     situation.

11 May 2017                          ATP 3-21.91                                               3-19


---

Chapter 3

Traveling
     3-57. Continuous movement characterizes the traveling technique. It is best suited for situations in which
     enemy contact is unlikely and speed is important. (See figure 3-12.)

Legend: MGS = mobile gun system, N = north
                                Figure 3-12. Traveling movement technique

3-20  ATP 3-21.91                                                          11 May 2017


---

                                                                                                                                            Offense

Traveling Overwatch
     3-58. Traveling overwatch is an extended form of traveling that provides additional security when speed is
     desirable but contact is possible. The intent is to maintain depth, provide flexibility, and maintain the ability
     to maneuver if contact occurs. MGS is preferred vehicle to overwatch Infantry and Cavalry units. When the
     ATGM and MGS are moving in the same element, the ATGM should overwatch the MGS forward of its
     position. (See figure 3-13.)

    Legend: MGS = mobile gun system, N = north

                             Figure 3-13. Traveling overwatch movement technique

Bounding Overwatch
     3-59. Bounding overwatch is used when contact is expected. It is the most secure, but slowest, movement
     technique. The purpose of bounding overwatch is to deploy before contact, giving the unit the ability to
     protect a bounding element by immediately suppressing an enemy force. The troop can employ either of two
     bounding methods; alternate or successive.

11 May 2017  ATP 3-21.91  3-21


---

Chapter 3

     3-60. A technique to employ for the length of a bound is based on half the distance of the maximum effective
     range of weapon system or line of sight of the overwatching element. For example: When ATGM and MGS
     move together in a formation. MGS should bound no further than 2 km (roughly half the maximum effective
     range of the TOW) forward of the ATGM. This allows the overwatching element to cover the bounding
     element by allowing enough range to identify and engage an enemy. The bounding element selects a covered
     position within that range and then becomes the overwatching element.

     3-61. For MGS platoons this technique is simple having four vehicles and can divide into sections. ATGM
     platoons have difficulty due to a three vehicle formation and the TOW weapons system covering their
     movement. The TOW is limited in that it cannot shoot on the move and the missile flies at a relative slow
     rate of speed for covering the move. ATGM platoons work best conducting successive bounds with other
     units.

Alternate Bounds

     3-62. Covered by the rear element, the lead element moves forward, halts, and assumes overwatch positions.
     The rear element advances past the lead element and takes up overwatch positions. This sequence continues,
     as needed, with only one element moving at a time. This method is usually more rapid than successive
     bounds. This technique is used for MGS pure formations. (See figure 3-14.)

Successive Bounds

     3-63. In the successive bounding method, the lead element covered by the rear element, advances and
     assumes overwatch positions. The rear element then advances to an overwatch position abreast of the lead
     element, halts, and assumes overwatch. The lead element then moves to the next position, and so on. Only
     one element moves at a time, and the rear element avoids advancing beyond the lead element. This method
     is easier to control and more secure than the alternate bounding method, but it is slower.

     3-64. Successive bounds are preferred when ATGM and MGS work with Infantry and Cavalry units. When
     ATGM and MGS sections move with one another they use a mix of alternate and successive bounds between
     the formations. The MGS sections use alternate bounds between the two vehicles while the ATGM section
     uses successive bounds with the trail MGS vehicle. This movement is conducted in a platoon Vee formation.
     (See figure 3-15, page 3-24.)

3-22  ATP 3-21.91  11 May 2017

                          Figure 3-14. Bounding overwatch alternate bounds
