     restore the original position, or block an enemy penetration. Once launched, the counterattack becomes a
     decisive operation for the commander conducting the counterattack.

     3-183. To be decisive, the counterattack occurs when the enemy is overextended, dispersed, and
     disorganized during his attack. All counterattacks should be rehearsed under the same conditions in which
     they will actually be conducted. Careful consideration is given to the event that will trigger the counterattack.
     Once committed, the counterattack force conducts the decisive operation.

     3-184. SBCT’s conduct counterattacks assisted with the use of mission command systems and maneuver to
     a point of tactical advantage to initiate the attack with the enemy forces. They take advantage of the speed
     and protection of their vehicles to deploy and initiate their attacks.

DEMONSTRATIONS

     3-185. A demonstration is a form of attack designed to deceive the enemy as to the location or time of the
     decisive operation by a display of force. Forces conducting a demonstration do not seek contact with the
     enemy as intended. The ATGM and MGS make use of their direct fire weapons systems on long range or
     unoccupied targets to display their accuracy or lethal capability during a demonstration.

FEINTS

     3-186. A feint is a limited attack used to deceive the enemy as to the location or time of the actual decisive
     operation. Forces conducting a feint seek direct fire contact with the enemy, but avoid decisive engagement.
     As in the demonstration, the commander uses feints in conjunction with other military deception activities.

RAID

     3-187. A raid is a limited-objective, deliberate attack that entails swift penetration of hostile terrain. It is
     not intended to hold territory and has to have a planned withdrawal. A raid requires detailed intelligence,
     preparation, and planning. The elements of the SBCT weapons troop support raids as part of a larger force.
     They will likely target the enemies most destructive weapon system or highest value target to support the
     initiation of the raid. Raids are designed to accomplish a number of missions such as—

                z Capture prisoners, installations, or enemy materiel.
                z Capture or destroy specific enemy command and control locations.
                z Destroy enemy materiel or installations.
                z Obtain information concerning enemy locations, dispositions, strength, intentions, or methods

                     of operation.
                z Confuse the enemy or disrupt his plans.
                z Liberate friendly personnel.

SPOILING ATTACK

     3-188. A spoiling attack is a tactical maneuver employed to seriously impair a hostile attack while the
     enemy is forming or assembling for an attack. A spoiling attack usually employs armored, attack helicopter,
     or fire support elements to attack on enemy assembly positions in front of a main line of resistance or battle
     position (FM 3-90-1).

     3-189. The objective of a spoiling attack is to disrupt the enemy’s offensive capabilities and timelines while
     destroying targeted enemy personnel and equipment, not to secure terrain and other physical objectives.
     Conditions when conducting spoiling attack are:

                z The spoiling attack’s objective needs to be obtainable. The enemy should be unable to respond
                     to the attack in a synchronized and coordinated manner.

                z The commander prevents the force conducting the spoiling attack from becoming over
                     extended.

3-46  ATP 3-21.91  11 May 2017


---

                          Offense

  SECTION IV – TRANSITIONS

     3-190. The commander halts an offensive task when it results in complete victory and ends hostilities,
     reaches a culminating point, or the commander receives a change in mission from a higher commander.

CONSOLIDATION

     3-191. Consolidation is organizing and strengthening a newly captured position so that it can be defended.
     The attacking unit tries to exploit its success regardless of the assault type. In some situations, however, the
     unit may have to consolidate its gains. Consolidation may vary from a rapid repositioning of forces and
     security elements on the objective, to a reorganization of the attacking force, to the organization and detailed
     improvement of the position for defense.

     3-192. Consolidation comprises actions taken to secure the objective and defend against an enemy
     counterattack. Consideration should be given that the enemy that occupied the objective may have planned
     indirect fire targets to assist in their counterattack. The commander ensures that the SBCT weapons troop is
     ready to conduct the following actions that usually are part of consolidation:

                z Eliminate enemy resistance on the objective.
                z Establish security beyond the objective by securing areas that may be the source of enemy direct

                     fires or enemy artillery observation.
                z Establish additional security measures such as OPs and patrols.
                z Prepare for and assist the passage of follow-on forces (if required).
                z Continue to improve security by conducting defensive actions. These defensive actions include

                     EA development, direct fire planning, and battle position preparation.
                z Adjust final protective fire (FPF), register indirect fire weapons systems, and assign targets

                     along likely mounted and dismounted avenues of approach.
                z Protect the obstacle reduction effort.
                z Secure EPWs and detainees.
                z Prepare for the enemy counterattack.

REORGANIZATION

     3-193. Reorganization is usually conducted concurrently with consolidation. It comprises actions taken to
     prepare the SBCT weapons troop for follow-on missions. As with consolidation, the SBCT weapons troop
     commander plans and prepares for reorganization as he conducts his TLPs. He ensures that the SBCT
     weapons troop takes the following actions:

                z Provide appropriate first aid, TC3, and evacuate casualties as needed.
                z Process EPW’s and detainees.
                z Treat and evacuate wounded EPWs and detainees, and process the remainder of EPWs and

                     detainees.
                z Cross-level personnel and adjust task organization when necessary to support the next phase or

                     mission. Link-up with or acquire attachments or detachments.
                z Conducts resupply operations, to include rearming and refueling.
                z Redistribute ammunition.
                z Conduct required maintenance.
                z Continue improvement of defensive positions, as needed.

CONTINUING OPERATIONS

     3-194. For all attacks, the SBCT weapons troop should plan to exploit success. However, at the conclusion
     of an engagement, the commander may be forced to defend or be relieved by follow on forces to continue
     operations. Units make use of the terrain to enhance their survivability for short defensive tasks. If a longer
     defense is envisioned, engineer assets immediately should shift the priority of their efforts to provide
     survivability support (fighting positions and similar activities). Engineer assets should do this even as they
     sustain mobility and integrate countermobility into the planned defense. The SBCT weapons troop

11 May 2017  ATP 3-21.91  3-47


---

Chapter 3

     commander considers the higher commander’s concept of operations, friendly capabilities, and the enemy
     situation when making the decision to defend or continue offensive tasks.

TRANSITION TO DEFENSIVE TASKS

     3-195. As offensive tasks approach a culmination, the commander orders a transition to a defense. The
     commander uses two basic techniques when he transitions to the defense. The first technique requires the
     leading elements to commit forces and move forward to claim enough ground to establish a security area
     anchored on defensible terrain. The second technique is to establish a security area along the unit’s final
     positions, moving the main body rearward to defensible terrain.

     3-196. The commander anticipating the termination of unit offensive actions prepares orders that include
     the time or circumstances under which the offense transitions to a defensive-focused mission, the missions
     and locations of subordinate units, and mission command measures. The commander takes the following
     actions as the unit transitions from an offensive to a defensive task:

                z Maintains contact and information collection on the enemy using a combination of
                     reconnaissance units and surveillance assets to develop the situation and facilitate planning of
                     future operations.

                z Establishes a security area and local security measures (to include biometric identity data
                     collection).

                z Redeploys indirect fire assets to ensure the support of security forces.
                z Redeploys forces based on probable future employment.
                z Maintains or regains contact with adjacent units in a contiguous AO and ensures that units

                     remain capable of mutual support in a noncontiguous AO.
                z Transitions the engineer effort by shifting the emphasis from mobility to countermobility and

                     survivability.
                z Consolidates and reorganizes.

TRANSITION TO STABILITY TASKS

     3-197. Upon order from higher headquarters, the commander orders a transition to operations focused on
     stability tasks. These tasks establish a safe, secure environment that facilitates reconciliation between local
     or regional threats. Stability tasks aim to establish conditions that support the transition to legitimate host
     nation (HN) governance, a functioning civil society, and a viable market economy.

     3-198. The SBCT weapons troop commander ensures that contingencies are planned for to transition
     quickly from offensive to stability tasks and vice versa. For example, it may be wise for commanders to plan
     a defensive contingency with an on-order offensive mission for stability tasks that could deteriorate.

     3-199. Subordinate leaders need to be fully trained to recognize activities that would initiate this transition.
     Actions in one unit’s AO can affect a change in whatever type task an adjacent unit is conducting. (For
     example, an offensive action may cause noncombatants to be displaced to another section of the city creating
     a need to support operation for the unit in that AO.)

CONCLUSION

     3-200. The SBCT weapons troop and its platoons best support offensive operations when they enable a
     flexible plan. Establishing command support relationships and spacing between units provide the operational
     flexibility supporting the plan. This allows the weapons troop commander to commit to contact with the
     enemy as the result of a deliberate decision. Transitioning them constantly to the main effort maintains the
     momentum and initiative for the SBCT.

3-48  ATP 3-21.91  11 May 2017


---

                                                 Chapter 4

                                    Defense

         A defensive task is a task conducted to defeat an enemy attack, gain time, economize
         forces, and develop conditions favorable for offensive or stability tasks (ADRP 3-0).
         The SBCT weapons troop uses the defense to mass the effects of direct fires from
         enemy approaches to defeat a numerically superior force in depth. The commander
         combines natural and man-made obstacles to canalize the attacking force into
         engagement areas. This chapter discusses the basics of the defense, defensive tasks,
         and forms of the defense, engagement area development, and transitions.

  SECTION I – BASICS OF THE DEFENSE

     4-1. As part of the higher echelon’s defensive tasks, the SBCT weapons troop may defend, delay, withdraw,
     or counterattack. The SBCT weapons troop may defend as part of the higher headquarters’ main battle area,
     or it may conduct autonomous defensive or stability tasks within a small scale contingency. The SBCTs
     weapons troop and its higher headquarters conduct operations to create conditions for success as enemy
     forces enter into the main battle area and by weakening the enemy prior to the commencement to close
     combat.

     4-2. The SBCT weapons troop usually defends in the main battle area against the most likely enemy
     armored threat. It may do this as the SBCT’s main effort oversaw by an SBCT Infantry battalion or task force
     element. It can also transition from the SBCT cavalry squadron in the security area en route to SBCT task
     forces in the main battle area. The SBCT weapons troop can conduct the defense to achieve one or more of
     the following actions:

             z Gain time.
             z Retain key terrain.
             z Support other missions.
             z Delay or fix the enemy in one area while friendly forces attack him in another.
             z Attrite enemy forces in depth.
             z Reinforce a friendly unit
             z React to an enemy penetration.

CHARACTERISTICS OF DEFENSE

     4-3. Successful defenses employ the characteristics of disruption, flexibility, maneuver, mass and
     concentration, operations in depth, preparation, and security (refer to FM 3-90-1 for more information).

DISRUPTION

     4-4. Defenders subvert an attacker’s tempo, formations and synchronization by countering his initiative and
     preventing him from massing overwhelming combat power. Disruption attacks the enemy’s will to fight and
     his means of effective command and control. Deep precision fires, long-range precision sniper fires,
     electronic countermeasures (jamming), scatterable mine, unexpected defensive positions, local
     counterattacks at all levels, and attacks delivered by a reserve force, combine to disrupt the enemy’s attack
     and break his will to continue offensive operations. Repositioning forces, implement aggressive protection
     measures, the employment of roadblocks, ambushes, checkpoints, and information operations combine to
     disrupt the threat of asymmetrical attack. These tasks disrupt enemy efforts to fight as a combined-arms team.

11 May 2017  ATP 3-21.91  4-1


---

Chapter 4

FLEXIBILITY

     4-5. The defender gains flexibility by sound preparation and task organization, disposition in depth,
     retention of reserves, repositioning, and effective mission command. The SBCT defense often displays rapid,
     simultaneous, and collaborative planning with flexible execution. Contingency planning permits flexibility.
     Flexibility also requires that the commander see the battlefield to detect the enemy’s scheme of maneuver
     early. The intelligence preparation of the battlefield (IPB) process determines likely enemy actions; while
     security elements confirm or deny those actions.

MANEUVER

     4-6. Maneuver allows the commander to take full advantage of the AO and mass and concentrate fires when
     desirable. Maneuver through movement in combination with fire, allows the commander to achieve a position
     of advantage over the enemy to accomplish the mission. It encompasses defensive actions such as defense
     support of civil authorities.

MASS AND CONCENTRATION

     4-7. The SBCT weapons troop helps shape the battle by massing the effects of overwhelming combat
     power. Effects are synchronized in time and space and should be rapid and unexpected so that they break the
     enemy’s offensive tempo and disrupt his attack. The commander arrays his combat power based on his
     assessment of the intelligence provided and the latest information from reconnaissance in a manner that
     overwhelms the enemy and provides the greatest impact on their forces while negating the risks to his own
     units. This can occur at a single decisive point or through multiple incursions in depth to achieve disruptive,
     destructive, and decisive effects upon the enemy’s attack.

OPERATIONS IN DEPTH

     4-8. Simultaneous application of all combat power throughout the area of operation improves the chances
     for success while minimizing friendly casualties. Quick, violent, and simultaneous action throughout the
     depth of the troops’ area of operations can attrite, confuse, and even degrade an enemy force when they are
     most exposed and vulnerable. This often happens when an enemy extends the lines of their attack and leaves
     an open flank vulnerable to counter attack. Such actions weaken the enemies’ morale and do not allow any
     early successes to build their confidence. Operations in depth prevent the enemy from gaining momentum in
     the attack. Synchronization of actions in decisive, shaping and sustaining efforts facilitate mission success.

     4-9. Alternate and supplementary positions, combat outposts, and mutually supporting strongpoint’s
     forward of the perimeter extend the depth. Fires are planned throughout the defensive area up to the maximum
     range of available weapons and reconnaissance assets. Fires move and reposition to maintain contact with
     enemy forces and observe NAIs in depth as the battle develops. During periods of reduced visibility, emplace
     obstacles around critical locations within the perimeter to disrupt the enemy’s plan, and add depth to the
     defense.

PREPARATION

     4-10. The troop commander determines likely enemy avenues of approach, likely enemy schemes of
     maneuver, where to kill the enemy, integration of obstacles, unit positioning, and integration of indirect fires,
     and he assigns missions accordingly. Defensive preparations include the following:

             z Conduct rehearsals to ensure synchronization. These include employment of the reserve and
                   counterattack forces.

             z Position forces in depth.
             z Reinforce terrain with obstacles that support the scheme of maneuver.
             z Improve routes between positions in depth.
             z Coordinate with assets forward in the battalion’s security area to gain combat information or early

                   warning.
             z Designate a reserve.

4-2  ATP 3-21.91  11 May 2017


---

                                                                                                                                           Defense

             z Enact survivability measures that involve action against conventional threats (preparation of the
                   engagement area, digging-in mission command nodes and caches, and similar actions) as well as
                   asymmetric threats (placing sensors near likely friendly fighting positions to prevent IEDs).

SECURITY

     4-11. Security operations are measures normally taken by the SBCT Cavalry squadron and Infantry battalion
     or higher echelons to protect itself against all acts designed to impair its effectiveness, and prevent the enemy
     from gaining an unexpected advantage. Because the SBCT weapons troop defends against the most likely
     enemy armored threat whose weapon systems have equal or great ranges than their own commanders must
     integrate Infantry and Cavalry elements working together to help identify the enemy. These elements must
     protect the ATGM and MGS variants allowing them to maneuver against the armored threat. The squadron
     and battalion ensures security by employing reconnaissance elements throughout the entirety of its assigned
     AO. The squadron and battalion may employ a counterreconnaissance force, combat outposts, or a force
     conducting a screen to provide security. ATGM and MGS platoons can augment those company level units
     to support the security tasks (see chapter 2 of this publication).

DEFENSIVE TASKS

     4-12. There are three basic defensive tasks—area defense, mobile defense, and retrograde. Each of these
     tasks contains elements of the others, and usually contains both static and dynamic aspects. The SBCT
     weapons troop provides firepower to support the SBCT in all three defensive tasks. They can defend AOs or
     positions, support security tasks or constitute the reserve as part of the SBCTs coordinated defense. They can
     also transition between these roles throughout the defensive operation.

     4-13. As part of defensive tasks, the SBCT weapons troop can defend, delay, withdraw, counterattack, and
     perform security tasks. The SBCT weapons troop supports the defense as outlined by the SBCT commanders
     intent normally aligned to defeat or destroy the enemy armored threat or to provide firepower at the decisive
     point of the operation. The SBCT weapons troop can conduct the defense to achieve one or more of the
     following actions:

             z Gain time.
             z Retain key terrain.
             z Support other missions.
             z Delay or fix the enemy in one area while friendly forces attack him in another.
             z Attrite enemy forces at a rapid rate while reinforcing friendly actions.

AREA DEFENSE

     4-14. The area defense is a defensive task that denies enemy forces access to designated terrain for a specific
     time rather than destroying the enemy outright (ADRP 3-90). The emphasis is on retaining terrain where the
     bulk of the defending force positions itself in mutually supporting positions and controlling the terrain
     between positions. The defeat mechanism is fires into EAs, which reserve units can supplement. The
     commander uses his reserve force to reinforce fires, add depth, block penetrations, restore positions, or
     counterattack to destroy enemy forces and seize the initiative.

Forms of Defensive Maneuver

     4-15. There are two forms of defensive maneuver within an area defense—defense in depth and forward
     defense. While the SBCT task force commander selects the type of area defense to use, the SBCT weapons
     troop commander often defines the general defensive scheme for his elements. The specific mission may
     impose constraints such as time, security, and retention of certain areas that are significant factors in
     determining how the troop will defend.

Defense in Depth

     4-16. A defense in depth reduces the risk of the attacking enemy force penetrating the defense quickly. The
     enemy is unable to exploit a penetration because of additional defensive positions employed in depth. The
     in-depth defense provides more space and time to defeat the enemy attack.

11 May 2017  ATP 3-21.91  4-3


---

Chapter 4

     4-17. The SBCT weapons troop uses a defense in depth when—
             z The mission allows the SBCT weapons troop to fight the depth of the AO.
             z The terrain does not favor a defense well forward, and there is better defensible terrain deeper in
                   the AO.
             z The terrain favors multiple positions of tactical advantage to engage enemy forces and displace
                   without becoming fixed or compromised.
             z Sufficient depth is available in the AO.
             z Weapons of mass destruction may be used.

Forward Defense

     4-18. The intent of a forward defense is to prevent enemy penetration of the defense. A forward defense is
     the least preferred due to its lack of depth. The SBCT task force commander will deploy majority of the
     weapons troop combat power into forward defensive positions near the forward edge of the battle area
     (FEBA). The ATGM and MGS positions should be protected by Infantry units in defensive positions that
     mutually support one another. The prepared positions of the ATGM and MGS must allow for fields of fire
     out of the SDZs of their weapon systems. The Infantry positions should optimally be forward of the ATGM
     and MGS hidden from enemy advancement and capable of engaging with their weapon systems. These
     elements should mass their fires on the enemy simultaneously to achieve the greatest lethal effects. While
     the SBCT task force may lack overall depth, the weapons troop and its platoons build depth into the defense
     at their levels. The commander fights to retain its forward position, and may conduct counterattacks against
     enemy penetrations, or destroy enemy forces in forward EAs. Often, counterattacks are planned forward of
     the FEBA to defeat the enemy.

     4-19. The SBCT weapons troop uses a forward defense when—
             z Terrain forward in the AO favors fields of fire, weapons range standoff, and good cover and
                   concealment.
             z Strong natural or man-made obstacles, such as a linear obstacle (river or a rail line), are located
                   forward in the AO.
             z The assigned AO lacks depth due to the location of the area or facility to be protected. Cover and
                   concealment in the rear portion of the AO is limited.
             z Directed by higher headquarters to retain or initially control forward terrain.

MOBILE DEFENSE

     4-20. The mobile defense is a defensive task that concentrates on the destruction or defeat of the enemy
     through a decisive attack by a striking force (ADRP 3-90). The mobile defense focuses on defeating or
     destroying the enemy by allowing enemy forces to advance to a point where they are exposed to a decisive
     counterattack by the striking force. The commander uses the fixing force to hold attacking enemy forces in
     position, to help channel attacking enemy forces into ambush areas, and to retain areas from which to launch
     the striking force. A mobile defense requires an AO of considerable depth.

     4-21. The SBCT weapons troop as a pure force in a mobile defense is either part of a fixing force or a striking
     force, but not both. It may have its subordinate elements task organized to support the fixing or striking force.
     As part of the fixing force, the SBCT weapons defends within its assigned AO. As part of the striking force,
     the SBCT weapons troop plans, rehearses, and executes offensive tasks.

             Note. Units smaller than a division do not usually conduct a mobile defense because of their
             inability to fight multiple engagements throughout the width, depth, and height of their AO, while
             simultaneously resourcing the striking, fixing, and reserve forces. Typically, the striking force in
             a mobile defense consists of one-half to two-thirds of the defender’s combat power.

     4-22. The striking force is a committed force and has the resources to conduct a decisive counterattack as a
     part of the mobile defense. The striking force decisively engages the enemy as he becomes exposed in his
     attempts to overcome the fixing force. The striking force normally attacks a moving enemy force, and is
     usually comprised of armored formations.

4-4  ATP 3-21.91  11 May 2017


---

                                                                                         Defense

RETROGRADE

4-23. The retrograde is a type of defense task that involves organized movement away from the enemy
(ADRP 3-90). The enemy may force these actions or the commander may execute them voluntarily. In either
case, the higher commander of the force executing the operation approves the retrograde. The intent of a
retrograde is to improve a tactical situation or to prevent a worse situation from developing. Company size
units execute retrogrades as part of a larger force, but may conduct independent retrogrades when necessary.

4-24.  Retrograde can accomplish the following:
       z Resist, exhaust, and defeat enemy forces.
       z Draw the enemy into an unfavorable situation.                                   other  friendly
       z Avoid contact in undesirable conditions.
       z Gain time.
       z Secure more favorable terrain
       z Reposition forces, shorten lines of communication, or conform to movements of

             units.
       z Disengage a force from an ongoing mission for use elsewhere in other missions.

4-25. Disengage is a tactical mission task where the commander has the unit break contact with the enemy
to allow the conduct of another mission or to avoid decisive engagement (FM 3-90-1). It involves moving to
a location where the enemy cannot engage the friendly force with either direct fires or observed indirect fires.
Disengaging from the enemy while displacing from one position to the next is a difficult procedure. A
disengagement plan includes the following:

        z The maneuver concept of operations for tactical elements after disengagement, along with the
             movement routes for each subordinate unit.

        z Fires to suppress the enemy and cover the unit’s movement.
        z Screening smoke to conceal the unit’s movement, as part of a deception operation, or to cover

             passage points.
        z Contact and passage points if moving through friendly lines.
        z The time disengagement starts.
        z The earliest time that functional and multifunctional support and sustainment elements move.

4-26. There are three types of retrograde—
        z Delay.
        z Withdrawal.
        z Retirement.

Delay

4-27. Delay is a task that allows the unit to trade space for time, avoiding decisive engagement and
safeguarding its elements. Delays gain time to—

        z Allow other friendly forces to establish a defense.
        z Cover a withdrawing force.
        z Protect a friendly force’s flank.
        z Allow other forces to counterattack.

4-28. The SBCT weapons troop conducts delays by initiating and breaking contact with enemy forces with
their ATGM and MGS fires to prevent from being decisively engaged. This technique forces the enemy to
deploy, and then reorganize to continue operations forcing them to use time to accomplish these tasks. The
ATGM and MGS fire and displace with at least two separate elements engaging a target and mutually
supporting each other’s maneuver. Indirect fires and Air support can further support the task as well as
engineers establishing counter mobility obstacles.

11 May 2017                                 ATP 3-21.91                                         4-5


---

Chapter 4

Withdrawal

     4-29. Withdrawal is a task that the commander uses to break enemy contact, especially when he needs to
     free the unit for a new mission. There are two types of withdrawals, assisted, and unassisted. The
     commander’s intent and METT-TC determine which type of withdrawal the unit uses.

Assisted Withdrawal

     4-30. The assisting force occupies positions to the rear of the withdrawing unit and prepares to accept control
     of the situation. It can assist the withdrawing unit with route reconnaissance, route maintenance, direct and
     indirect fire support, and sustainment. Both forces closely coordinate the withdrawal.

Unassisted Withdrawal

     4-31. The withdrawing unit establishes routes and develops plans for the withdrawal, then establishes
     security force as the rear guard while the main body withdraws. Sustainment and protection elements usually
     withdraw first, followed by combat forces. The detachment left in contact (DLIC) disengages from the enemy
     and follows the main body to its final destination as the unit withdraws.

Retirement

     4-32. Retirement is a task that moves a force that is not in contact away from the enemy. A retiring unit
     organizes for combat but does not anticipate interference by enemy ground forces. Typically, another unit’s
     security force covers the movement of one formation as the unit conducts a retirement. However, mobile
     enemy forces, unconventional forces, air strikes, air assaults, or long-range fires may attempt to interdict the
     retiring unit. The commander plans for enemy actions and organizes the unit to fight in self-defense. The
     commander usually conducts a retirement to reposition his forces for future actions or to accommodate the
     concept of the operation. Units conduct retirements as tactical road marches where security and speed are the
     most important considerations.

FORMS OF THE DEFENSE

     4-33. The three forms of the defense (defense of a linear obstacle, perimeter defense, and reverse-slope
     defense) have special purposes and require special planning and execution. The three forms of the defense
     provide distinct advantages for the defender and its subordinate units. (Refer to FM 3-90-1 for additional
     information.)

DEFENSE OF A LINEAR OBSTACLE

     4-34. A defense of a linear obstacle generally favors the use of a forward defense. Maintaining the integrity
     of the linear obstacle is the key to this type of defense. The defending unit constructs obstacles to stop the
     enemy forces and channel them into planned engagement areas. When attacked, the defending force isolates
     the enemy, conducts counterattacks, and delivers fires onto the concentrated force to defeat attempts to breach
     or cross the linear obstacle.

     4-35. Defense of a linear obstacle is often part of an economy of force measure. Forces that can counterattack
     and destroy the enemy may not be available immediately; therefore, defending forces must be able to—

             z Detect enemy penetrations early enough so that local counterattacks can defeat them.
             z Defend after being isolated.
             z Use reconnaissance elements, sniper teams, and other elements to detect enemy forces and request

                   indirect fire support.
             z Bring the fight to the enemy side of the obstacle to destroy its forces and disrupt enemy

                   preparations.
             z Use fires to their maximum effect.
             z Use its mobility to concentrate combat power.

PERIMETER DEFENSE

     4-36. A perimeter defense is a defense oriented in all directions. The SBCT weapons troop often use a
     perimeter defense when conducting operations in noncontiguous areas of operations. The perimeter defense
     presents no assailable flanks to the enemy and allows the defender to reinforce a threatened area rapidly.

4-6        ATP 3-21.91  11 May 2017


---

                                                                                                                                           Defense

     Some disadvantages of a perimeter defense include its isolation and the vulnerability of its concentrated units
     to enemy fires.

     4-37. The commander establishes a perimeter defense when the unit must hold critical terrain, such as a
     strong point, or when it must defend itself in areas where the defense is not tied in with adjacent units. Units
     can organize a perimeter defense to accomplish a specific mission, such as protecting a base or providing
     immediate self-protection, such as during resupply operations when all-around security is required. During a
     perimeter defense, leaders at all levels ensure that—

             z Units physically tie into each other.
             z Direct fire weapons use flanking fire to protect the perimeter.
             z Field artillery and mortars are protected.
             z Primary, alternate, contingency, and emergency communications systems are secure and

                    redundant.
             z Obstacles are employed.
             z Final protective fires are established.

REVERSE-SLOPE DEFENSE

     4-38. An alternative to defending on the forward slope of a hill is to defend on a reverse slope. The reverse-
     slope defense allows units to concentrate their direct fires into a relatively small area while being protected
     from the enemy’s direct observation and supporting fires. The defender can destroy the enemy’s isolated
     forward units through surprise and concentrated fires. The control of the forward slope is essential for
     success. Gaining control of the forward slope can be done by using dominating terrain behind the defenders
     or with the use of stay behind forces, such as reconnaissance and sniper teams, that can observe and call in
     fires on the attackers.

COMMON DEFENSIVE PLANNING CONSIDERATIONS

     4-39. Planning a defense is a complex effort requiring detailed planning and extensive coordination. The
     most complex for the SBCT weapons troop is transition to the defense after supporting the Cavalry Squadron
     screen or guard. Key to the transition is the coordinating passage of lines, resupply, security, and
     acknowledgement between the losing element (Cavalry squadron), gaining element (Infantry battalion), and
     the weapons troop. As a mission evolves, the commander knows he may need to shift his decisive and shaping
     operations to press the fight and keep the enemy off balance. The synchronization and coordination of
     activities within each warfighting function and between the various warfighting functions are critical to the
     success of the SBCT weapons troop.

MISSION COMMAND

     4-40. The SBCT battalion commander and the SBCT commander usually define where and how they will
     defeat or destroy the enemy. The weapons troop commander refines where he will integrate the other
     warfighting functions into a coherent whole to mass the effects of combat power at the decisive place and
     time. (Refer to ADRP 6-0 for more information on mission command.) He contacts the anticipated units
     about the possible coordination points, linkups, handover’s and passage lanes through main defensive areas.
     The weapons troop commander defines how he envisions the SBCT weapons troop will execute its portion
     of the brigade fight based off the commander’s intent one and two levels up with associated taskforces.

MOVEMENT AND MANEUVER

     4-41. Effective movement and maneuver is critical to the SBCT troop success in the defense. The weapons
     troop has the most firepower with its direct fire weapons systems in the SBCT. For the SBCT to capitalize
     the weapons troop will have to move to the position of advantage against the armored threat who is the
     enemy’s main effort. This force will seek to remain hidden to exploit a disadvantage in the of the SBCTs
     defensive operation. The SBCT weapons troop uses movement and maneuver in conjunction with mission
     command to arrive at the decisive point of the defense or attrits the enemy by piecemeal in depth.

11 May 2017  ATP 3-21.91  4-7


---

Chapter 4

INTELLIGENCE

     4-42. The weapons troop commander describes the anticipated enemy actions throughout the SBCT AO
     beginning with the security area. He should request timely intelligence briefs from the task force intelligence
     sections, such as the COIST, MICO, or S-2, to answer PIR that are integrated into specific NAIs. This allows
     the SBCT weapons troop commander to decide where to place his forces and what units to coordinate with
     or monitor to get the latest combat information. (Refer to ATP 2-01.3 for more information.)

     4-43. As with all tactical planning, intelligence preparation of the battlefield (IPB) is a critical part of
     defensive planning. IPB is the systematic process of analyzing the mission variables of enemy, terrain,
     weather, and civil considerations in an area of interest to determine their effect on operations. IPG helps the
     commander define where to concentrate combat power, where to accept prudent risk, and where to plan
     potential decisive operations. The IPB presents all feasible enemy COAs to aid in the development of a
     flexible defensive plan. Accessing those intelligence products and comparing them give the weapons troop
     commander a better understanding of how the enemy will fight, and the enemy’s plan for the operation. The
     essential areas of analysis for IPB are—

             z Analyze terrain and weather.
             z Determine enemy force size and likely COAs with associated decision points. Specifically choke

                   points and areas the enemy stages before an assault.
             z Determine enemy vulnerabilities and high-value targets.
             z Impact of civilian population on the defense.

     4-44. The commander makes his determination of how and where to defeat the enemy on where he believes
     the enemy will go, the terrain, and the forces available. The SBCT task force commander may define a defeat
     mechanism that includes the use of single or multiple counterattacks to achieve success. The weapons troop
     commander analyzes his unit’s role in the supported SBCT task force fight to determine how to achieve
     success.

FIRES

     4-45. For the fire support plan to be effective in the defense, the SBCT weapons troop plans and executes
     fires in a manner that achieves the intended task and purpose of each target. Indirect fires serve a variety of
     purposes in the defense, to include the following:

             z Slow and disrupt enemy movement.
             z Prevent the enemy from breaching.
             z Destroy or delay enemy forces at obstacles using massed fires or precision-guided munitions.
             z Disrupt enemy support-by-fire elements.
             z Defeat attacks along dismounted avenues of approach with the use of FPF.
             z Disrupt the enemy to enable friendly elements to disengage or conduct counterattacks.
             z Obscure enemy observation or screen friendly movement during disengagement and

                   counterattacks.
             z Provide smoke screens to separate enemy echelons or to silhouette enemy formations to facilitate

                   direct fire engagement.
             z Provide illumination, as needed.
             z Execute suppression of enemy air defenses missions to support aviation missions.

     4-46. The FSV attached to the weapons troop also can serve a variety of roles in the defense. It can serve as
     the primary observer for the weapons troop, it can co-locate with the weapons troop commander for security
     and mission command functions, or it can serve as an alternate means of mission command placed at a
     different location of the defensive scheme of maneuver.

     4-47. The SBCT weapons troop commander evaluates the air integration plan with indirect fire systems
     available to provide support when developing the fire plan. Considerations, when developing the plan,
     include tactical capabilities, weapon’s ranges, available munitions, time on station, communication and
     control, airspace management, and marking of enemy and friendly units. These factors help the SBCT
     weapons troop commander and FSO determine the best method for achieving the task and purpose of each

4-8  ATP 3-21.91  11 May 2017


---

                                                                                                                                           Defense

     target in the fire plan. The ATGM and MGS platoons contribute significantly to fire support. Effective
     positioning is critical to optimize the use of their optics. They may engage targets using their optics and range
     finders and laser designators for indirect fires ensuring the vehicles do not give away their positions. They
     adjust indirect fires onto the targets, synchronize effects, assist to clear airspace and guide aircraft onto
     targets.

SUSTAINMENT

     4-48. Besides the sustainment functions required for all missions, the SBCT weapons troop commanders’
     planning process includes supply trains that are coordinated for their current position and are also flexible
     enough to support contingency operations where the SBCT weapons troop may have to reposition, or change
     task organization during the operation. For defensive plans that involve depth and multiple decision points,
     an area support scheme in coordination with FSC commanders and task force XO’s is best suited to support
     the weapons troop. Class I, III, V and IX supply point coordination as well as vehicle recovery and
     maintenance, and CASEVAC procedures have been rehearsed.

     4-49. The commander’s mission analysis may reveal that the troop’s ammunition requirements during an
     upcoming mission exceed its basic load. This requires the troop to preposition ammunition caches. The troop
     usually positions ammunition caches at alternate or subsequent positions. The troop may construct
     survivability positions to house these caches and guard them to prevent their capture or destruction by the
     enemy.

     4-50. The SBCT weapons troop relies on the supporting task force trains to provide immediate recovery and
     medical support. Clear assignments of which task force each element of the weapons troop is assigned to and
     the scheme of support are crucial for the weapons troop. To mitigate confusion and provide the most
     alternatives, the SBCT weapons troop commander ensures all of his elements know the casualty evacuation
     procedures and the locations of their nearest task force combat and field trains CCPs and battalion aid station.
     The weapons troop commander analysis determines the most effective measures for every mission, which
     includes the decision to evacuate or recover forward and consolidate based on the tactical situation.

PROTECTION

     4-51. Air and missile defense support to the SBCT may be limited. The SBCT weapons troop protects itself
     primarily by passive measures. The SBCT weapons troop should use concealment to protect from enemy air
     threats such as rotary-wing, fixed-wing, or class III UAS and use their organic weapons systems for self-
     defense against smaller enemy air threats such as class II and below UAS or lighter than air aircraft.
     Techniques include good concealment, tactical movement, active pace and changing location and task
     organization in the operating area.

     4-52. The SBCT weapons troop may use digging assets for ammunition caches. It is not recommended to
     emplace survivability positions for ATGM and MGS vehicles because they must displace in order to prevent
     themselves from being located or fixed.

ADDITIONAL PLANNING CONSIDERATIONS

     4-53. Additional defensive task planning considerations include missions in urban environment, mountain,
     desert, and jungle terrain.

URBAN ENVIRONMENT

     4-54. Weapons troop elements defend urban areas to defeat an attack, gain time, economize forces, protect
     infrastructure, protect a populace, and shape conditions for offensive or stability tasks. Usually two or more
     of these tasks apply to the urban defense.

     4-55. Success depends on synchronizing the following simultaneous operations as one action:
             z Decisive operation may not be effective if the tasks mentioned previously are not integrated into
                   the overall mission plan.
             z Shaping operations vary greatly depending on the type of defense and create the conditions for
                   decisive operations.

11 May 2017  ATP 3-21.91  4-9


---

Chapter 4

             z Sustaining operations ensure freedom of action, secure lines of communications, and establish
                   movement control.

     4-56. The weapons troop commander considers restrictions to the attacker’s ability to maneuver and observe.
     The Stryker vehicles can establish blocking positions at intersections, streets, alleyways, and entrances to
     structures. By using the terrain and fighting from well-prepared and mutually supporting positions, a
     defending force can delay, block, fix, or destroy a much larger attacking force. The defense of a built-up area
     is organized around key terrain features, buildings, and areas that preserve the integrity of the defense and
     provide the defender with ease of movement to include above ground and subterranean areas. The defender
     organizes and plans his defense by observation and fields of fire, avenues of approach, key terrain, obstacles,
     cover and concealment (OAKOC). (Refer to ATTP 3-06.11 for more information.) (See figure 4-1.)

     4-57. In a built-up area, the defender takes advantage of the cover and concealment afforded by the urban
     environment. The SBCT weapons troop maximizes the use of their weapon systems by placing them in areas
     with long engagement ranges and narrow fields of fire. Optimally, these locations are points where the enemy
     mounted forces have to slow down or stop, providing the perfect time to engage the target. Further enhance
     the advantages of the urban defense by constantly displacing to secondary and alternate positions. Infantry
     and Cavalry should support the weapons troop by providing local security.

     4-58. The ATGM require more TOW 2A and TOW 2B missiles if the enemy is armored or is vehicular
     equipped force. Gunners should prepare to fire the TOW 2B using line-of-sight mode when their vehicles are
     emplaced inside buildings. Use the TOW BB when attackers have occupied buildings or fortifications.

     4-59. The SBCT weapons troop must be aware of sewers and underground tunnels. The ATGM and MGS
     vehicles may be too large to use those features but enemy forces may use them to bypass their position.
     Consider the use of obstacles and dismounted observation posts to control access to these features.

     4-60. Before occupation of buildings is included in the defensive plan, the buildings must be inspected to
     make sure they are acceptable. This requires a review of the floor to ensure it can hold the weight of the
     ATGM and MGS vehicle, the ceiling is tall enough to allow the vehicle to enter and exit the building, and
     the backblast area is sufficient that the structure will not crumble. Factories, civic buildings, and other heavy
     structures, which provide adequate cover and are more suitable for defending, may be found deeper in the
     town and will offer more limited fields of fire on the likely avenues of approach.

     4-61. When elements of the SBCT weapons troop are attached, gaining commanders should employ them to
     take advantage of their long-range fires and mobility. Some built up areas may restrict the mobility of these
     vehicles and make them vulnerable to the enemy. Mutually supporting Infantry should be positioned to
     provide security against close enemy direct fires and to identify targets. ATGM and MGS elements should
     be assigned EAs in support of the defensive scheme of maneuver. The Infantry unit’s antiarmor weapons
     should supplement the TOW and MGS main gun. Antiarmor weapon systems may—

             z Be positioned on the edge of the city in mutually supporting positions.
             z Be positioned on key terrain on the flanks of towns and villages.
             z Be used to cover barricades and obstacles by fire.
             z Be part of the reserve.
             z Use thermal sights to detect enemy activity through darkness and most smoke.
             z Monitor large areas of a city while making their presence known to the local populace (when

                   acting as part of a mounted patrol).
             z Add security to resupply convoys with their mobile firepower.
             z Resupply units quicker and with more ammunition than a resupply by foot.

4-10  ATP 3-21.91  11 May 2017


---

                          Defense

    Legend: Û= degree, max = maximum

          Figure 4-1. Elevation and depression graphic for engaging in urban environment

DEFENSIVE TASKS IN MOUNTAINOUS TERRAIN

     4-62. Defensive tasks in a mountainous terrain are conducted to resist, defeat, or destroy an enemy attack to
     support subsequent offensive actions. Commanders use defensive tasks to withstand an enemy attack while
     preparing to seize the initiative and develop conditions favorable for transitioning to the offense. During a
     defense, friendly forces withstand enemy attacks and hold the enemy while preparing to seize the initiative
     and transition to an attack or to conduct stability tasks. A thorough understanding of the commander’s intent
     is especially critical in the defense, which demands precise integration of all assets to include maneuver and
     sustainment.

     4-63. Stryker units operating in mountainous terrain environments often possess weapons and equipment
     more advanced in technology than the enemy. Knowing this, enemy offensive tactics commonly involve

11 May 2017  ATP 3-21.91  4-11


---

Chapter 4

     short violent engagements followed by a hasty withdrawal through preplanned routes. The enemy often
     strikes quickly and fights only as long as the advantage of the initial surprise is in their favor. Attacks may
     include direct fires, indirect fires, or mines and may be against stationary or moving forces. (Refer to
     ATTP 3-21.50 for more information.) In the defense of mountainous terrain, the Stryker vehicles uses their
     mobility to maneuver to places of advantage, while the infantry secure severely restricted and key terrain.
     ATGM and MGS use the range and firepower of their weapon systems to neutralize enemy that shoot from
     built up positions, defilade, cave openings and other terrain.

DESERT TERRAIN

     4-64. In the desert terrain, the SBCT weapons troop orients on primary enemy approaches in their assigned
     area of operation, units prepare for attack from any direction. It is neither possible nor necessary to have
     maximum firepower in all directions, move weapons to threatened areas before the enemy reaches them. Air
     cover or an air defense umbrella is necessary for a successful defense. (Refer to FM 90-3 for more
     information.) In general, some considerations for defensive tasks in a desert environment include, obstacles
     to site a defense are limited, and strong points to defend choke points and other key terrain, and mobility and
     sustainment are key factors. The SBCT weapons troop should take advantage of reverse slope defenses, and
     counter attacks from the enemy flanks whenever possible in desert environments to negate weapon ranges
     standoff. They should also deploy and engage from low ground such as depressions or whaddis and move to
     subsequent low ground positions to prevent from being detected. While moving into and staying in low
     ground elements of the weapons troop should be mindful of local security and early warning.

JUNGLE TERRAIN

     4-65. Jungle defensive operations are based on the same defense fundamentals used in other operations.
     Some of the fundamentals, however, may acquire a special significance in the jungle. (Refer to FM 90-5 for
     more information.) Considerations for defensive operations in a jungle environment are limited visibility and
     fields of fire, limited and restricted maneuver. ATGM vehicles are isolated to clear areas with open fields of
     fire. This includes most road ways and over passes. The MGS can take advantage of its canister rounds at
     close range in semi restricted terrain to engage enemy in undergrowth.

COMMON DEFENSIVE CONTROL MEASURES

     4-66. The commander uses control measures to control the defense and provide flexibility to respond to
     changes in the situation. Control measures allow the defending commander to rapidly concentrate combat
     power at the decisive point and shift locations as the enemy reacts. Defensive control measures within the
     commander’s AO include designating the security area, the battle handover line (BHL), the main battle area
     (MBA) with its associated FEBA, and the echelon support area.

     4-67. The commander can use battle positions and additional direct fire control and fire support coordination
     measures (FSCMs) to further synchronize the employment of combat power. The commander designates
     disengagement lines to trigger the displacement of subordinate forces. He may also use passage lanes for
     forces that may have to pass through unit’s engagements areas such as scouts or other elements of the SBCT
     weapons troop. (Refer to ADRP 3-90 for more information.)

BATTLE HANDOVER LINE

     4-68. The BHL is a designated phase line on the ground where responsibility transitions from the stationary
     force to the moving force and vice versa.

BATTLE POSITIONS

     4-69. A BP is a defensive location oriented on a likely enemy avenue of approach. Units as large as battalion
     task forces and as small as squads or sections use BPs. They may occupy the topographical crest of a hill, a
     forward slope, a reverse slope, or a combination of these areas. The commander selects his positions based
     on terrain, enemy capabilities, and friendly capabilities. A commander can assign all or some subordinates
     BPs within the AO. Leaders within the weapons troop should manage integration of forces to maximize the
     firepower. Infantry should be in close proximity of their Stryker vehicles but far enough away from their
     weapons secondary effects, especially with the ATGM and MGS vehicles.

4-12  ATP 3-21.91  11 May 2017


---

                                                                                                                                     Defense

4-70. There are five types of BPs. They are as follows:
        z Primary positions cover the area where the commander intends to contain or destroy an enemy
             force (engagement area). (See figure 4-2 on page 4-14.)
        z Alternate positions are those assigned when the primary position becomes untenable or unsuitable
             for carrying out the assigned task. These positions allow the defender to carry out his original
             task. The following considerations apply for an alternate BP:
              It covers the same avenue of approach or sector of fire as the primary BP.
              It is located slightly to the front, flank, or rear of the primary BP.
              It may be positioned forward of the primary BP during limited visibility.
              It is employed to supplement or support positions with weapons of limited range.
        z Subsequent positions are those to which the unit expects to move during the course of the battle.
             A defending unit may have a series of subsequent positions. Subsequent positions can have
             primary, alternate, and supplementary positions associated with them.
        z Supplementary position is a defensive position located within a unit’s assigned AO that provides
             sectors of fire and defensible terrain along an avenue of approach that is not the enemy’s expected
             avenue of attack. (See figure 4-3 on page 4-14.) For example, an avenue of approach into a
             company’s AO from one of its flanks could require the weapons troop to direct its platoons to
             establish supplementary positions to allow the platoons to engage enemy forces traveling along
             that avenue. The platoon leader formally assigns supplementary positions when the platoon covers
             more than one avenue of approach.
        z Strong point is a heavily fortified BP tied to a natural or reinforcing obstacle to create an anchor
             for the defense or to deny the enemy decisive or key terrain. The mission to create and defend a
             strong point implies retention of terrain to stop or redirect enemy formations. Strong points require
             extensive time, engineer support, and class IV resources to construct. A strong point is also used
             to—
              Canalize enemy forces. Canalize is a tactical mission task in which the commander restricts
                   enemy movement to a narrow zone by exploiting terrain coupled with the use of obstacles,
                   fires, or friendly maneuver.
              Contain enemy forces. Contain is a tactical mission task that requires the commander to stop,
                   hold, or surround enemy forces or to cause them to center their activity on a given front and
                   prevent them from withdrawing any part of their forces for use elsewhere.

11 May 2017  ATP 3-21.91  4-13


---
