    Legend: BP = battle position, ENY = enemy
                       Figure 4-2. Primary, alternate, and subsequent battle positions

Legend: BP = battle position, ENY = enemy
                                  Figure 4-3. Supplementary battle position

4-14  ATP 3-21.91                                                                       11 May 2017


---

                                                                                                                                           Defense

FORWARD EDGE OF THE BATTLE AREA

     4-71. The FEBA is the foremost limits of a series of areas in which ground combat units are deployed.
     Excluded areas are those in which the covering or screening forces are operating, designated to coordinate
     fire support, the positioning of forces, or the maneuver of units. These units normally require no fire areas
     (NFAs) placed over their position to prevent fratricide.

     4-72. A passage lane is a lane through an enemy or friendly obstacle that provides a safe passage for a
     passing force (FM 3-90-2). The elements of the SBCT weapons troop use a passage lane when transitioning
     from the FEBA to the main battle area. This occurs after the screen or guard line collapses and a handover of
     the SBCT weapons troop is underway. The SBCT weapons troop moves through a passage lane normally
     towards its rear of another taskforce to support the defense. This is normally within the SBCT commander’s
     scheme of defensive maneuver to preserve combat power for the main effort.

MAIN BATTLE AREA

     4-73. The MBA is the area where the commander intends to deploy the bulk of the unit’s combat power and
     conduct decisive operations to defeat an attacking enemy. The defending commander‘s major advantage is
     the ability to select the ground on which the battle takes place. The defender positions subordinate forces in
     mutually supporting positions in depth to absorb enemy penetrations or canalize them into prepared EAs,
     defeating the enemy‘s attack by concentrating the effects of overwhelming combat power. The natural
     defensive strength of the position determines the distribution of forces in relation to both frontage and depth.
     Defending units typically employ field fortifications and obstacles to improve the terrain‘s natural defensive
     strength. The MBA includes the area where the defending force creates an opportunity to deliver a decisive
     counterattack to defeat or destroy the enemy.

  SECTION II – AREA DEFENSE

     4-74. There are two forms of defensive maneuver within an area defense—defense in depth and forward
     defense. The SBCT weapons troop can do both. The SBCT weapons troop commander is given the type of
     area defense to use, and develops the general defensive scheme for the troop. He selects terrain to defend
     from based on the weapons capabilities of the enemy and considers his own. If he has an advantage with
     range and standoff for example the terrain selected would offer fields of fire allowing engaging of targets at
     long distances such as areas with linear obstacles. If the enemy has the advantage of range then the
     commander may select covered terrain, reverse slopes, and urban areas that allow the ATGM and MGS to
     mass fires, attrite enemy in depth, or reposition after engagements. The specific mission may impose
     constraints such as time, security, and retention of certain areas that are significant factors in determining
     how it will defend.

AREA DEFENSE IN DEPTH

     4-75. A defense in depth reduces the risk of the attacking enemy force penetrating the defense quickly. The
     enemy is unable to exploit a penetration because of additional defensive positions employed in depth. The
     in-depth defense provides more space and time to defeat the enemy attack.

     4-76. The SBCT weapons troop uses a defense in depth when—
             z The mission allows the SBCT weapons troop to fight throughout the depth of the AO.
             z The terrain does not favor a defense well forward, and there is better defensible terrain deeper in
                   the AO.
             z Sufficient depth is available in the AO.
             z Cover and concealment forward in the AO is limited.
             z Weapons of mass destruction may be used.

AREA FORWARD DEFENSE

     4-77. The intent of a forward defense is to prevent enemy penetration of the defense. A forward defense is
     the least preferred due to its lack of depth. The SBCT weapons troop deploys most of its combat power into
     forward defensive positions near the FEBA. The commander fights to retain its forward position, and may

11 May 2017  ATP 3-21.91  4-15


---

Chapter 4

     conduct counterattacks against enemy penetrations, or to destroy enemy forces in forward engagement areas.
     Often, counterattacks are planned forward of the FEBA to defeat the enemy.

     4-78. The SBCT weapons troop uses a forward defense when—
             z Terrain forward in the area of operation favors the defense.
             z Strong natural or man-made obstacles, such as a river or a rail line, are located forward in the area
                   of operation.
             z The assigned area of operation lacks depth due to the location of the area or facility to be protected.
                   Cover and concealment in the rear portion of the area of operation is limited.
             z Directed by higher headquarters to retain or initially control forward terrain.

ORGANIZATION OF FORCES

     4-79. The task force commander organizes his forces for an area defense into security force, main body,
     reserve and sustainment. Elements of the SBCT weapons troop can be found in all four.

SECURITY FORCES

     4-80. The Task force conducts reconnaissance and security by orienting sensors forward covering enemy
     likely avenues of approach as well as areas not directly covered by a ground element to detect the enemy. All
     detection must be confirmed or denied of enemy presence. The task force establishes security by placing
     platoon size units forward to screen. If a guard is required the task force may attach platoons from the
     weapons troop to a company size unit.

MAIN BODY

     4-81. In the defense Stryker units often place the Infantry defensive positions forward of the Stryker vehicle.
     Their Infantry position is less likely to be identified than the Stryker vehicle. With Infantry forward and
     Stryker vehicles towards their rear, and with MGS and ATGM vehicles further back; allows for concentrated
     direct fires to be massed all at once in an engagement area. Surface danger zones have to be carefully
     calculated to prevent fratricide considering TOW missiles and 105mm main gun direct fires. Adding the
     indirect fires from assets within range such as field artillery, battalion mortar platoon, and company mortar
     section further enhance concentration of effects.

RESERVE

     4-82. The task force reserve force is normally one of its Infantry rifle platoons. METT-TC determines its
     exact composition. The reserve force could consist of an entire Infantry rifle company, a mix of MGS and
     Infantry, an MGS platoon or an attached armored element if augmented. The reserve force should be mobile
     and capable of reinforcing a point of possible enemy penetration, used as the counter attacking force,
     conducting a spoiling attack, or as the task force commander designates.

SUSTAINMENT

     4-83. The sustainment element consists of the battalion trains and FSC. They are normally one terrain feature
     from the main body or co-located with the SBCT support area (see chapter 7 of this publication for more
     information). As with the tasks organization changes the SBCT weapons troop undergoes so must the
     maintainers and specific classes V and IX of supply for the ATGM and MGS variants.

PLANNING AN AREA DEFENSE

     4-84. A successful area defense integrates and synchronizes all assets on terrain preferable to the defender
     to provide fires against an enemy force at a point that they are vulnerable. The SBCT weapons troop
     commander decides where to concentrate the fires in his assigned area to defend, where to take risks, and
     adjusts as information on the attacking enemy force is gained. The keys to a successful area defense includes
     the—

             z Capability to concentrate effects.
             z Integration with Infantry elements.
             z Depth of the defensive area.

4-16  ATP 3-21.91  11 May 2017


---

                                                                                                                                           Defense

             z Reconnaissance and security.
             z Ability to take full advantage of the terrain (such as intervisibility lines, restricted terrain, natural

                   obstacles).
             z Flexibility of defensive tasks.
             z Timely resumption of offensive actions.

MISSION COMMAND

     4-85. During the troop leading procedures the SBCT weapons troop IPB determines how the commander
     envisions how the enemy will fight, and the enemy’s plan for the operation. The SBCT weapons troop
     commander determines where and how they will defeat or destroy the enemy aligned with the supported task
     forces commander’s guidance. This guidance synchronizes maneuver and fires schemes to mass the effects
     of combat power at the decisive place and time throughout the depth of the entire SBCT’s area defense. This
     occurs for the weapons troop even if the SBCT is fighting a forward defense.

     4-86. Coordination, decision points, with executable guidance from the SBCT and task force commanders
     are all analyzed by the weapons troop commander. He may assign subordinate leadership from his platoon
     leaders to coordinate directly with units based on the defensive scheme of maneuver to speed the transitions
     and passage of lines between various task force areas of operations. Key to the transitions and being in place
     to support the defensive scheme of maneuver is the linkup with friendly forces and having those elements
     guide them to their positions.

INTELLIGENCE

     4-87. The SBCT weapons troop commander assisted by his COIST if formed must obtain or develop
     intelligence products utilizing information obtained through IPB and conducting continuous analysis,
     processing, exploitation, and dissemination of intelligence throughout the mission. The COIST should
     leverage the intelligence enterprise while working closely with task force information collection assets to
     answer PIR that are integrated into specific NAIs. This allows the weapons troop commander to adjust his
     defensive preparation effort.

     4-88. IPB is a critical part of defensive planning. It helps the commander define where to concentrate combat
     power, where to accept prudent risk, and where to plan potential decisive operations. The IPB must present
     all feasible enemy courses of action to aid in the development of a flexible defensive plan. The essential areas
     are—

             z Analyze terrain and weather.
             z Determine enemy force size and likely course of action with associated decision points.
             z Determine enemy vulnerabilities and high value targets.
             z Impact of civilian population on the defense.

     4-89. The commander makes his determination of how and where to defeat the enemy on where he believes
     the enemy will go, the terrain, and the forces available. The task force may define a defeat mechanism that
     includes single or multiple counterattacks to achieve success. The weapons troop commander analyzes his
     unit’s role in the SBCT or task force defensive scheme of maneuver to determine how to achieve success.

     4-90. SBCT commanders must be aware that the enemy considers the ATGM and MGS high value targets
     because of the firepower their main weapon systems possess. Therefore the SBCT weapons troop commander
     must be aware of their locations, repositioning, and synchronization of fires to maintain lethality on
     battlefield. Information regarding compromise of the ATGM and MGS positions should be considered
     essential element of friendly information in the defense. An essential element of friendly information is a
     critical aspect of a friendly operation that, if known by the enemy, would subsequently compromise, lead to
     failure, or limit success of the operation and therefore should be protected from enemy detection. The enemy
     is likely to designate ATGM and MGS as high-priority targets to gain the advantage of freedom of maneuver
     against the SBCT defending units.

     4-91. The unit’s defensive plans address how the preparations and the conduct of area defense impacts the
     civilian population in the AO, and the commander’s legal obligations to the civilian population are met.
     Ideally, the HN government has the capability to conduct the five primary stability tasks. If they are unable

11 May 2017  ATP 3-21.91  4-17


---

Chapter 4

     to conduct the minimum essential stability tasks, the defending unit attempts to make up the shortfall. (Refer
     to chapter 5 of this publication for more information.)

MOVEMENT AND MANEUVER

     4-92. Once the SBCT commander has assigned AOs to his maneuver units, he must determine any potential
     gaps between units. The task force echelon should plan to cover these gaps with reconnaissance assets,
     aggressive patrolling from the company and local observation posts. The task force must plan local
     counterattacks to isolate and destroy any enemy that manages to penetrate through a gap in the AO. The task
     force commander should also plan to reposition units not in contact to mass the effects of combat power
     against an attacking enemy.

     4-93. The need for flexibility through the mobility of forces requires graphic control measures to assist in
     maneuver during local counterattacks and repositioning of forces. Specified routes, phase lines, attack- and
     support-by-fire positions, BPs, EAs, TRPs, and other fire control measures are required to synchronize
     maneuver effectively.

     4-94. In noncontiguous operations, the elements of the SBCT weapons troop have the dilemma to defend
     either on a broad front, or in an AO so large that it would be unrealistic to employ units in mutually supporting
     positions. This requires a judicious effort by the SBCT commander and his staff in determining the
     positioning of maneuver forces as well a strong effort placed on reconnaissance and security. During the
     terrain analysis, the commander and staff must look closely for choke points, intervisibility lines, and reverse-
     slope opportunities to take full advantage of the capabilities to mass firepower while providing protection for
     the Infantry. The SBCT commander may keep the SBCT weapons troop consolidated, task organize and
     distribute platoons to SBCT Infantry battalions, place them with the SBCT Cavalry squadron and transition
     to other units as the situation develops or combination of to defend his area.

Depth of the Defensive Area

     4-95. Depth for the SBCT and the SBCT task force is established by creating a security area, main battle
     area, and a support area. Task force and company elements create further depth within the areas by
     positioning their forces usually two thirds of their combat power forward and one third to the rear of the
     formation in their defensive positions. The SBCT unit plans branches and sequels into their defensive plan
     allowing criteria for each of the subordinate elements to reposition, reinforce, or counter attack. The SBCT
     weapons troop and its subordinate elements can participate at any other area within the defense, assigned to
     any other headquarters, or augmented with other forces.

Ability to Take Full Advantage of the Terrain

     4-96. SBCT units take full advantage of terrain in the defense due to the limited digging capability. MGS
     and ATGM should take advantage of intervisibility lines that allow maximum protection for the hull while
     limiting the exposure of the vehicle. Frequent fire and repositioning from preplanned alternate and
     supplementary positions further limit exposure.

Flexibility of Defensive Tasks

     4-97. SBCT units provide flexibility in the area defense through task organization, fighting as a combined
     arms team, mobility, information sharing, and initiative of subordinate elements in their area of operation.
     The SBCT weapons troop will likely have to reposition upon identification of the enemy armored threat.
     They will move to a position of advantage in the area defense and engage, attriting the enemy throughout the
     depth of the defense. For the weapons troop depth is possible even in a forward defense by transitioning
     backwards from taskforce to task force from the security area to the main battle area.

Timely Resumption of Offensive Actions

     4-98. The SBCT weapons troop may quickly transitions to offensive actions upon identified combat
     information that triggers the transition. Active reconnaissance and information collection efforts quickly
     identify opportunities to regain the initiative and begin offensive operations. This information is quickly
     shared through mission command systems. The key to resume the offense will be logistics with resupply of
     class I, III, and V for the SBCT weapons troop.

4-18  ATP 3-21.91  11 May 2017


---

                          Defense

FIRES

     4-99. For the fire support plan to be effective in the defense, the SBCT weapons troop plans and executes
     fires in a manner that achieves the intended task and purpose of each target. Indirect fires serve a variety of
     purposes in the defense, to include the following:

                z Slow and disrupt enemy movement.
                z Prevent the enemy from breaching.
                z Destroy or delay enemy forces at obstacles using massed fires or precision-guided munitions.
                z Disrupt enemy support-by-fire elements.
                z Defeat attacks along dismounted avenues of approach with final protective fire.
                z Disrupt the enemy to enable friendly elements to disengage or conduct counterattacks.
                z Obscure enemy observation or screen friendly movement during disengagement and

                     counterattacks.
                z Provide smoke screens to separate enemy echelons or to silhouette enemy formations to

                     facilitate direct fire engagement.
                z Provide illumination using infrared (IR) or traditional methods.
                z Execute suppression of enemy air defense missions to support aviation missions.

     4-100. The weapons troop commander and his FSO must consider echelon of fires from their available
     direct and indirect fire systems. When attached to a task force they may call for fire support from mortars,
     field artillery, CAS, and attack aviation. These factors help the SBCT weapons troop commander and FSO
     determine the best method for achieving the desired effects on each target in the fire plan and which asset
     engages which target. Characteristics of defensive fire plans include—

                z Ensuring fires assets are in positions to support maneuver forces within time constraints.
                z Targets are confirmed or denied by information collection efforts.
                z Targets are destroyed in the main battle area (Support area or MBA).
                z Establish and rehearse sensor-to-shooter communication links.
                z Integrate CAS or rotary-wing attack aviation to destroy enemy units, artillery assets, and

                     command and control nodes.
                z Integrate suppression of enemy air-defense effort.
                z Suppression and obscuration fire plan to support breaking contact with the enemy.
                z Prepositioned ammunition backed by prepackaged munitions stocks capable of rapid delivery.
                z Integration of primary and alternate observers to engage targets.
                z Fire support coordination used to disrupt, delay, or destroy enemy forces.
                z Air space graphic control measures are planned for expedient clearance of airspace. (See

                     chapter 7 for more information.)

SUSTAINMENT

     4-101. The SBCT weapons troop XO, 1SG, and supply sergeant coordinates with task force XO, S-4, and
     FSC XO for sustainment support. If a field trains command post is established in the BSB a representative
     from the SBCT weapons troop is placed there to ensure LOGPACs are configured correctly and transported
     to the correct locations. The following sustainment considerations apply to defensive planning:

                z Preposition ammunition, petroleum, oil, and lubricants, and barrier materiel in a centrally
                     located position.

                z Make plans to destroy those stocks, if necessary.
                z Resupply during limited visibility to reduce the chance of enemy interference.
                z Plan to reorganize to reconstitute lost sustainment capability.
                z Use maintenance support teams in the maintenance collection point to reduce the need for

                     recovering equipment in the brigade support area.
                z Consider and plan for the additional transportation requirements for movement of class IV

                     barrier materiel, mines, and prepositioned ammunition; plus the sustainment requirements of
                     additional engineer units assigned for preparation of the defense.

11 May 2017  ATP 3-21.91  4-19


---

Chapter 4

                z Plan for prepositioning and controlling ammunition on occupied and prepared defensive
                     positions.

                z MEDEVAC LZ’s are positioned far enough behind friendly forces to not compromise the
                     location of their position, out of range of enemy direct fire weapon systems, and out of
                     observation of enemy units. They must be close enough (such that the response time for the air
                     MEDEVAC is not greater than the time for a ground MEDEVAC) to provide timely response
                     and to get the casualty to the appropriate role of medical care.

                z Role 1 and 2 medical treatment facilities are positioned to respond as soon as tactically feasible
                     and possible. Ambulance exchange points are coordinated with higher echelons of planning,
                     with Role 1 and Role 2 AHS support elements

     4-102. The SBCT weapons troop commander’s mission analysis may reveal that the troop’s ammunition
     requirements during an upcoming mission exceed its basic load. This requires the task force to approve for
     pre-position ammunition caches between the FSC and the weapons troop for the emplacing the cache. The
     FSC usually positions ammunition caches at alternate or subsequent positions.

PROTECTION

     4-103. Survivability includes the construction of fighting positions, protective positions, and hardening
     facilities. These are prepared to protect vehicles, personnel, and weapons systems. All leaders must
     understand the survivability plan and priorities.

     4-104. The survivability effort for the defense must enable units to concentrate firepower from fixed
     positions. To avoid detection and destruction by the enemy, units move frequently and establish survivability
     positions quickly. To provide flexibility SBCT units should concentrate on counter mobility and mobility for
     engineer support. Survivability emplacement negates the Stryker’s greatest strength; the ability to displace
     and maneuver quickly even in the defense.

     4-105. The SBCT has limited engineer capacity. The maneuver commander determines the priority of effort
     between countermobility, mobility, and survivability. Countermobility construction and survivability
     activities must begin as soon as possible and often occur concurrently with the planning process. The progress
     requires detailed status updates and synchronization. Planning at the task force level to ensure subordinate
     engineers have adequate time for TLPs. The assistant brigade engineer, with guidance from the brigade
     engineer and brigade engineer battalion staff, plans for the brigade defense. During planning, defensive
     positions and mobile reserves are identified as well as gaining a thorough understanding of the tactical-
     obstacle effort of subordinate units. The brigade engineer battalion staff, in conjunction with the assistant
     brigade engineer, ensures that task force defensive plans are mutually supporting. The brigade engineer
     battalion staff and assistant brigade engineer track preparation by monitoring subordinate unit status reports
     and specific progress on obstacle emplacement and mobility improvements timelines, anticipating and
     resolving problems that may occur. The following planning considerations apply to engineer support:

                z Position site obstacles early and link them to natural and other manmade obstacles.
                z Plan situational obstacles based on time or event triggers and synchronize them with a decision

                     support template.
                z Plan multiple obstacle locations to support depth and flexibility in the defense. Ensure adequate

                     security for obstacle emplacement systems. Integrate triggers for the execution of situational
                     and reserve obstacles in the decision support template.
                z Focus the countermobility effort to encourage the enemy to maneuver into positions of
                     vulnerability where the task force and weapons troop intends to kill them.
                z Ensure adequate mobility support for withdrawing security forces, the reserve, the
                     counterattack force, and the repositioning of MBA forces.
                z Ensure the integration of survivability priorities for critical systems and units through the
                     development and implementation of an execution matrix and timeline.
                z The mobility effort for the defense must enable units to concentrate firepower by repositioning
                     constantly throughout the fight. To provide flexibility, units may need sequential, alternate, and
                     supplementary hasty positions and improved paths to get there.

4-20  ATP 3-21.91  11 May 2017


---

                                                                                                                                           Defense

PREPARING AN AREA DEFENSE

     4-106. The SBCT weapons troop uses the preparation time available to provide security and collect
     information for other forces to their rear by conducting security tasks, build the strongest defensive position
     possible, or refine counterattack plans.

ESTABLISH SECURITY

     4-107. The SBCT or SBCT task force commander may direct the establishment of a forward security area.
     The size of the security force is based on METT-TC and increases in its composition as the enemy situation
     is less certain. It must be able to destroy enemy reconnaissance forces, disrupt its advance guard and displace
     upon identification of the main body. This element must have clear displacement criteria to prevent it from
     becoming fixed or destroyed. The SBCT Cavalry squadron with the weapons troop is will execute the guard,
     if required, for the SBCT. A guard in support of a task force may require a company team mix of Cavalry,
     elements of the SBCT weapons troop, and SBCT Infantry. Screen security tasks require less combat power
     but the same elements.

     4-108. All units must maintain a high level of local security. Employment of patrols, establishment of
     observation posts, skillful use of UAS and sensors, and effective use of the terrain to conceal dispositions are
     essential for effective security.

     4-109. The SBCT weapons troop commander also considers the potential threat to the defense that
     noncombatants with access to communication and recording devices may present. Security measures such as
     shutting down telephone exchanges and cell phone towers, and preventing unauthorized personnel from
     moving in the defensive area, may be required. The SBCT weapons troop should request guidance from
     higher headquarters before implementing any defensive measures that could affect the civil population.

Deception and Operational Security

     4-110. As part of the defense, higher headquarters may have created a military deception operation and
     associated story to protect the force, cause early committal of the enemy, and mislead the enemy as to the
     true intentions, composition, and disposition of friendly forces. The SBCT weapons troop aids in the
     execution of a military deception plan to—

                z Exploit enemy prebattle force allocation and sustainment decisions.
                z Exploit the potential for favorable outcomes of protracted minor engagements and battles.
                z Lure the enemy into friendly territory exposing their flanks and rear to attacks.
                z Conceal the size operational forces committed to the defense and the supporting elements

                     building the engagement area.

     4-111. Defensive tasks contain branches and sequels that provide the commander decision points to exploit
     the situation. It is around these branches and sequels that military deception potentials exist. Specific
     deceptive actions the SBCT weapons troop commander can take to hasten exhaustion of the enemy offensive
     include, but are not limited to—

                z Deceiving the enemy of the defensive scheme of maneuver. This is often done by transitions
                     the SBCT weapons troop from the security area to the main battle area during the collapse of a
                     screen or guard. The enemy often mistakes it as the friendly force trying to break contact.

                z Luring the enemy into a decisive battle, the outcome of which facilitates branching or
                     sequencing to the offense.

                z Employing camouflage, decoys, false radio traffic, and movement of forces.

Main Battle Area Preparation

     4-112. The command group monitors preparatory actions and tracks the higher and adjacent unit situations
     and the enemy situation. The status of engineer equipment and progress is tracked to ensure synchronization
     with overall defensive plan. The SBCT weapons troop takes full advantage regarding preparation and
     adjustments of positions by using the line of site function on their mission command systems. This provides
     a quick means to develop an overlay that shows initial fields of fire.

11 May 2017  ATP 3-21.91  4-21


---

Chapter 4

4-113. Plans are refined based upon status of friendly force preparations, additional combat information
provided by the security force and intelligence updates. The SBCT task force staffs assure that defending
elements maintain a highly accurate view of the enemy, environment, and significant civilian factors.

Position the Reserve

4-114. The reserve is positioned away from hostile enemy fire and observation and positioned to commit
rapidly. The reserve commander should expect to receive specific planning priorities and triggers for
employment on each contingency. This allows the reserve commander to conduct quality rehearsals and to
anticipate his commitment as he monitors the fight. The composition of the reserve for the SBCT weapons
troop supporting a task force will normally be an MGS vehicle, section or platoon that may have attached
Infantry with it. For the SBCT the reserve may consists of an MGS platoon, or elements of SBCT weapons
troop augmented with Infantry based on METT-TC.

4-115. During preparation of the task force defense, the reserve sometimes conducts other tasks. The
commander might initially position his reserve in a forward location to deceive the enemy, obscure
subordinate boundaries, or show strength in an area where he intends to accept risk. The reserve could serve
in the forward security area and provide area security for the logistics sites or unoccupied areas of the AO.
However, the commander must consider the impact of these types of missions on his reserve force’s ability
to prepare for its critical role as the reserve during the MBA engagement.

Aviation Support

4-116. In defensive operations, the speed and mobility of aviation can help maximize concentration and
flexibility. During preparation for defensive operations, aviation units sometimes support the commander
with aerial reconnaissance and fires.

4-117. During the defense, aviation forces can attack deep against high-payoff targets, enemy
concentrations, and moving columns and they can disrupt enemy centers of gravity. The division will likely
employ attack reconnaissance helicopter units to attack follow-on echelons before they can move forward to
the close battle. Aviation forces might conduct screening operations, and might conduct guard operations of
an open flank in conjunction with ground forces.

4-118. Attack reconnaissance helicopters routinely support security area operations and mass fires during
the MBA fight. Synchronization of aviation assets into the defensive plan is important to ensure aviation
assets are capable of massing fires and to prevent fratricide. Detailed air-ground operation and coordination
are necessary to ensure efficient use of aviation assets.

Rehearsal

4-119. The SBCT weapons troop should rehearse its defense, if METT-TC permits. The commander can
use any of the five types of rehearsals. They are confirmation brief, backbrief, combined arms rehearsal,
support rehearsal, and battle drill.

4-120. The SBCT weapons troop commander ensures that the enabling forces are completely integrated
into the defensive scheme of maneuver. Rehearsals provide a mechanism for ensuring this integration.
Rehearsals should cover where the SBCT weapons troop commander sees where to disrupt the enemy forces,
when it will be fixed, where it will be destroyed, engagement and displacement criteria and counterattack.
Rehearsals should cover where the enemy force will conduct disruption points, when it will be fixed, where
it will be destroyed, engagement and displacement criteria, and counterattack.

MONITORING DEFENSE PREPARATIONS

4-121.     As the enemy closes on the AO, the final preparations typically include:
           z Final coordination for battle handover and passage of lines.
           z Positioning of situational obstacle employment systems.
           z Verification of communications status.
           z Evacuation of unused classes IV and V (obstacle materiel and ammunition) to prevent capture

                or loss to enemy action.
           z Withdrawal of engineer forces from forward areas.

4-22                  ATP 3-21.91  11 May 2017


---

                                                                                                                                           Defense

                z Linkup of mobility, protection, and sustainment assets with reserve or other supported combat
                     forces (if not previously accomplished).

                z Final positioning or repositioning of reconnaissance assets, security forces, and observers.
                z Positioning of teams to close lanes in obstacles or execute reserve obstacles.
                z Execution of directed, reserve, or situational obstacles.
                z Conduct mortar registration if primary observer for mortar targets.
                z Periodic situation updates and issuing of final guidance to subordinates.

     4-122. As units occupy positions, the SBCT weapons troop commander may have to make minor
     adjustments to area of operations, engagement areas, obstacles, battle positions, and other defensive
     coordination measures based on unanticipated METT-TC conditions. The SBCT weapons troop commander
     makes only critical changes to the defense plan due to the loss of time and wasted expenditure of
     unrecoverable Class IV barrier and obstacle material.

EXECUTING AN AREA DEFENSE

     4-123. In an area defense, the SBCT weapons troop fights mainly from positions of tactical advantage to
     concentrate combat power effects against attempted enemy breakthroughs and flanking movements. They
     must constantly reposition into subsequent or alternate positions. This allows them to adjust to the enemy as
     they react providing constant pressure on them.

GAIN AND MAINTAIN ENEMY CONTACT

     4-124. The security forces detect first with sensors forward to confirm or deny to gain and maintain contact
     with the enemy. Normally, this happens in the security area with a unit conducting a screen or a guard. The
     units in the security area are first concerned with identification of committed enemy units, their disposition,
     composition and strength. This information informs the task force staff, led by the S-2, to determine the
     enemy’s intent and direction of attack.

     4-125. The security force gains contact with the approaching enemy, reports their movements, avoids
     becoming fixed or destroyed, disrupts enemy reconnaissance, and withdraws as the enemy main body
     formations enter the security area. The commander can direct the security forces to disrupt, delay, or destroy
     lead or rear portions of enemy formations. The security forces can also be used as part of an effort to deceive
     the enemy as to the actual location of the MBA or conduct a rear passage of lines with the main body. The
     commander considers the follow-on missions of the security forces, their potential to be over-run or isolated,
     and the overall impact their direct combat achieves. Main battle forces monitor the reconnaissance and
     security force fight to remain abreast of the situation and maintain mutual support

DISRUPT THE ENEMY

     4-126. Within the main battle area, the task force executes shaping operations, to include the conduct of
     military deception operations, to disrupt the enemy regardless of their location within the area of operation.
     After making contact, the task force commander seeks to disrupt the enemy’s plan, their ability to control
     forces. Ideally, the results of the commander’s shaping operations should force a disorganized enemy, whose
     ability to synchronize its elements has been degraded. Once the process of disrupting the attacking enemy
     begins, it continues throughout a defensive task. Using indirect fires, air ground integration, and other
     available lethal and nonlethal effects during this phase of the battle allows the SBCT to—

                z Support the security force’s delaying action.
                z Disrupt or limit the momentum of the enemy’s attack.
                z Support the friendly force’s decisive operation by destroying high-payoff targets.
                z Divert the enemy’s attack.
                z Reduce the enemy’s combat power.
                z Separate enemy formations.

     4-127. The weapons troop fights best deploying from low ground areas in the center of the formation when
     fighting in open terrain and augmented with or supporting Infantry formations. The Infantry should occupy
     higher ground preferably to the flanks of the defensive position in covered and concealed terrain. As the

11 May 2017  ATP 3-21.91  4-23


---

Chapter 4

     weapon troop disrupts the enemy’s approach with the aid of obstacles they should force they enemy closer
     towards the flanks where they can be engaged unknowingly at close range by the Infantry. This disruption
     technique leads to the enemy becoming fixed.

Execution of Planned Indirect Fires

     4-128. The purpose of these obstacles is to force premature deployment of the enemy’s breaching assets,
     slowing the enemy’s advance and allowing for more effective engagement with indirect fires. These obstacles
     are planned against specific enemy attack options. Situational obstacles may support an essential fire support
     task. In this situation, an SBCT weapons troop may deploy forward to cover fire support elements with direct
     fires, and then withdraw to its own defensive positions within the MBA.

     4-129. During the MBA engagement, the SBCT and task force shift combat power and priority of fires to
     defeat the enemy’s attack. This may require—

                z Adjusting subordinates’ AOs and missions.
                z Repositioning of forces.
                z Shifting of the main effort.
                z Committing the reserve.
                z Modifying the original plan.

     4-130. Forward forces, obstacles, and fires within the MBA usually break the enemy’s momentum, reduce
     his numerical advantage, and force his troops into positions of vulnerability. The SBCT weapons troop
     masses fires (direct and indirect) to destroy attacking enemy forces as they enter the EAs.

Execution of Situational Obstacles

     4-131. The purpose of these obstacles is to force premature enemy deployment, thus slowing the enemy’s
     advance. This allows for more effective engagement with indirect fires and, therefore, forces early
     deployment of enemy breaching assets. These obstacles usually are planned and triggered about specific
     enemy attack options. Situational obstacles may support an essential task for fires support. In this situation,
     an SBCT weapons troop may be employed forward to cover fire support elements with direct fires, and then
     withdraw to its own defensive positions within the MBA.

FIX THE ENEMY

     4-132. The commander uses shaping operations to fix the enemy and allow friendly forces to execute
     decisive maneuver elsewhere. This could include numerous offensive or defensive tasks such as feint, raid,
     ambush, or show of force. The intent is to make the enemy move into the engagement area and friendly forces
     to initiate with fires to achieve surprise. Fixing the enemy takes the initiative away and allows the defending
     force to synchronize effects against them.

Maneuver

     4-133. In an area defense, the decisive operation occurs in the main battle area. This is where the effects of
     shaping operations and sustaining operations, combine with the decisive operation to defeat the enemy. The
     commander’s goal is to prevent the enemy’s further advance with a combination of effects from fires and air
     ground operations from positions overwatching obstacles, and synchronized with the deployment of the
     mobile reserves.

     4-134. Generating massed effects is especially critical for the commander conducting a defense of a large
     area against an enemy with a significant combat power advantage. The attacker has the ability to select the
     point and time of the attack, and the attacking enemy can mass forces at a specific point. The defending
     commander quickly determines the intent of the enemy commander and the effects of terrain. This allows the
     defender to maneuver to positions of tactical advantage and place a combination of effects from fires and air
     ground operations to restore a more favorable force ratio.

Main Battle Area Engagement

     4-135. The forward forces mission within the MBA is usually breaking the enemy’s momentum, reducing
     their numerical advantage, and forcing them into positions of vulnerability. The SBCT weapons troop does
     this by destroying enemy armored vehicles and concentrated forces. They may use techniques such as shift

4-24       ATP 3-21.91  11 May 2017


---

                                                                                                                                     Defense

fires, reposition forces, and maneuver the reserve or MBA forces to gain local fire superiority at critical
locations. Obstacles, security forces, surveillance assets, and fires can assist covering areas where risk is
accepted. Massing forces and fires is done swiftly because opportunities to gain an advantage are usually
brief (see figure 4-4, page 4-26 and figure 4-5, page 4-27).

Penetrations

     4-136. Each maneuver commander is responsible for controlling enemy advances within an assigned area
     of operation. These commanders provide the SBCT task force commander early warning and reaction time

11 May 2017  ATP 3-21.91  4-27


---

Chapter 4

     for potential enemy penetrations. If a maneuver unit is threatened with a penetration, the SBCT task force
     commander may take several actions to counter the situation. In order of priority, the commander can—

                z Maintain contact with the penetrating enemy force. Forward MBA forces may be able to
                     transition into a delay to maintain contact, or the commander may redirect reconnaissance
                     assets, security forces, and observers to locate enemy. The commander seeks to determine the
                     enemy force’s size, composition, direction of attack, and rate of movement. Forces in contact
                     adjust indirect fires and air ground operations against the enemy to disrupt, delay, or divert their
                     attack.

                z Take immediate actions to reinforce at the point of penetration. This may require changing task
                     organization, adjusting adjacent unit boundaries and tasks, emplacing obstacles, committing the
                     reserve, or shifting priority of fires.

                z Move threatened sustainment units. Based upon the enemy’s direction of attack, sustainment
                     units may need to move away from the penetration. These movements are controlled to ensure
                     they do not interfere with counterattack plans or movement of combat forces.

                z Determine where and how to engage the penetrating enemy force. Based upon the enemy’s size,
                     composition, and direction of attack, the commander selects the best location to engage the
                     enemy. The reserve may counterattack into the enemy’s flank, or it may establish a defensive
                     position in depth to defeat or block them. The staff establishes control measures for the reserve’s
                     attack. The reserve can use an engagement area or objective to orient itself to a specific location
                     to engage the enemy, or can use a BP as a position along defensible terrain. When the situation
                     is vague or the enemy has multiple avenues of approach, the commander may establish an AO
                     for the reserve. This requires the reserve to locate and move to intercept and engage the enemy,
                     anywhere in the assigned AO. The commander and staff develop a concept of fires and consider
                     required adjustments to fire support coordinating measures. They decide on the commitment of
                     directed, reserve, or situational obstacles to support the action. Traffic control is especially
                     critical. Sufficient routes are designated for the reserve force to use, and military police conduct
                     traffic regulation and enforcement to ensure those routes remain clear of civilian traffic,
                     dislocated civilians, and stragglers.

                z Issue fragmentary orders that reflect adjustment decisions necessary to modify the original plan
                     to account for the actual situation. If the operation is not well controlled, the situation could
                     easily deteriorate into a total force failure.

COUNTERATTACK

     4-137. The SBCT task force conducts counterattacks to take advantage of an attacking enemy’s weakened
     condition by striking against the flanks or rear, or denying the enemy commander momentum and initiative.
     As the enemy’s advance slows, weakens, or overextends, there are fewer maneuver options. As a result, the
     enemy could transition to a hasty defense along the forward line of own troops, or attempt to gain a foothold
     within the MBA from which to defend. The SBCT weapons troop or any of its elements may take part in a
     counter attack as part of Cavalry or Infantry company teams and troops or task force size elements.

     4-138. This situation enables the SBCT weapons troop to seize the initiative from the enemy by leading the
     counter attack to seek decisive opportunities. Timing and intelligence is critical to a counterattack. Managing
     the rate of march of the counterattack force while out of contact against the enemy is critical. If committed
     too soon, the counterattack force might not have the desired effect, or may not be available for a more
     dangerous contingency. If committed too late, they might be ineffective. Once committed, counterattack
     forces can penetrate the enemy’s flanks, attack the enemy’s artillery and logistics areas, or attack from the
     rear. All of these are potentially decisive, creating concern for the enemy.

FOLLOW THROUGH

     4-139. The purpose of defensive tasks is to retain terrain and create conditions for a counteroffensive that
     regains the initiative. The area defense does this by causing the enemy to sustain unacceptable losses short
     of their objectives. A successful area defense allows the commander to transition to an attack. An area defense
     could result in a stalemate with both forces left in contact with each other.

4-28  ATP 3-21.91  11 May 2017


---

                          Defense

     4-140. It could result in the defender being overcome by the enemy attack and needing to transition to a
     retrograde operation. Any decision to withdraw takes into account the current situation in adjacent defensive
     areas. Only the commander who ordered the defense can designate a new FEBA or authorize a retrograde
     operation.

  SECTION III – MOBILE DEFENSE AND RETROGRADE

MOBILE DEFENSE

     4-141. The SBCT weapons troop participates in a mobile defense as either part of a fixing force or striking
     force, but not both. As part of the fixing force, the SBCT weapons troop defends within its assigned area of
     operation. It can use numerous offensive and defensive tasks, such as movement to contact, raid, spoiling
     attack, and area defense to fix the enemy.

             Note. Units smaller than a division do not usually conduct a mobile defense because of their
             inability to fight multiple engagements throughout the width, depth, and height of their AO, while
             simultaneously resourcing the striking, fixing, and reserve forces. Typically, the striking force in
             a mobile defense comprises one-half to two-thirds of the defender’s combat power.

     4-142. As part of the striking force, the SBCT weapons troop plans, rehearses, and executes offensive tasks.
     The striking force is a committed force and has the resources to conduct a decisive counterattack as a part of
     the mobile defense. The striking force decisively engages the enemy as they become exposed in attempts to
     overcome the fixing force. The striking force normally attacks a moving enemy force, and is usually
     augmented with armored forces.

RETROGRADE

     4-143. The SBCT weapons troop supports delays and withdrawals by providing the firepower with their
     weapon systems at maximum ranges to create distance between the SBCT and the enemy unit. Through a
     series of limited engagements, constantly bounding towards the friendly direction of travel the weapons troop
     maintains contact with the enemy force but creates distance. They are most effective when inflicting some
     casualties on a unit but not destroying outright. This forces the engaged unit to evacuate their casualties
     slowing their advance or pursue with a lesser force.

     4-144. The SBCT weapons troop participates in retirement as part of a larger force and normally
     consolidated under the Cavalry squadron. This preserves unit integrity and consolidates for logistical
     purposes allowing the unit to conduct a tactical road march to the retirement until it is ready for follow on
     operations.

DELAY

     4-145. The SBCT weapons troop will support a delay as part of another force by participating in a Guard
     with the SBCT Cavalry squadron or task force size unit. The same plan prepare and execute considerations
     apply (see chapter 2 of this publication for more information). The key difference is the intent of the unit that
     the delay is for. In the defense the weapons troop can expect to transition to either the main defense are or
     remain with the Cavalry Squadron after to displace after the guard area collapses. In a delay the weapons
     troop may break contact and with draw, reposition and support a counter attack, or support a passage of lines
     and battle handover.

WITHDRAWAL

     4-146. The SBCT weapons troop will participate in an unassisted withdrawal with part of a larger force.
     Their capability makes them the most viable option for a DLIC. The weapons troop supports an unassisted
     withdrawal by conducting a series of delays with limited engagements. During a withdrawal the commander
     of the DLIC will either choose to follow the main body of the withdrawing unit and break contact with the
     enemy unit after it ceases its pursuit or it may divert the enemy attack drawing the enemy pursuit to follow
     the DLIC instead of the main body. The conditions for this option are when the main body has enough

11 May 2017  ATP 3-21.91  4-29


---

Chapter 4

     distance from the DLIC and the enemy reconnaissance is unable to locate the main body. Often a linear
     obstacle can be used to separate the DLIC from the pursuing enemy and the DLIC can linkup with the main
     body at a predetermined point.

  SECTION IV – DEFENSE OF A LINEAR OBSTACLE

     4-147. Linear obstacles such as mountain ranges or river lines favor a forward defense rather than a defense
     in depth. In a forward defense, the linear obstacle may force the enemy forces to slow down, stop, mass, or
     canalize during their attempt to cross the linear obstacle providing an opportunity for the defending force to
     strike the enemy force.

PLAN

     4-148. Planning for a defense of a linear obstacle requires a thorough IPB analysis during the planning
     phase. Anticipation of how the enemy will react when it encounters the linear obstacle requires detailed
     analysis and understanding. Utilization of IPB information is vital to defensive planning. Since IPB integrates
     information analysis, information from reconnaissance and security tasks, and evaluating mission variables
     in order to determine the most likely enemy COA. Identifying the enemy COA allows the friendly unit to
     complete an effective defense of a linear obstacle and mass effective fires on the enemy force.

     4-149. A defensive plan should be more flexible when there is less information available on the enemy
     COA. Techniques for SBCT commanders are as follows:

                z Attach an MGS or ATGM vehicle to Infantry platoons to allow them greater fire power.
                z Keep an MGS platoon(s) as a reserve or strike force to deploy to the decisive point to engage

                     the enemy.
                z Assign an MGS or ATGM platoon(s) an area that allows them the ability to engage with longer

                     ranges of their weapon systems.
                z Emplacing obstacles in front of the bridgehead of the linear obstacle to block enemy. Normally,

                     attached engineers will conduct this task with a scatterable mine field.
                z Emplacing obstacles behind a bridgehead to separate or disrupt enemy forces. Normally,

                     supported field artillery will conduct this task with a family of scatterable mines (FASCAM).
                z Attaching a sniper team to an Infantry squad in an OP forward of the main defensive area to

                     provide early warning.
                z Supporting or having a scout platoon operating forward of the defensive position.

PREPARE

     4-150. Preparations for a linear obstacle are the same as discussed in an area defense task.

EXECUTE

     4-151. Execution begins with early warning or contact with the enemy force. Warning can come from
     sensors, enemy contact with adjacent or higher units, or your own forces in security areas. When this occurs
     the Soldiers occupy their positions and increase their alert status.

GAIN AND MAINTAIN ENEMY CONTACT

     4-152. Warning or contact allows the defending troop to know where at least a portion of the attacking
     enemy is currently positioned. When the warning of enemy is received it needs to immediately disseminate
     and the defense needs to begin. Organic sensors from within the weapons troop if assigned or directly
     supporting should orient on the direction of travel from the enemy detection according to the most likely
     COA.

     4-153. Actions on contact develop actionable information that the troop reacts to. The weapons troop should
     phase their forms of contact using sensors in depth first, to units using direct fire contact to provide the most
     reaction time and to gather information on the likely enemy COA. UAS are the best sensors for the weapons
     troop to employ when defending a linear obstacle. They can detect the enemy beyond the obstacle without

4-30  ATP 3-21.91  11 May 2017


---

                                                                                                                                           Defense

     commitment of a friendly unit. Ground sensors are also effective in front of the linear obstacle if the
     commander develops a plan to recover or assumes risk that they may be compromised.

     4-154. Forces forward of the linear obstacle that gain contact with the enemy should have a rehearsed and
     prepared plan to cross the linear obstacle before the enemy can engage or fix them. Alternatively, they should
     have a plan to displace or hide if they are unable to cross the linear obstacle.

DISRUPT THE ENEMY

     4-155. Key to the defense of a linear obstacle is the identification of the how the enemy intends to negotiate
     the obstacle. Leadership adjustments the defensive scheme of maneuver as the enemy approaches. Engineer
     assets are high value targets for the weapons troop. They severely disrupt the enemy’s operation when that
     are destroyed regardless of the enemy COA such as cross the obstacle, bypass the obstacle, or destroy the
     obstacle.

Execution of Planned Fires

     4-156. Effective fires, mass of combat forces and engagement/disengagement must be executed. The troop
     should try to force the enemy into its engagement areas, reinforce fires, add depth, block penetrations, restore
     positions, and reposition forces as needed. The fires over the linear obstacle must also be sustained throughout
     the engagement to prevent enemy control over or isolating areas of the obstacle.

Execution of Situational Obstacles

     4-157. As with an area defense situational obstacles must be tied to decision points, planned for and
     rehearsed prior to execution. Scatterable mines make effective situational obstacles but have their limitations.
     The intent of the obstacles can include closing gaps or repair existing obstacles, reinforce existing obstacles
     protect the flanks, and isolate an enemy force. (See ATP 3-90.8 for more information on situational
     obstacles.)

FIX THE ENEMY

     4-158. The weapons troop should fix the enemy with fires when it consolidates to negotiate the obstacle.

Maneuver

     4-159. Similar to how the U.S. army crosses and breaches obstacles the enemy will apply SOSRA
     techniques. The weapons troop is effective at preventing the enemy from suppressing the friendly force by
     engaging from multiple locations and mixing ATGM and MGS fires from various distances. When
     augmented with Infantry and Cavalry and fighting as a combined arms team the force prevents the enemy
     conducting all the elements of SOSRA.

     4-160. Should the enemy force cross the linear obstacle a counterattack or an assault is best employed at
     that moment to secure the crossing point? The enemy is most vulnerable at that point because they have
     piecemeal forces and are canalized. Destroying an enemy unit and its vehicles can block the crossing point.

COUNTERATTACK

     4-161. The counter attack in a linear obstacle is best conducted by another unit that is on the far side of the
     linear obstacle. This unit is not limited to the movement restrictions that the linear obstacle creates. Counter
     attacking over the obstacle the enemy just encountered provides the enemy with the same advantages the
     defending force just had. The unit must use the principals of SOSRA and cross the linear obstacle in the same
     manner as a gap crossing or breaching an obstacle. (See chapter 6 of this publication for more information.)

ASSESS

     4-162. Assessment is continuous throughout the completion of prepare and execution phases. The SBCT
     weapons troop commander’s assessment reveals threats to the chosen defensive scheme, suggests
     improvements to effectiveness, and reveals opportunities. The troop commander determines the differences
     (variances) between the actual situation and what the original defensive scheme forecasted the situation
     would be at any given time based on the commander’s own observations and reports from the troop’s
     subordinate organic units and any attachments. The commander’s evaluation of these variances considers

11 May 2017  ATP 3-21.91  4-31


---

Chapter 4

     whether the desired conditions have changed, are no longer achievable, or are not achievable if the troop
     continues to execute the original defensive scheme. The commander makes adjustments to that defensive
     scheme and issues the necessary oral orders (see figure 4-6). These adjustments may have been previously
     developed as part of a previously developed decision support template. They include assigning new tasks to
     subordinates, reprioritizing support, and significantly modifying the chosen defensive course of action.

 Legend: EA = engagement area, FPF = final protective fire, MGS = mobile gun system, NAI = named
 area of interest

                                     Figure 4-6. Defense of a linear obstacle

SECTION V – PERIMETER DEFENSE

  4-163. A perimeter defense is a defense oriented in all directions. The SBCT weapons troop uses it to secure
  itself, and to protect other units located within the perimeter. The SBCT weapons troop can employ a
  perimeter defense in most terrain. The commander conducting a perimeter defense plans to respond to the
  widest possible range of enemy actions when positioning forces, weapons emplacement, direct and indirect
  fire integration, and reserve employment. The SBCT weapons troop may be called upon to execute the
  perimeter defense under a variety of conditions, including—

             z When securing itself against attacks in an urban area.

4-32  ATP 3-21.91  11 May 2017


---

                                     Defense

             z When holding critical terrain in areas where the defense is not tied in with adjacent units.
             z When it has been bypassed and isolated by the enemy and must defend in place.
             z When occupying an independent assembly area or reserve position.
             z When beginning preparation of a strong point.
             z When directed to concentrate fires on two or more adjacent avenues of approach.

4-164.  The commander should consider the following while in a perimeter defense:
        z Placing security as far out as possible, maximizing the use of optics and sensors to provide early

             warning.
        z Positioning ATGM and MGS vehicles in protected positions, and concentrating their fires on

             armor avenues of approach and related EAs.
        z Retention of key terrain.
        z Maintaining a reserve. Normally an MGS section.
        z Location of the reserve and criteria to deploy them.
        z Mission command.
        z Sustainment operations and sustainment security.

PLANNING AND PREPARING

4-165. Planning for a perimeter defense can be deliberate or a battle drill, executed on command. Estimated
duration of the perimeter defense will determine the level of planning and coordination involved. The longer
the duration the more built up the perimeter should be. An example of a long term perimeter defense is a
combat outpost that requires barriers, structures, additional communication assets, and engineer support.
Hasty perimeter defense can be established during security halts by forming a perimeter with Stryker
vehicles. Preparing a perimeter defense is a continuous task that is conducted with the weapons troop
commander establishing priorities of work. Perimeter defenses are prepared in this order:

          z Reconnaissance of the area to defend.
          z Occupation of the area.

          z Establish security.
          z Begin priorities of work.

EXECUTING

4-166. The troop commander organizes his forces according to the perimeter defense plan (see figure 4-7,
page 4-34). He places his most casualty producing weapon system first at the most likely enemy avenue of
approach and tries the maximum effective range. The remainder of the Stryker vehicles form a perimeter in
their assigned areas. Once the perimeter is formed and the commander establishes his priorities of work, he
can begin to send out security patrols.

ASSESSING

4-167. Adjustments in the perimeter are constant to ensure the security. Leaders will continuously check,
inspect and update the perimeter defense plan. All adjustments are communicated to the commander. He is
responsible for the coordination of the perimeter.

SECTION VI – REVERSE-SLOPE DEFENSE

  4-168. An alternative to defending on the forward slope of a hill or a ridge is to defend on a reverse slope.
  In such a defense, the SBCT weapons troop is deployed on terrain that is masked from enemy direct fire and
  ground observation by the crest of a hill. Although some units and weapons might be positioned on the
  forward slope, the crest, or the counterslope (a forward slope of a hill to the rear of a reverse slope), most
  forces are on the reverse slope. The key to this defense is control of the crest by direct fire.

  4-169. The commander can adopt a reverse slope position when—
             z Enemy fire makes the forward slope untenable.

4-34                          ATP 3-21.91                    11 May 2017


---

                                     Defense

          z The weapons systems on the ATGM and MGS cannot depress enough to engage enemy.
          z Lack of cover and concealment on the forward slope makes it untenable.
          z The forward slope has been lost or has not yet been gained.
          z The forward slope is exposed to enemy direct fire weapons fired from beyond the effective

                range of the defender’s weapons. Moving to the reverse slope removes the attacker’s standoff
                advantage.
          z The terrain on the reverse slope provides better fields of fire than the forward slope.
          z The defender avoids creating a dangerous salient or reentrant in friendly lines.
          z Surprising and deceiving the enemy as to the true location of the defensive positions is essential.

4-170. When executing a reverse slope defense, the SBCT weapons troop commander places special
emphasis on—

          z A fire support plan to prevent the enemy’s occupation and use of the crest of the hill.
          z The coordination with reconnaissance elements on the forward slope to provide observation

                across the entire front and security to the main battle positions.
          z A counterattack plan that specifies measures necessary to clear the crest or regain it from the

                enemy.
          z Fire support to destroy, disrupt, and attrite enemy forces on the forward slope.

4-171. The forward edge of the position should be within small arms range of the crest. It should be far
enough from the crest that fields of fire allow the defender time to place well-aimed fire on the enemy before
he reaches friendly positions. The troop can deploy the FSV or establish FOs on or forward of the
topographical crest. This allows long-range precision fires, observation, and early warning over the entire
front and indirect fire coverage of forward obstacles. OPs are usually provided by the unit that owns the
terrain being observed, and may vary in size from a few Soldiers to a reinforced squad.

SPECIAL CONSIDERATIONS

4-172.  The following are considerations that commanders may apply when defending on a reverse slope:

        z Observation of the enemy is more difficult. Soldiers in this position see forward no farther than
             the crest. This makes it hard to determine exactly where the enemy is as he advances, especially
             when visibility is poor. OPs are placed forward of the topographic crest for early warning and
             long-range observation.

        z Displacing from the reverse slope defense is difficult because the defender must move up a hill.
             The defender is decisively engaged in a reverse slope defense.

        z Fields of fire are usually short negating weapon range advantages. Normally, the defender has
             the opportunity to engage with direct fire weapon systems first.

        z Obstacles on the forward slope can be covered only with indirect fire or by units on the flanks
             of the troop unless some weapons systems are initially placed forward.

        z If the enemy gains the crest, he can assault downhill. This may give him a psychological
             advantage.

        z If OPs are insufficient or improperly placed, the defenders might have to fight an enemy who
             suddenly appears in strength at close range.

        z The slope degrades the enemy from being able to conduct reconnaissance and retreat if
             committed to an attack. The MGS vehicles can engage with direct fire with their main gun from
             the base of the slope providing them maximum cover and most likelihood to engage the enemy
             first. They should be placed in positions that angle the vehicle slightly upwards towards the hill.
             The position should not expose the top of the vehicle towards the enemy where the armor is
             more vulnerable to enemy fires. The ATGM vehicle is best placed where is can get flank shots
             or it can engage in slope to slope. Slope to slope engagements are negating the overhead fires
             restrictions of their surface danger zone (SDZ) with the use of Aero TOW missiles. (See figure
             4-8, page 4-36.)

11 May 2017             ATP 3-21.91  4-35


---

Chapter 4

    Legend : EA = engagement area

                                      Figure 4-8. Reverse slope considerations

PLAN

     4-173. Planning for a reverse slope defense will require thorough IPB analysis during the planning phase.
     Anticipation of how the enemy will react when it encounters the defense requires detailed analysis and
     understanding. Making a defensive plan will require determining where the enemy descends and can be
     identified with a thorough information collection plan. By identifying where the enemy descends, allows the
     troop commander time to mass effective fires on the enemy force.

     4-174. The commander must also anticipate how to commit the enemy force to come over the hill. Often
     the SBCT weapons troop can engage in from the top of elevation from a primary position and transition to a
     reverse slope as a secondary position. This technique lures the enemy into the reverse slope engagement as
     the attempt to pursue the SBCT forces.

PREPARE

     4-175. Preparing for the reverse slope defense begins with the assembling of the troop task organization
     and the completion of TLPs. Strong emphasis should be placed on concealing defensive positions, channeling
     the enemy into EAs, and blocking attempts to occupy dead space by emplacing obstacles and covering with
     indirect fires. Mobility enhancement should concentrate on improving routes that lead out of low ground
     along covered and concealed routes towards the rear of the defensive position up the hill or lateral routes to
     escape an enemy downhill assault.

EXECUTE

     4-176. Execution begins with warning or contact with the enemy force. Warning can come from the
     adjacent, higher, or subordinate units. Ideally direct fire contact should be initiated by friendly forces, due to
     the close proximity of the enemy forces in a reverse slope defense (see figure 4-7, page 4-34). The commander
     should quickly assess his defensive plan if an enemy force initiates contact.

GAIN AND MAINTAIN ENEMY CONTACT

     4-177. Contact with the enemy force is preferably established as far out as possible to provide early warning
     through sensors, the security forces, preferably in the security area. As the enemy approaches the defensive
     position their location and disposition are communicated to the SBCT weapons troop. At some point an
     element of the SBCT weapons troop makes contact with the enemy force somewhere forward of the reverse
     slope and it must be maintained until the culmination of the engagement.

4-36       ATP 3-21.91  11 May 2017


---

                                                                                                                                           Defense

DISRUPT THE ENEMY

     4-178. Disruption naturally occurs as the enemy force crests the elevation. The SBCT weapons troop must
     be flexible with his disrupting efforts. Too much disruption on the enemy force before they crest the elevation
     prevents from their committal to their attack and into their engagement area. Too little disruption and the
     enemy force is down the slope and decisively engaged in close combat. The SBCT weapons troop should
     build disruption obstacles within the engagement area first in their priority and then forward. They should
     place their forces to disrupt forward and then have them bound to the rear to assume positions in the reverse
     slope defense. This technique allows flexibility in the disruption plan.

FIX THE ENEMY

     4-179. The enemy must be fixed on the reverse slope within the engagement area. Blocking obstacles at the
     base of the slope and sides assist to fix the enemy. Fires from the MGS and ATGM should be synchronized
     and suppress the enemy force to prevent them from returning accurate fire and continuing their movement.
     Indirect fires and FASCAM placed on the crest or forward slope further prevent the enemy force from
     withdrawing once their main body has entered then engagement area.

MAIN BATTLE AREA ENGAGEMENT

     4-180. Once the enemy main body has crested the elevation and is in the MBA the SBCT weapons troop
     must stop their advance short of their defensive positions. The enemy will have the advantage of attacking
     downhill giving them momentum and speed. Blocking obstacles, direct fires, and indirect fires should be
     used to stop the enemy advance. ATGM and MGS vehicles have the advantage of multiple round selection
     against enemy targets. In the reverse slope engaging enemy with intended ammunition to maximize effects
     provides the best outcome in the reverse slope. (See chapter 9 of this publication for more information) (see
     figure 4-9, page 4-38).

11 May 2017  ATP 3-21.91  4-37


---
