Weapons Control Status

     9-54. The three levels of WCS outline the conditions, based on target identification criteria, under which
     friendly elements can engage. The commander sets and adjusts the WCS based on friendly and enemy
     disposition, and the clarity of the situation. In general, the higher the probability of fratricide and friendly
     fire, the more restrictive the WCS. The three levels, in descending order of restrictiveness, are as follows:

             z WEAPONS HOLD. Engage only if engaged or ordered to engage.
             z WEAPONS TIGHT. Engage only targets that are positively identified as enemy.
             z WEAPONS FREE. Engage any targets that are not positively identified as friendly.

     9-55. As an example, the commander may establish the weapons control status as WEAPONS HOLD when
     friendly forces are conducting a passage of lines. By maintaining situational understanding of his own
     elements and adjacent friendly forces, however, he may lower the WCS. In such a case, the commander may
     set a WEAPONS FREE status when he knows there are no friendly elements near the engagement. This
     permits his elements to engage targets at extended ranges even though it is difficult to distinguish targets
     accurately at ranges beyond 2000 meters under combat conditions. Another consideration is that the WCS is
     extremely important for forces using combat identification systems. Establishing the WCS as WEAPONS
     FREE permits leaders to engage an unknown target when they fail to get a friendly response.

11 May 2017        ATP 3-21.91                                                     9-15


---

Chapter 9

Engagement Priorities

     9-56. Engagement priorities provide platoon and company/team level direct/indirect fire distribution
     examples that combine engagement priorities (targeting by composition) and fire patterns/target arrays
     (targeting by disposition). This can serve one or more of the following critical fire control functions:

     9-57. Prioritize high payoff targets. In concert with his concept of the operation, the commander determines
     which target types provide the greatest payoff. He can then set these as a unit engagement priority. (For
     example, he may decide that destroying enemy engineer assets is the best way to prevent the enemy from
     breaching an obstacle.)

     9-58. Employ the best weapons for the target. Establishing engagement priorities for specific friendly
     systems increases the effectiveness with which the unit employs its weapons. As an example, the engagement
     priority for the ATGM could be enemy tanks first using TOW 2A (Aero) missiles, then enemy armored
     personnel carriers using the HEAT round from the 105mm main on the MGS or TOW 2B missiles. This
     provides the least amount of exposure time to the threat of the enemy fire.

     9-59. Distribute the unit’s fires. Establishing different priorities for similar friendly systems helps to prevent
     overkill and achieve effective distribution of fires. (For example, the commander may designate the enemy’s
     tanks as the initial priority for the ATGM platoon, while making the enemy’s Infantry and personnel carriers
     the priority for MGS platoon.) This would decrease the chances of units engaging against the same enemy
     targets, while ignoring the dangers posed by the other enemy vehicles.

Trigger

     9-60. A trigger is a specific set of conditions that dictates initiation of fires. Often referred to as engagement
     criteria, a trigger specifies the circumstances in which subordinate elements should engage. The
     circumstances can be based on a friendly or enemy event. (For example, the trigger for a friendly platoon to
     initiate engagement could be three or more enemy combat vehicles passing or crossing a given point or line.)
     This line can be any natural or man-made linear feature (such as a road, ridge line, or stream). It may be a
     line perpendicular to the unit’s orientation, delineated by one or more reference points.

Engagement Techniques

     9-61. Engagement techniques are effects-oriented fire distribution measures. The following engagement
     techniques, the most common in SBCT weapons troop operations, are covered in this discussion:

             z Point fire.
             z Area fire.
             z Simultaneous.
             z Alternating fire.
             z Observed fire.
             z Sequential fire.
             z Time of suppression.
             z Reconnaissance by fire.

Point Fire

     9-62. Point fire entails concentrating the effects of a unit’s fire against a specific, identified target such as a
     vehicle, machine gun bunker, or ATGM position. When leaders direct point fire, all of the unit’s weapons
     engage the target, firing until it is destroyed or the required time of suppression has expired. Employing
     converging fires from dispersed positions makes point fire more effective because the target is engaged from
     multiple directions. The unit may initiate an engagement using point fire against the most dangerous threat,
     and then revert to area fire against other, less threatening point targets.

             Note. Use of point fire is fairly rare because a unit seldom encounters a single, clearly identified
             enemy weapon.

9-16  ATP 3-21.91  11 May 2017


---

                                                                                                         Direct Fire Planning and Control

Area Fire

     9-63. Area fire involves distributing the effects of a unit’s fire over an area in which enemy positions are
     numerous or are not obvious. If the area is large, leaders assign sectors of fire to subordinate elements using
     a terrain-based distribution method such as the quadrant technique. Typically, the primary purpose of the
     area fire is suppression; however, sustaining effective suppression requires judicious control of the rate of
     fire.

Simultaneous Fire

     9-64. Units employ simultaneous fire to rapidly mass the effects of their fires or to gain fire superiority. (For
     example, a unit may initiate an SBF with simultaneous fire, and then revert to alternating or sequential fire
     to maintain suppression.) Simultaneous fire is employed to negate the low probability of the hit and kill of
     certain antiarmor weapons. The ATGM and MGS platoon may fire all of its vehicles at once on an enemy
     column to reduce the enemy’s ability to return fire on a single vehicle position.

Alternating Fire

     9-65. In alternating fire, pairs of elements continuously engage the same point or area target one at a time.
     Alternating fire permits the unit to maintain suppression for a longer duration than does volley fire; it also
     forces the enemy to acquire and engage alternating points of fire. For example, a MGS platoon may alternate
     fires of two sections to maintain suppression on a target while an infantry squad moves to its assault position.

Observed Fire

     9-66. Observed fire is usually used when the SBCT weapons troop is in protected defensive positions with
     engagement ranges in excess of 2500 meters. The commander or platoon leader directs one element or section
     to engage. The remaining elements or sections observe fires and prepare to engage on order in case the
     engaging element consistently misses its targets, experiences a malfunction, or runs low on ammunition.
     Observed fire allows for mutual observation and assistance while protecting the location of the observing
     elements.

Sequential Fire

     9-67. Sequential fire entails the subordinate elements of a unit engaging the same point or area target one
     after another in an arranged sequence. Sequential fire can help to prevent the waste of ammunition. Sequential
     fire permits elements that have already fired to pass on intelligence they have learned from the engagement.
     An example would be an MGS who missed a flank shot on enemy armored fighting vehicle, communicates
     its position to an ATGM that fires a TOW IIB missile.

Time of Suppression

     9-68. Time of suppression is the period, specified by the commander, during which an enemy position or
     force must be suppressed. Suppression time is typically dependent on the time it will take a supported element
     to maneuver. Usually, a unit suppresses an enemy position using the sustained rate of fire of its automatic
     weapons. In planning for sustained suppression, leaders needs to consider several factors: the estimated time
     of suppression, the size of the area being suppressed, the type of enemy force to be suppressed, range to the
     target, rates of fire, and available ammunition quantities.

Reconnaissance by Fire

     9-69. Reconnaissance by fire is the process of engaging possible enemy locations to elicit a tactical response
     (such as return fire or movement). This response permits the commander and subordinate leaders to acquire
     the target and then to mass fires against the enemy element. Typically, the commander directs a subordinate
     element to conduct the reconnaissance by fire. For example, he may direct an overwatching MGS platoon to
     conduct the reconnaissance by fire against a probable enemy position hidden in dense vegetation authorizing
     use of canister rounds, COAX, and fires from browning .50 cal.

Fire Patterns

     9-70. Fire patterns are a threat-based measure designed to distribute the fires of a unit simultaneously
     between multiple, similar targets. Platoons use those most often to distribute fires across an enemy formation.

11 May 2017  ATP 3-21.91  9-17


---

Chapter 9

     Leaders designate and adjust fire patterns based on terrain and the anticipated enemy formation. The basic
     fire patterns, illustrated in figure 9-8, page 9-18, are—

             z Frontal.
             z Cross.
             z Depth.

Frontal Fire

     9-71. Leaders may initiate frontal fire when targets are arrayed in front of the unit in a lateral configuration.
     Weapons systems engage targets to their respective fronts. For example, the left flank weapon engages the
     left-most target; the right flank weapon engages the right-most target. As weapons systems destroy targets,
     weapons shift fires toward the center of the enemy formation from near too far.

Cross Fire

     9-72. Leaders initiate cross fire when targets are arrayed laterally across the unit’s front in a manner that
     permits diagonal fires at the enemy’s flank, or when obstructions prevent unit weapons from firing frontally.
     Right flank weapons engage the left-most targets; left flank weapons engage the right-most targets. Firing
     diagonally across an engagement area provides more flank shots, thus increasing the chance of kills. It
     reduces the possibility of the enemy detecting friendly elements should the enemy continue to move forward.
     As friendly elements destroy targets, weapons shift fires toward the center of the enemy formation.

             Note. ATGM do not use cross fire patterns due to wires on TOW missiles.

Depth Fire

     9-73. Leaders initiate depth fire when enemy targets disperse in depth, perpendicular to the unit. Center
     weapons engage the closest targets; flank weapons engage deeper targets. As the unit destroys targets,
     weapons shift fires toward the center of the enemy formation. Mixing ATGM and MGS fires provides the
     greatest opportunity for depth. (See figure 9-7.)

9-18  ATP 3-21.91  11 May 2017


---

                          Direct Fire Planning and Control

    Legend: MGS = mobile gun system

                                            Figure 9-7. Fire pattern examples

Target Array

     9-74. Target array enables the commander to distribute fires when the enemy force is concentrated and
     terrain-based controls are inadequate. Forces create this threat-based distribution measure by superimposing
     a quadrant pattern on the enemy formation. Soldiers center the pattern on the enemy formation, with the axes
     running parallel and perpendicular to the enemy’s direction of travel. The target array fire control measure is
     effective against an enemy with a well-structured organization and standardized doctrine. However, it may
     prove less effective against an enemy that presents few organized formations, or does not follow strict
     prescribed tactics. Leaders describe quadrants using the quadrants’ relative locations. The examples in
     figure 9-8, page 9-20, illustrate the target array technique.

11 May 2017  ATP 3-21.91  9-19


---

Chapter 9

    Legend: TRP = target reference point
                                            Figure 9-8. Target array examples

FIRE COMMANDS

     9-75. Fire commands are oral orders issued by leaders to direct and distribute fires when necessary to achieve
     decisive effects against the enemy. They allow leaders to rapidly and concisely articulate their firing
     instructions using a standard format. Unit fire commands (refer to ATTP 3-21.9 for more information on fire
     commands) include these elements, which are discussed below:

             z Alert.
             z Weapon or ammunition (optional).
             z Target description.
             z Orientation.
             z Range (optional).
             z Control (optional).
             z Execution.

ALERT

     9-76. The alert specifies the elements that are directed to fire. It does not require the leader initiating the
     command to identify him. Examples of the alert element (call signs and code words based on unit SOP)
     include the following:

             z HELLCATS – All subordinate elements.
             z RED – First platoon only.
             z THIRD – Third squad only.

WEAPON OR AMMUNITION (OPTIONAL)

     9-77. This element identifies the weapon and ammunition to be employed by the alerted elements. Leaders
     may designate the type and number of rounds to limit expenditure of ammunition. Examples of this element
     include the following:

9-20       ATP 3-21.91  11 May 2017


---

                          Direct Fire Planning and Control

             z HEP, HEAT, canister, SABOT
             z TOW 2A.
             z TOW 2B Aero.
             z TOW 2BB.
             z M240B. (ATGM)
             z COAX (MGS)

TARGET DESCRIPTION

     9-78. Target description designates which threat elements are to be engaged. Leaders may use the description
     to focus fires or achieve distribution. Examples of target description include the following:

             z Three tanks.
             z Two personnel carriers.
             z Troops
             z Bunker.
             z Wall, Window.

ORIENTATION (DIRECTION)

     9-79. This element identifies the location of the target. Examples of orientation include the following:
             z Closest TRP (such as TRP 3).
             z Clock direction (such as ONE O’CLOCK).
             z Terrain quadrant (such as QUADRANT ONE).
             z Friendly quadrant (such as LEFT FRONT).
             z Target array (such as FRONT HALF).
             z Laser pointer (such as ON MY POINTER).

RANGE (OPTIONAL)

     9-80. The range element identifies the distance to the target. Announcing range is not necessary for TOWs.

CONTROL

     9-81. The weapons troop commander may use this element to direct desired target effects, distribution
     methods, or engagement techniques. Subordinate leaders may include the control element to supplement the
     commander’s instructions and achieve effective distribution. Examples of control include the following:

             z Target array (such as FRONT HALF).
             z Fire pattern (such as FRONTAL, DEEP CROSS).
             z Terrain quadrant (such as QUADRANT ONE).
             z Engagement priorities (such as ENGAGE TANKS THEN ATGMS).
             z Engagement technique (such as VOLLEY, AREA).

     9-82. The three levels of weapons control status outline the conditions, based on target identification criteria,
     under which friendly elements may engage. The commander sets and adjusts the weapons control status
     based on friendly and threat disposition and the clarity of the situation. In general, the higher the probability
     of fratricide, the more restrictive the weapons control status. The three levels, in descending order of
     restrictiveness, are:

             z Weapons Hold. Engage only if engaged or ordered to engage.
             z Weapons Tight. Engage only targets that are positively identified as threat.
             z Weapons Free. Engage any targets that are not positively identified as friendly.

     9-83. As an example, the commander may establish the weapons control status as “Weapons Hold” when
     friendly forces are conducting a passage of lines. By maintaining situational understanding of his own
     elements and adjacent friendly forces however, he may be able to lower the weapons control status. In such
     a case, the commander may be able to set a “Weapons Free” status when he knows no friendly elements are
     near the engagement. This permits his elements to engage targets at extended ranges. A further consideration

11 May 2017  ATP 3-21.91  9-21


---

Chapter 9

     is the weapons control status is extremely important for forces using combat identification systems;
     establishing the weapons control status as “Weapons Free” permits leaders to engage an unknown target
     when they fail to get a friendly response.

EXECUTION

     9-84. The execution element specifies when fires will be initiated. The commander may wish to engage
     immediately, delay initiation, or delegate authority to engage. Examples of execution include the following:

             z FIRE.
             z AT MY COMMAND...FIRE.
             z AT YOUR COMMAND.
             z AT PHASE LINE BLUE.

     9-85. The following is an example of fire commands for an ATGM crew:
             z Vehicle commander, driver, or loader identifies target: TWO TANKS IN THE OPEN, CLOSER
                  TANK FIRST.
             z Gunner: IDENTIFIED, TRACKING (implies track gates are locked).
             z Loader: CLEAR (implies backblast area clear, hatch closed, and round loaded).
             z Gunner: READY (implies umbilical is inserted and trigger enabled).
             z Vehicle commander: FIRE (implies vehicle commander identified target and is prepared to send
                  report to higher headquarters).
             z Gunner: ON THE WAY (fires round one).
             z Vehicle commander: one of three possible responses (first two are to the crew and to higher
                  headquarters/adjacent crews, last one is crew internal).
             z TARGET DESTROYED.
             z OBSTRUCTION (dust or debris limiting bomb damage assessment and observation).

     9-86. FIRE ROUND TWO (implies target not destroyed or a miss).

9-22  ATP 3-21.91  11 May 2017

An antiarmor leader must understand the capabilities of his TOW system to employ it
appropriately. The current and future TOW missiles capabilities are provided in the
following paragraphs.

CURRENT TOW MISSILES

     A-1. The TOW missiles in the current inventory are the 2A, 2B, 2B Aero, and BB. The designation,

Appendix A

                        Table A-2. TOW target set

     Type    Primary           Secondary

     TOW 2A  Armor             Light armor and typical urban structures

     TOW 2B  Armor             Urban structures

     TOW 2B Aero Armor         Urban structures

     TOW BB  Urban structures  Light armored vehicles

     Legend: TOW = tube-launched, optically tracked, wire-guided (missile), BB = bunker buster

     A-5. The TOW 2A was developed to defeat enemy armor with explosive reactive armor. The missile has a
     tandem warhead to achieve increased lethality. The precursor warhead in the tip of its extended probe
     detonates the explosive reactive armor. The primary warhead then penetrates the base armor. The TOW 2A
     is a line-of-sight missile.

     A-6. The TOW 2B is a top attack missile that uses a fly-over shoot-down mode to destroy armored targets.
     This missile penetrates the more vulnerable top of an armored vehicle. This permits the attack of targets in
     defile or protected by berms. The TOW 2B has a dual sensor that senses the mass of iron and a laser measuring
     device which ranges down to the ground and identifies when the profile changes relative to known target
     profiles below the missile. In fly-over shoot-down mode, the gunner aims at the target. The missile flight
     path is 2.25 meters above the target. The gunner has the option to change to line-of-sight mode when engaging
     targets under cover or when engaging targets such as buildings or bunkers. The missile is more effective
     against buildings after being flown through a door or window. The TOW 2B is not effective as a breaching
     weapon.

     A-7. The TOW 2B Aero is a variant of the TOW 2B. The TOW 2B Aero has an aerodynamic nose and
     additional wire to provide the extended range for this missile.

     A-8. The TOW 2B and TOW 2B Aero have variants of missiles with counter active protection system
     capability. These variants were developed to defeat the active protection systems on target vehicles.

     A-9. When using the TOW 2B or 2B Aero, prevent fratricide by ensuring the missile flight path is at least
     ten meters above friendly vehicles to prevent detonation on friendly vehicles. When using the TOW 2B or
     2B Aero, prevent overkill by ensuring the missile flight path is at least 10 meters above dead vehicles to
     prevent detonation on dead vehicles.

     A-10. Do not fire any member of the TOW 2B family of missiles over friendly vehicles or personnel, or over
     vehicles that have been destroyed by friendly fire. This may cause the missile to detonate on friendly or dead
     vehicles.

     A-11. TOW limitations are listed below: (Refer to TC 3-22.32 and FM 3-22.34 for more information.)
             z Firing over water.
             z Firing over power lines.
             z Firing through area fires.
             z Firing in windy conditions.
             z Firing from buildings.

RADIO FREQUENCY MISSILES

     A-12. In 2010, the Army began accepting a new version of TOW missiles. These missiles use wireless radio
     frequency (RF) for guidance. Missiles with radio frequency are designated TOW RF. These missiles
     eliminate TOW wires in the AO, eliminate firing limitations over water and power lines and through fires,
     and adjust the synchronization of indirect fires.

     A-13. The TOW RF missiles will be TOW 2A RF, TOW 2B Aero RF, and TOW BB RF. The TOW 2B Aero
     RF variant was the first missile accepted.

A-4                     ATP 3-21.91                                                             11 May 2017


---

                                                                                                                   Weapons Reference Data

     A-14. No launcher modifications are required to fire a RF missile. Current launchers can fire TOW and TOW
     RF missiles. TOW RF missiles have the same maximum safe arm and maximum range as TOW missiles.

MGS WEAPONS CONSIDERATIONS

     A-15. Numerous factors related to vehicles and equipment affects the MGS platoon’s planning in the urban
     environment. These factors include the following aspects:

             z The preferred main gun rounds in urban operations are HEAT-T and high explosive plastic tracer.
                   These rounds outperform sabot rounds against bunkers and buildings.

             z HEAT-T ammunition arms approximately 60 feet from the gun muzzle. It loses most of its
                   effectiveness against urban targets at ranges of less than 25 to 30 meters.

             z High explosive plastic tracer is used primarily against troops (where blast concussion and
                   fragmentation are desired), field fortifications, bunkers, buildings, and crew-served weapon
                   emplacements.

             z The 1040 canister round, used in an antipersonnel role, enables the MGS to defeat a squad of
                   enemy infantry in the open. The canister round can also be used in an antimaterial role to defeat
                   technical trucks and surfaced laid obstacles such as concertina wire.

             z The M240 coaxial machine gun can effectively deliver suppressive fires against enemy personnel
                   and against enemy positions that are behind light cover. If the unit is equipped with the M240
                   dismount kit, troops can dismount the machine gun and use it in a ground role.

             z When buttoned up, the MGS crew has limited visibility to the sides and rear, and no visibility to
                   the top. Figure A-3, page A-6, illustrate the dead space associated with MGS operations in an
                   urban environment. When buttoned up, dismounted Infantry provide local security to the side,
                   top, and rear of the MGS.

             z An elevation of +15 degrees is also required to provide effective fires to support Infantry assaults
                   on high ground at ranges up to 1000 meters for machine guns, and 2000 meters for the main gun.
                   This capability is crucial when MGS platforms are unable to maneuver on designated Infantry
                   axis of attack, and must support Infantry at a distance.

             z Depression to -5 degrees is needed when MGS is used to mass fires in low ground EAs during
                   defensive operations.

     A-16. SABOT petals endanger accompanying Infantry elements. They create a hazard area extending
     70 meters on each side of the gun target line, out to a range of 1 kilometer.

                                                Appendix B

              TOW Employment in Restrictive Terrain

         A thorough METT-TC analysis is necessary to choose the best employment option for
         the TOW. Antiarmor leaders must know when and how to effectively employ the TOW
         in restrictive terrain.

RESTRICTIVE TERRAIN

     B-1. Restrictive terrain is defined as terrain that hinders movement to some degree. Units may have
     difficulty maintaining optimum speed or combat formation. Antiarmor leaders must recognize TOW system
     limitations and take advantage of the TOW system’s capabilities in restrictive terrain. The types of terrain
     described below are examples of the types of restrictive terrain in which friendly forces operate. Often the
     categories blend together (such as forests and steep hills).

FORESTS AND JUNGLES

     B-2. The degree and type of vegetation affects mounted movement, altering formations and speeds.
     Typically, armored vehicles will take advantage of roads or trails to move through these areas as quickly as
     possible. The ability to mass antiarmor fires on a slowed, contained enemy armored force may outweigh the
     degraded effectiveness of wire-guided missiles in this environment. (Refer to FM 90-5 for more information.)

EUROPEAN MODEL FORESTS

     B-3. These forests are well tended, free of underbrush or secondary growth, and often hilly. Unopposed
     armored forces can traverse these forests rapidly because of a large number of good trails. If armored vehicles
     are forced off the trails, the thickness and spacing of trees and the degree of slope determine how freely they
     can maneuver. The lack of undergrowth often allows visibility to several hundred meters.

CUT OVER OR PRIMEVAL FORESTS AND JUNGLES

     B-4. These forests have thick patches of vegetation and poor trail networks. Armored vehicles can travel
     through these forests if the ground is dry and not too steep, but such jungle busting is slow and visibility and
     engagement ranges are short, often measured in the tens of meters. TOW employment is seriously degraded
     unless fire lanes are cut through the underbrush.

HILLS AND MOUNTAIN RANGES

     B-5. The degree of slope and vegetation affect the mounted movement, altering formations and speeds. Do
     not drive an ATGM vehicle up a hill greater than 60 percent grade or on a side slope greater than 30 percent
     grade. (Refer to ATP 3-90.97 for more information.)

     B-6. Forested hills usually force armored vehicles to move on trails, which canalizes mounted movement.
     This restriction allows carefully positioned TOW systems to engage enemy vehicles unable to maneuver out
     of an EA. However, because tree growth and underbrush restrict visibility and engagement ranges, antiarmor
     leaders carefully place the TOW systems for a maximum range as possible.

     B-7. Bare hills and mountains, where steep terrain and lack of roads again canalize maneuver, often allow
     engagement out to maximum TOW ranges. However, if an antiarmor unit emplaces TOW systems in obvious
     positions, a mounted enemy can suppress them with direct and indirect fires. To avoid obvious positions, an
     antiarmor unit may emplace TOW systems in locations that sacrifice standoff. This increases their protection
     from the preplanned enemy suppressive direct and indirect fires and allows them to gain surprise. Enemy
     utilizing caves and overhangs to avoid indirect fire and attack aircraft may be engaged by TOWs firing up
     into those positions.

11 May 2017  ATP 3-21.91  B-1


---

Appendix B

SWAMPS AND WETLANDS

     B-8. Swamps stop mounted movement except on any hard surfaced, such as elevated roads that pass through
     them. Flood plains and moors can support lightly armored vehicles, but this type of terrain is often trafficable
     only during dry periods. In northern Europe, terrain broken by drainage ditches and dikes is common; this
     type of terrain prevents armored vehicles from moving off roads. Clear weather and good visibility provide
     the antiarmor unit with better observation. However, better visibility also aids the enemy in suppressing
     obvious TOW system firing positions.

URBAN AREAS AND VILLAGES

     B-9. The world is becoming increasingly urbanized, and antiarmor units will likely find themselves
     employed during urban operations. This environment obviously affects TOW system employment. (Refer to
     chapter 2 and 3 of this publication for more information.)

MANEUVER

     B-10. Infantry forces are normally employed on restrictive terrain that makes TOW employment more
     challenging. The TOW must be moved and positioned on terrain that supports the Infantry, but it can be
     positioned apart from Infantry positions. The SBCT weapons troop or ATGM platoon can be moved on a
     separate axis of advance with task-organized Infantry to provide security. Timing of the attack is critical.
     This is complicated because the main attack force usually moves from the attack position to the objective on
     foot while the antiarmor unit moves by vehicle to its supporting position.

PROTECTION

     B-11. In restrictive terrain, the antiarmor unit requires protection from close assault since it has limited self-
     defense capabilities. Only after conducting a detailed analysis of the mission variables of METT-TC does the
     commander or platoon leader decide how to move the SBCT weapons troop and its elements. Options
     include:

             z Attaching an ATGM or MGS platoon to an Infantry company.
             z Attaching an SBCT Infantry rifle platoon to the SBCT weapons troop.
             z Attaching an SBCT Infantry rifle squad to an ATGM or MGS platoon.

GUIDELINES FOR TOW EMPLOYMENT

     B-12. The basics of TOW employment outlined in chapters 1 through 5 of this publication, and direct fire
     planning and control described in chapter 6 of this publication, still apply when antiarmor units operate in
     restrictive terrain. However, commanders and leaders must look at these basics from a different perspective;
     certain principles may be more important than others, depending on the situation. Leaders can best employ
     the TOW missile in ambush scenarios where antiarmor units fire from unobtrusive positions to surprise the
     enemy and where TOW, Infantry fires, and indirect fires are closely coordinated. Finally, commanders must
     realize employing the TOW in restrictive terrain is less than ideal, and they should do so only if the results
     of a detailed METT-TC analysis dictate it. The seven principles of TOW employment have been modified
     for consideration in restrictive terrain.

MUTUAL SUPPORT

     B-13. Mutual support is vital when terrain inhibits the engagement range. Instead of massing the TOW
     missile fires from the SBCT weapons troop into an EA, leaders may have to mass fires with other Infantry
     weapons systems (MGS, M2, MK19, Javelin, and AT4) and indirect fires.

SECURITY

     B-14. Shorter engagement ranges and ample concealment make ATGM vehicles employing TOWs more
     vulnerable to enemy infantry. Therefore, the antiarmor unit should be positioned so Infantry can protect it. If
     the Infantry is not available, the ATGM must self-protect. The ATGM and MGS platoon and vehicles cannot
     sustain self-protection for an extended period or during limited visibility. Rotations of active crews
     conducting local security and rest plan in extending the amount of time.

B-2  ATP 3-21.91  11 May 2017


---

                                                                                              TOW Employment in Restrictive Terrain

FLANK SHOT ENGAGEMENT

     B-15. The feasibility of flank shots often determines whether TOW systems should be employed, especially
     in restrictive terrain. Flank shots provide the largest view of the target and come from an unexpected
     direction. Enemy armored vehicles are better protected on the frontal slope since crew orientation is primarily
     to the front, and it is difficult to traverse a turret in most restrictive terrain. An enemy tank gunner or
     commander will fire his main gun or coaxial machine gun as a reaction to the flash of a TOW missile launch.
     The TOW gunner could lose focus on, and control of, the missile after seeing the enemy return fire. Leaders
     should strive for flank shots in restrictive terrain.

     B-16. Simultaneous fire is used to rapidly mass the effects of fires or to gain immediate fires superiority over
     an enemy. It is also used to negate a low probability of hit given the terrain and expected engagement ranges.
     Other weapon systems may be employed to complement the TOW to gain immediate fire superiority.

STANDOFF

     B-17. Standoff in restrictive terrain may be difficult to obtain. The shorter engagement ranges in restrictive
     terrain reduce standoff. Shorter ranges also result in a shorter travel time from missile launch to impact as
     well as arming distance.

COVER AND CONCEALMENT

     B-18. Cover and concealment are especially important for TOW firing positions in restrictive terrain.
     Although antiarmor units try to fight from prepared positions, engineers may not be available to modify the
     terrain. Therefore, antiarmor units must often employ TOW missiles from hasty positions. In restrictive
     terrain, antiarmor units use the existing terrain for cover and concealment.

EMPLOYMENT IN DEPTH

     B-19. Restrictive terrain limits TOW employment in depth. Depth in restricted terrain is achieved by focusing
     on where or how to destroy the enemy, not where to locate the system. One method is to emplace TOW
     systems so they each can fire into an EA from different locations, rear and flanks, at the same time. Strict
     direct fire control measures must be implemented to reduce the risk of fratricide. Another method to achieve
     depth is to designate different weapons systems, such as the MGS, M2, MK19, Javelin, and AT4, and to
     engage different enemy targets throughout an EA or an AO.

EMPLOYMENT AS PART OF A COMBINED ARMS TEAM

     B-20. This is especially important in restrictive terrain. Assets such as TOW, MGS, M2, MK19, Javelin,
     AT4, mines and other obstacles, small arms or machine guns, M203 grenade launchers, and indirect fires
     must be skillfully blended to achieve the desired effect on the enemy. Antiarmor systems alone will not be
     sufficient. Leaders must position all assets so rounds impact on the appropriate portions of the enemy
     formation. Integration of indirect fires into massed direct ATGM fires enhances the effect of a unit’s
     engagements. Mortar and artillery fires can damage TOW wires, causing missiles to miss targets. Leaders
     must ensure adequate fire control measures are implemented to prevent indirect fires from falling between
     the antiarmor unit and the targets during TOW engagements.

11 May 2017  ATP 3-21.91  B-3

                                                Appendix C

                 Weapons Troop Fictional Scenario

         The following Appendix illustrates a fictional vignette as a discussion vehicle
         illustrating one of many ways the SBCT weapons troop participates in operations. This
         scenario will describe reconnaissance, security, offense, defense, and sustainment tasks
         as well as some enabling operations. The intent of this fictional scenario is to show
         application and describe challenges commanders and leaders within the SBCT will
         encounter. For simplicity and brevity, the vignette presented describes only select
         planning considerations, guidance, graphics, and warfighting functions that apply to
         each section. Actual mission analysis and planning require more detail than what is in
         the examples below.

  SECTION I – OVERVIEW OF THE SCENARIO

     C-1. In this vignette, the SBCT is augmented with enabling forces and support conducts operations against
     hybrid enemy forces to occupy the area of operations and establish the conditions to begin stability
     operations.

SITUATION

     C-2. The scenario occurs in Atropia. In the republic of Atropia, there are three major factions competing for
     control: the U.S.- and NATO-friendly elected civilian government, the military, and the resistance
     organization comprised of the ethnic- and religious-minority population that is under-represented in the
     elected government. The civilian government is generally corrupt, has been unable to provide adequate basic
     services to the general population, and has used the military and internal security apparatuses to retain control
     of the country, oppress its minority population, and stifle dissent.

     C-3. After repeated failures to pay the military’s salaries despite lavish spending on government officials
     during the same period, the military staged a coup to seize power. However, the military’s rule has proven to
     be as brutal and inefficient as the government it replaced. The military has imprisoned or executed members
     of the civilian government and political opponents of the military regime, and continued the policy of
     oppression of the country’s ethnic and religious minority populations. Refugees have begun to arrive in
     neighboring countries, threatening to destabilize and bankrupt their governments, and potentially widen the
     conflict into a regional war.

     C-4. Internally, an armed militia has arisen within the ethnic and religious minority in response to the sudden
     cessation of government-sponsored oppression. Their goal is to take advantage of the instability surrounding
     the military’s consolidation of power to advance their political agenda. As the military began its campaign
     of oppression and atrocities against the minority populations, the resistance movement has grown into an
     active, armed rebellion that is reportedly responsible for acts of terrorism, strikes against military regime
     forces, violent reprisals against majority populations, and attacks on foreign nationals. The resistance has
     also begun publicly broadcasting anti-U.S. and NATO rhetoric, threatening action against foreigners in their
     country, and pledging to attack targets in other countries if the international community takes any action
     opposing their cause.

     C-5. Fueled by media coverage of the civil war and the atrocities being committed in (country) and
     mounting political pressure to intervene to stabilize the region, the U.S. and NATO have decided to deploy
     ground forces to restore the legitimate elected government, secure any weapons of mass destruction, end the
     atrocities being committed against the civilian population, and ensure national and regional stability. The

11 May 2017  ATP 3-21.91  C-1


---

Appendix C

     scenario begins at the Aerial Port of Debarkation (APOD) in the neighboring Portas where the SBCT has
     arrived, assembled, and is planning and preparing for operations.

WEAPONS TROOP TASK ORGANIZATION

     C-1. Within this scenario, the weapons troop is assigned to the SBCT’s Cavalry squadron. (See figure C-1.)
     A medic team with an MEV, a fire support team with an FSV, and a contact team with wrecker have been
     attached to the weapons troop.

     Legend: BEB = brigade engineer battalion, CAV = cavalry, FA = field artillery, FLD = field,
     FWD = forward, IN = infantry, MI = military intelligence, MP = military police, PSYOP =, SPT
     = support, TACP = tactical air control party

                                      Figure C-1. SBCT task organization

C-2  ATP 3-21.91  11 May 2017


---

                                                                                                     Weapons Troop Fictional Scenario

ENEMY FORCES

     C-2. The enemy is a mix of elements comprised of military forces, paramilitary/local militia,
     irregular/insurgent, and criminal elements. These elements can act independently, collaborate for a common
     goal or objective for a limited amount of time or gain, and frequently engage each other as a part of their
     competition for political power and resources.

Regular Military Forces

     C-3. Atropia is a former trading partner of the Soviet Union. They acquired most of their military equipment
     from the former Soviet Union and continue to trade with Russia for weapons and war materiel. Most of their
     military is modeled after the Russian military and they use a derivative of Russian tactics. They are a mostly
     motorized force, but have some mechanized formations that include the T-72 main battle tank, the Boyevaya
     Mashina Pekhoty (BMP)-2 armored fighting vehicle, and the Bronetransporter (BTR)-80 wheeled infantry
     carrier vehicle. The Army has struggled to maintain their equipment and vehicles due to budget limitations,
     however, their equipment is still functional. Their Infantry soldiers are armed with AK-47s and Pulemyot
     Kalashnikova (PKMs), RPG-7s or RPG-14s, and 82mm mortars. They have some self-propelled 2S1 122mm
     artillery and ZSU-23s for air defense. The army has also been known to use complex obstacles (such as the
     mine-wire obstacle) in the defense.

     C-4. Atropia does have a non-persistent chemical weapons capability and have indicated a willingness to
     use them under certain, currently unspecified conditions. They also possess the capability to develop
     biological weapons, and the necessary delivery systems to employ them.

Paramilitary

     C-5. The military regime does have a paramilitary force composed primarily of former police officers,
     private security contractors, and mercenaries. They have a various array of weapons and capabilities. Some
     units have acquired anti-armor weapons, and possess the capability to construct IEDs capable of disabling or
     destroying light and medium vehicles.

Irregular and Insurgent forces

     C-6. The republic of Atropia liberation forces has access to a limited number—but wide variety—of weapon
     systems. They have captured a large cache of former U.S. weaponry that includes small arms, and limited
     quantities of recoilless rifles and AT-4s. The resistance has acquired Russian-made equipment from the
     military and deposed national government that includes mostly small arms and RPG-7s, but did include some
     SA-7 Man Portable Air Defense (MANPADs). They have modified various civilian trucks to be equipped
     with heavy machine guns. Due to their limited ability to confront conventional forces directly, the resistance
     frequently constructs and employs IEDs capable of disabling or destroying light and medium vehicles. They
     have also employed IEDs against non-military targets for political gain.

     C-7. The insurgent groups have also stepped in to fill some civil service roles that became vacant following
     the coup. However, these services (such as small schools, food banks, medical clinics, and transportation)
     are available only to populations that support them in an effort to maintain and bolster popular support. They
     also have a very capable and media-savvy propaganda machine that heavily leverages digital communications
     when possible. They also have the capability to conduct some cyber warfare, but have mostly been limited
     to low-impact attacks on military regime networks.

Criminal

     C-8. There are criminal elements continuing to operate in Atropia. These groups typically try to avoid
     detection by the main belligerents, and are generally motivated by profit over politics. They will interact with
     the country’s main political factions, particularly if it is advantageous to their operations, and are willing to
     work for or with multiple opposing parties simultaneously. They will avoid direct and open confrontation
     whenever possible, but are willing to use force to defend or pursue their interests.

     C-9. Atropia’s criminal organizations have resorted to kidnapping, trafficking people, cybercrime, pirating,
     smuggling, and identity theft in addition to more traditional crime such as armed robbery, arson, extortion,
     racketeering, murder-for-hire, and illegal gambling, typically establishing fronts to avoid detection and law

11 May 2017  ATP 3-21.91  C-3


---

Appendix C

     enforcement interference. These groups also impose a tax on local citizens for highlighting the government’s
     inability to maintain law and order.

     C-10. The criminal elements tap into well-established smuggling networks to acquire their equipment. Most
     of their equipment is available on the black market, but they have acquired some weapons and equipment
     from the deposed government’s stockpiles and by importing equipment from neighboring countries. Some
     of their equipment includes former US small arms, recoilless rifles, and AT-4s. They also have Russian-made
     small arms, machine guns, and RPG-7s. They primarily utilize civilian light trucks, and have modified a very
     limited number with heavy machine guns, preferring stealth to open confrontation. They have the capability
     to make IEDs, and have used them to pursue generally criminal objectives or impede governmental forces’
     ability to apprehend them.

  SECTION II – RECONNAISSANCE

     C-11. The reconnaissance phase of the SBCT operation begins with the SBCT weapons troop commander
     receiving the WARNORD from the Cavalry squadron. He receives a brief overview of the area of operation
     and the following tentative mission statements:

             SBCT Mission: XX conducts movement to contact from LD to phase line XX to determine the
             enemy’s composition, disposition, and set the conditions for subsequent operations in the AO.

             CAV SQDN: XX conducts zone reconnaissance of the AO to facilitate the SBCT’s follow on
             deliberate operations. O/O locate and engage hostile forces within capabilities to retain freedom
             of maneuver.

     C-12. The SBCT commander keeps the SBCT weapons troop pure to support the Cavalry squadron
     reconnaissance mission. He has authorized the squadron commander to task organize the SBCT weapons
     troop across the squadron at his discretion. The Cavalry squadron commander splits the weapons troop by
     task organizing one platoon weapons platoon (+) to each Cavalry troop. The remainder of the SBCT weapons
     troop follow and support the squadron behind C troop in the order of march.

PLAN

     C-13. To meet the squadron commander’s intent, the SBCT weapons troop commander task organizes his
     six platoons into mixed-platform weapons platoons. He then assigns a four-vehicle weapons platoon to each
     Cavalry troop (see figure C-2).

C-4  ATP 3-21.91  11 May 2017


---

                          Weapons Troop Fictional Scenario

                        Figure C-2. SBCT Cavalry squadron task organization

C-14. The weapons platoons attached to the Cavalry troop are immediately sent to link up with their
supported units and integrate themselves into their troop leading procedures. The weapons troop commander
attaches weapons platoons (+) to SBCT Cavalry troops in accordance with the habitual relationships (See
chapter one for more information). He maximizes preparation time by linking up his reassigned platoons with
their supported units immediately following the approval of the task organization.

C-15. The Cavalry squadron commander determines the SBCT weapons troop’s commitment decision
criteria, engagement criteria, and how he envisions employing the SBCT weapons troop to support the
Squadron’s zone reconnaissance. He specifies that the SBCT weapons troop will focus on the destruction of
enemy armored vehicles that cannot be destroyed or defeated by indirect fires due to tactical (METT-TC)
considerations or because a direct fire engagement is more practical or effective. The squadron commander
plans to commit the SBCT weapons troop if the weapons platoons attached to the Cavalry troops are
insufficient to defeat the threat in their individual sectors.

C-16. The Cavalry squadron commander directs the SBCT weapons troop to maintain a tactical position
between the TAC and at least one terrain feature behind the attached weapons platoons. The squadron
commander’s intent is to prevent the premature commitment of the SBCT weapons troop, maximize the
combat power available in the SBCT weapons troop by reducing exposure to enemy effects, and allow the
weapons troop to mass and engage from a position of tactical advantage.

C-17. The weapons troop commander develops his plan based on warfighting functions and the commander’s
guidance. His plan considers: maximizing freedom of maneuver to allow rapid response, limit exposure,
maintain element of surprise, and keep effective mission command to adjust to changing conditions. The
weapons troop commander then conducts the necessary analysis and develops his plan (see figure C-3, page
C-6).

        Legend: LD = line of deployment, LOA = limit of advance, NAI = named area of interest, PL = phase

PREPARE

     C-18. The weapons troop prepares by conducting rehearsals. These rehearsals include: battle handover,
     occupying a fighting position, target engagement, displacing from a fighting position, movement techniques,
     movement formations, hide site occupation procedures, engagement area SOPs, battle drills, MEDEVAC
     and CASEVAC, and vehicle recovery. The Weapons Troop also rehearses its communications primary,
     alternate, contingency, and emergency plan and contingency plans.

     C-19. The troop also conducts pre-combat checks and inspections by platoons to ensure the unit remains
     secure. During the precombat checks and PCI, the troop leadership pays special attention to special
     equipment, including PAS-13s and binoculars.

     C-20. Finally, the troop conducts a ‘short count’ to start their vehicles simultaneously. Each platoon moves
     into the order of movement specified by the commander as: fourth platoon in the lead, then the commander,
     fifth platoon, headquarters, and sixth platoon. Once the troop has moved into its marching order, the platoons
     adjust into their specified formations: troop column, lead platoon wedge, following platoons in staggered
     column.

     C-21. The troop commander makes the final necessary coordination between the Cavalry troops and
     squadron to ensure synchronization. The weapons troop then moves into its first hide site. The squadron
     commander waits until the sun has set before initiating the zone reconnaissance. Once conditions are set, the
     squadron commander directs the Cavalry troops cross the LD. The weapons troop stands by for the Cavalry
     troops to attain their planned distances forward.

C-6         ATP 3-21.91  11 May 2017

                                                                                                     Weapons Troop Fictional Scenario

EXECUTE

     C-22. The Cavalry troops begin their zone reconnaissance to PL Sue (LOA [limit of advance]). They move
     forward stealthily and deliberately towards PL Tracy. They encounter enemy reconnaissance elements
     consisting of observation posts (OPs) and supporting BRDMs and technical trucks. A and B troops with their
     attached weapons platoons (+) engage and defeat the enemy’s reconnaissance elements and pursue them
     towards PL Nita. C Troop continues to move behind A and B troops, providing depth to the zone
     reconnaissance and ensuring that no enemy reconnaissance elements have passed A and B Troops’ zone
     reconnaissance efforts. The Weapons troop remains in Hide Site 1 (see figure C-4).

  Legend: EA = engagement area, ENY = enemy, PL = phase line, TAC = tactical command post

                             Figure C-4. Target handoff with A and B troops

C-23. A and B troops cross PL Tracy and the weapons troop moves rapidly to Hide Site 2 in accordance with
the squadron scheme of maneuver. A and B troops identify elements of an enemy mechanized Infantry
company in the vicinity of PL Nita. A troop observes a tank platoon and a mechanized Infantry platoon in its
zone, and B troop identifies a mechanized Infantry platoon in its path. Both troops report the contact to

11 May 2017  ATP 3-21.91  C-7


---

Appendix C

     squadron. The squadron commander identifies that the enemy in A troop’s sector exceeds that troop’s ability
     to engage. He contacts the weapons troop commander and instructs him to engage to destroy the enemy
     platoons in EA Eagle.

     C-24. The weapons troop commander coordinates with the A troop commander to incorporate first weapons
     troop platoon into the engagement area plan. They agree to position first platoon to the southeast of BP
     Hammer. The weapons troop commander and A troop commander also discuss the current locations of A
     troop’s elements and OPs to ensure an accurate picture to prevent fratricide. The weapons troop commander
     plans his engagement to avoid placing any A troop elements within the SDZ’s of the weapons systems firing
     into EA Eagle. He then shares his plan with the A troop commander to confirm that all A Troop elements are
     outside of the EA Eagle SDZs. Once the A troop commander has confirmed his elements’ locations are clear
     of EA Eagle, he indicates to the weapons troop commander that he can begin the engagement.

     C-25. The weapons troop commander issues his orders to his platoon leaders, orienting his forces on line
     around EA Eagle. He positions his vehicle and the fire support officer’s vehicle behind the weapons troop’s
     battle position. He then orders his platoon to occupy their platoon battle positions and assume a hull-down
     posture. Once in position, the commander conducts a leader’s reconnaissance of the EA to confirm his
     engagement area SOP and template, and make any necessary fire control measure or positioning adjustments.
     Once satisfied that his template with a near-to-far frontal fire pattern will work, he gives his final instructions
     to his four platoon leaders who prepare accordingly.

     C-26. As the approaching enemy enters EA Eagle, the weapons troop commander initiates his engagement.
     The ATGM in 4th, 5th, and 6th platoons engage the three enemy tanks. One MGS from 4th, 5th, and 6th platoons
     fire their main guns aiming at the tanks’ flanks in an attempt to immobilize the enemy to prevent them from
     focusing on the ATGMs. The second MGS in 4th, 5th, and 6th platoons exposes itself only as much as necessary
     to provide observation of effects, battle damage assessment, decide whether to re-engage if necessary, and
     report that information to the weapons troop commander. 1st platoon provides redundancy to the engagement,
     fighting in two ATGM/MGS pairs, and ensuring they remain in a hull down position throughout the initial
     volley.

     C-27. 4th, 5th, and 6th platoons destroy three enemy tanks with TOW missiles and do not sustain any losses.
     However, the mechanized Infantry platoon attempts to bypass the troop’s position to the south, and moves
     into 1st platoon’s sector of EA Eagle. The weapons troop commander identifies the maneuver and orders first
     platoon to engage. 1st platoon utilizes two MGSs for the attack with the ATGMs providing redundancy on
     the two targeted BMPs if needed. The MGSs engage and destroy the BMPs using two HEAT rounds each.
     The remaining BMP withdraws from the engagement and successfully breaks contact by heading west. The
     weapons troop commander assesses that the enemy was unable to accurately identify all of the weapons
     troop’s battle positions during the engagement.

     C-28. As soon as 1st platoon’s engagement is complete, the weapons troop commander contacts the A troop
     commander and returns control of first platoon back over to him. He instructs the troop to move rapidly to
     Hide Site 3, where they begin reloading operations while in a covered and concealed location, and prepare
     to assist B troop if necessary. 2nd platoon—attached to B troop—successfully engages the approaching
     mechanized Infantry platoon utilizing two volleys from their MGSs. In that engagement, the first volley
     results in only one destroyed enemy vehicle. The BMPs identify the MGS positions, maneuver to engage,
     and initiate direct fires on the identified MGS positions. The MGSs then re-engage from their alternate
     positions, and destroy the remaining two BMPs before they can adjust and reacquire them.

PHASE III

     C-29. As the Cavalry squadron continues zone reconnaissance, it confirms a mechanized Infantry company
     (+). This company approaches A troop’s sector in company wedge with three mechanized infantry platoons.
     Each platoon is composed of one tank and two BMPs. To the north, the enemy approaches east into B troop’s
     sector with a tank platoon composed of three tanks. Both A and B Troops report their contacts to the squadron
     commander. The squadron commander orders the weapons troop to engage and destroy the enemy. The
     weapons troop commander assesses the relative strengths of the enemy in each sector. He decides to split his
     formation and engage simultaneously. He assigns 5th platoon to B Troop, adding an extra ATGM vehicle to
     permit a single-volley engagement of the opposing tank platoon. The weapons troop commander moves into
     position to engage the enemy in EA Thunder. (See figure C-5.)

C-30. The weapons troop commander coordinates with both A and B Troop commanders to facilitate the
attachment of 2nd platoon to B troop, and first platoon back to the weapons troop. Once the B troop
commander confirms radio contact with fifth platoon’s platoon leader, he returns to coordinating actions in
EA Lightning with 2nd (+) and 5th (-) weapons troop platoons. The A troop commander updates the weapons
troop commander on the disposition of his reconnaissance forces and OPs. He also verifies the weapons troop
commander’s engagement plan for EA Thunder. The weapons troop commander verifies receipt of first
platoon from A troop, and assumes mission command of the platoon for the duration of the engagement.

C-31. In B troop’s sector, the weapons troop platoons engage the enemy tanks. The ATGM in 5th platoon
targets the closest tank on the south side of the enemy’s formation. The ATGMs in 2nd platoon target the
remaining two tanks. The MGSs target the same vehicles as their ATGM partner with the intent to distract
the enemy from concentrating on the ATGMs, and to achieve mobility kills that improve the probability of a
successful TOW missile engagement. The second MGS in 5th platoon provides observation of effects, battle

11 May 2017  ATP 3-21.91  C-9


---

Appendix C

     damage assessment, determines whether to re-engage if necessary, and reports that information to the B troop
     commander. B troop’s engagement with second and fifth weapons platoons disables two tanks before all
     three are destroyed by the ATGMs’ TOW missiles almost simultaneously to the MGSs’ 105mm HEAT round
     strikes.

     C-32. In A troop’s sector, 1st platoon targets the enemy’s southernmost platoon, 6th platoon targets the lead
     enemy platoon, and 4th platoon targets the northernmost enemy platoon. 1st and 4th platoons use a depth fire
     pattern where the ATGMs target the furthest vehicles and the MGSs target the closer vehicles. In first platoon,
     only one ATGM engages, while the other provides observation of effects, battle damage assessment, and
     reports that information to the weapons troop commander. Each vehicle ensures that they will have an
     unobstructed view of their target vehicles at the time fires are initiated, and that friendly munitions will not
     impede other friendly munitions, such as the TOW missiles and their guidewires. The platoons also designate
     alternate vehicle fighting positions in case re-engagement is necessary.

     C-33. The weapons troop commander initiates the engagement with a troop fire command. All three weapons
     platoons engage simultaneously. Three of the six MGSs fail to destroy their targets with the first round, and
     they engage a second time from their alternate positions, successfully destroying their targets. The firing
     ATGM in first platoon experiences a temporary malfunction with its TOW missile launcher. However, the
     targeted BMP has begun withdrawing to the west, and quickly begins to pass 2500 meters in range from first
     platoon’s MGSs. The Weapons Troop commander instructs first platoon to continue the engagement with its
     second ATGM, which engages and destroys the withdrawing BMP with a TOW missile at a range of 2900
     meters. The weapons troop commander reports the engagement’s results to the Squadron commander.

     C-34. At the completion of the engagement, the weapons troop commander returns mission command of first
     platoon to A troop, and coordinates with the B troop commander to receive 5th platoon. Once the weapons
     troop commander has successfully made contact with 5th platoon leader, he provides link up instructions to
     the platoon. The weapons troop returns to Hide Site 3, and conducts rearming and resupply operations as
     necessary. The weapons troop then continues to follow and support the squadron’s zone reconnaissance,
     moving to Hide Site 4 once the Cavalry troops pass PL Alex.

     C-35. The Cavalry squadron completes its zone reconnaissance to PL Sue (LOA), and transitions to a guard.
     The Cavalry troops establish their observation posts, arrayed in depth, while the weapons troop remains in
     Hide Site 4 and completes resupply operations.

  SECTION III – GUARD

     C-36. The Cavalry squadron completes its zone reconnaissance to PL Sue (LOA), and transitions to a guard.
     The Cavalry troops establish their observation posts, arrayed in depth, while the weapons troop remains in
     Hide Site 4 and completes resupply operations. The brigade commander directs the squadron to guard the
     brigade as the Infantry battalions complete their offensive missions from the previous tactical phase,
     transition to the defense in anticipation of an enemy attack, and construct their battle positions.

     C-37. The squadron commander retains the same task organization as the zone reconnaissance phase. He
     provides the weapons troop commander discretion to utilize hide sites throughout the squadron’s AO between
     PL Sue and PL Nita, so long as they remain in supporting distance of the Cavalry troops.

PLAN

     C-38. The weapons troop commander contacts the squadron S-2 to obtain the most recent intelligence update.
     Based on the S-2’s intelligence assessment, the weapons troop commander determines that the enemy’s most
     likely course of action is to neutralize the weapons troop by identifying the weapons troop’s hide site and
     deny the use of that area.

     C-39. The weapons troop commander also assesses that the enemy will likely conduct its own reconnaissance
     prior to attacking with a battalion task force. He predicts that the enemy’s attack will lead with two
     mechanized Infantry company teams in an attempt to defeat reconnaissance units. This attack is an attempt
     by the enemy to force weapons troop platoons to reveal their positions and engage them. Once the weapons
     platoon’s positions are identified, the enemy will fix and isolate each weapons platoon. (See figure C-6.)

C-40. The weapons troop commander identifies the enemy’s anticipated avenues of approach, plans
advantageous engagement areas to destroy the enemy, and covered and concealed routes to and from those
engagement areas. He selects and plans four EAs: Bronze, Steel, Copper, and Iron. The EAs are arrayed in
depth in both A and B troop sectors, with two EAs per Cavalry troop sector. He assigns direct fire control
measures (DFCMs) and indirect fires in accordance with the troop EA SOP and template.

C-41. The commander also selects platoon hide sites for 4th, 5th, and 6th platoons, and the headquarters section
to facilitate dispersion. He also plans for alternate hide sites, displacement criteria, and all necessary routes
for rapid movement of platoons in the event of compromise. 4th, 5th, and 6th platoons are then moved to Hide
Sites 441, 451, and 461 respectively. The troop headquarters remains at Hide Site 4. 4th, 5th, and 6th platoons
alternate hide sites are Hide Sites 442, 452, and 462 respectively. The troop headquarters alternate hide site
is Hide Site 41. The commander then tasks the platoons with providing bottom-up refinement of the selected
positions, and making recommendations on an additional alternate hide site for their platoons.

C-42. The troop FSO plans indirect fires in accordance with the weapons troop commander’s intent for fires
and the troop EA SOP and template. He also plans for the use of any enabling forces or additional assets that
the troop may receive during the course of the operation, such as attack aviation, fixed-wing aircraft, artillery,

11 May 2017  ATP 3-21.91  C-11


---

Appendix C

     or Cavalry troop mortar sections. He does this despite not having any pre-planned assets given to the troop
     by higher headquarters, but to be prepared to utilize any assets that he may receive as the situation develops.

     C-43. Finally, the weapons troop commander plans operations that delay the enemy to allow the retrograde
     of reconnaissance forces once the enemy’s main attack is identified. He also plans for the rearward passage
     of lines (RPOL) of the weapons troop, and coordinates directly with the infantry companies in whose sectors
     the RPOL will occur.

PREPARE

     C-44. As the weapons troop begins preparations for the guard mission, the troop’s leaders continue to
     maintain situation awareness of friendly forces’ disposition along the screen line, and the depth of the
     squadron’s positions. They also maintain awareness of the infantry battalions’ positions, with particular
     attention paid to the units involved with the RPOL and passage lanes.

     C-45. As the reconnaissance forces conduct their counter-reconnaissance mission, the weapons troop
     leadership monitors their reports and actions. Particular attention is paid to the composition and disposition
     of enemy forces engaged and any information that might indicate an enemy course of action. The weapons
     troop commander also monitors the employment of the weapons troop platoons attached to the Cavalry troops
     by the troop commanders.

     C-46. The weapons troop continues to conduct TLPs in accordance with the troop SOP. In addition to the
     standard tasks, the commander includes rehearsals of: conduct of the EAs, survivability moves in the event
     elements or the entire troop are compromised by enemy reconnaissance forces, massing the troop at an
     anticipated EA from dispersed hide sites, consolidation and reorganization after an engagement, displacement
     to subsequent positions, the passing of the Cavalry troops upon collapsing the screen line, the weapons
     troop’s RPOL, and potential contingencies.

     C-47. The weapons troop commander also conducts a leader’s reconnaissance of his anticipated EAs to the
     extent possible to confirm DFCMs and fires targets, and adjusts them as necessary. Once he has completed
     his reconnaissance and finalized his plans, he coordinates with squadron and the Cavalry troops to ensure a
     common operating picture and that friendly elements will be clear of the SDZs at each EA within commander-
     determined acceptable levels of risk.

EXECUTE

     C-48. The Cavalry squadron completes its zone reconnaissance to PL Sue (LOA), and transitions to a guard.
     The Cavalry troops establish their observation posts, arrayed in depth, while the weapons troop remains in
     Hide Site 4 and completes resupply operations.

     C-49. A and B troops identify a battalion-sized element moving into the Squadron’s sector. A and B troops
     identify a mechanized Infantry company/team with two tank platoons in each of their sectors. In both sectors,
     the mechanized Infantry company/team lead the attack. One enemy company approaches EA Bronze, and
     one company approaches EA Copper. Both are arrayed in a company wedge formation. A and B troops report
     the approaching enemy forces to the squadron headquarters.

     C-50. Based on the Cavalry troops’ reports, the squadron commander decides to employ the weapons troop
     to destroy the enemy in A troop’s sector first, and then the enemy in B troop’s sector at EA Steel. He also
     orders the squadron to begin collapsing the screen line. A and B troops conduct a reconnaissance handover
     with C Troop, and utilize their weapons troop platoons to cover the Cavalry troops’ displacement. The
     weapons troop’s 2nd platoon (attached to B troop) covers B troop’s displacement by delaying the enemy at
     EA Bronze while the weapons troop engages at EA Copper along with 1st platoon. Once the enemy is
     destroyed in EA Copper, the weapons troop moves to EA Steel, and destroys the enemy forces in that
     engagement area along with 2nd platoon. The squadron commander then contacts his troop commanders and
     directs the course of action.

     C-51. The weapons troop commander issues his orders to his platoon leaders to occupy their positions at EA
     Copper, and the troop begins its movement. The weapons troop commander contacts the A troop commander
     to finalize the engagement plan, and to obtain temporary mission command of the weapons troop’s 1st
     platoon. 1st platoon moves into its designated battle position, while 4th, 5th, and 6th platoons mass at the troop

C-12  ATP 3-21.91  11 May 2017


---

                          Weapons Troop Fictional Scenario

  battle position and occupy their platoon battle positions. Once the troop is set in their positions facing EA
  Copper, they conduct a radio rehearsal.

  C-52. The enemy’s mechanized Infantry company teams and tank platoons enter EA Bronze and EA Copper.
  The weapons troop engages the enemy in EA Copper, and 2nd platoon engages the enemy in EA Bronze. In
  EA Bronze, 2nd platoon destroys two enemy tanks and two enemy BMPs with its two ATGMs and two MGSs.
  They then displace towards EA Steel. In EA Copper, the weapons troop engages the enemy with a
  simultaneous volley from all four platoons. They destroy three enemy tanks and six enemy BMPs, but fifth
  platoon has one ATGM catastrophically destroyed by an enemy tank.

  C-53. The squadron commander directs the weapons troop to move to EA Steel to engage the approaching
  enemy in B troop’s sector, support the weapons troop’s 2nd platoon, and facilitate B troop’s retrograde. Three
  enemy tanks and three BMPs continue moving through A troop’s sector. The squadron commander instructs
  the C troop commander to send the weapons troop’s 3rd platoon (attached to C troop) to support the weapons
  troop’s 1st platoon at EA Iron. The squadron commander also orders C troop to conduct its RPOL through
  the SBCT’s defensive positions and rejoin the squadron. The weapons troop commander leaves his troop XO
  with 1st platoon to control the engagement at EA Iron utilizing 1st and 3rd platoons, and moves the troop with
  4th, 5th, and 6th platoons to EA Steel.

  C-54. The Troop XO engages the enemy in EA Iron with 1st and 3rd platoons and utilizing the EA SOP
  template. They destroy three enemy tanks and three enemy BMPs, but one ATGM and one MGS in 1st platoon
  are destroyed by direct fire. The first platoon sergeant manages to recover one wounded soldier from the
  MGS, and places him inside his ATGM for evacuation. Upon completion of the engagement in EA Iron, the
  weapons troop XO contacts the weapons troop commander and provides a situation report. The weapons
  troop commander gives the order to conduct the RPOL with 1st and 3rd platoons, and then moves out to RP
  E1.

  C-55. Once the weapons troop has occupied its battle positions over-watching EA Steel and conducts its link
  up with 2nd platoon, the enemy enters EA Steel. Once the enemy is inside EA Steel, the weapons troop
  commander initiates his fires, and destroys three enemy tanks and six enemy BMPs. One ATGM—the
  platoon sergeant’s ATGM—in 2nd platoon is destroyed by an enemy BMP. The second senior section
  sergeant is able to recover one soldier from the platoon sergeant’s ATGM and places him on his MGS. Also,
  the platoon leader’s MGS from 6th platoon is destroyed, and the 6th platoon sergeant assumes leadership of
  the platoon. The C troop commander contacts the squadron commander and weapons troop commander to
  inform them that he has completed his RPOL.

  C-56. While conducting the engagement, the weapons troop commander also identifies the next enemy
  battalion task force in the enemy brigade’s formation. He reports this information to the squadron
  commander, and receives confirmation to begin conducting the rearward passage of lines.

  C-57. The weapons troop moves to RP E1 and consolidates in low ground. The troop first sergeant works
  with the medics and platoon sergeants to transfer the casualty to the troop MEV. The remainder of the troop
  reloads and refuels as time permits, and the troop quickly moves out towards the RPOL passage lane.

  C-58. Once the weapons troop links up with the stationary Infantry unit, it begins its rearward passage of
  lines through the passage lane. Once released on the far side of the SBCT Infantry rifle company’s battle
  positions at the passage lane release point, the weapons troop begins its transition to supporting the SBCT’s
  defense. This includes attaching 2nd and 3rd platoons to the SBCT’s main effort, 1st SBCT Infantry battalion,
  and placing the weapons troop with 1st, 4th, 5th, and 6th platoons in reserve.

SECTION IV - DEFENSE

  C-59. The SBCT plans a defense in depth to defeat the enemy attack. It arrays its three SBCT Infantry
  battalions on line across the width of the SBCT area of operation with each battalion preparing multiple
  engagement areas in depth. The first SBCT Infantry battalion occupies the center position with the second
  Infantry battalion on its left (north) and the third Infantry battalion on its right (south).

  C-60. The SBCT S-2 assesses that the enemy will attack at two points with a battalion task force. Those two
  task forces will attempt to achieve to penetrate the defensive position. Whichever task force achieves the
  greatest degree of success will be the avenue of approach for the enemy’s assault force, comprised of another

11 May 2017  ATP 3-21.91  C-13


---

Appendix C

     battalion task force. The assault force will be followed by the enemy’s exploitation force once the assault
     force has defeated the friendly battalion opposing it. The SBCT S-2 predicts that the enemy is most likely to
     use the avenue of approach through the first SBCT Infantry battalion’s sector, as it provides the best avenue
     of approach for a high tempo attack.

     C-61. The SBCT commander splits the weapons troop. He designates the first Infantry battalion as the
     SBCT’s main effort. He assigns two weapons platoons (2nd and 3rd) to the first SBCT Infantry battalion, and
     places the weapons troop with four platoons, and one SBCT Infantry rifle company from the 3rd SBCT
     Infantry battalion in the SBCT reserve. The weapons troop ensures 2nd and 3rd platoons link up with the SBCT
     Infantry battalion, and occupies TAA Eagle and begins planning for operations. (See figure C-7.)
