                                 Figure C-7. SBCT task organization for a defense

PLAN

     C-62. The 1st SBCT Infantry battalion plans three engagement areas in its sector to destroy the enemy. They
     are arrayed in depth sequentially from east to west. The SBCT Infantry battalion commander places A and C
     companies in battle positions oriented on EA Pirate. He places B company oriented on EA Raider. The
     commander plans to utilize the weapons troop by placing it into position to the right (south) of B company
     at EA Raider should the SBCT commander commit the weapons troop to his sector. He also plans a battle
     position for the weapons troop at EA Buccaneer for the final planned engagement area in the battalion defense
     in depth.

11 May 2017  ATP 3-21.91  C-15


---

Appendix C

     C-63. The SBCT Infantry battalion plans to employ obstacles. In EA Pirate, obstacles are planned to delay
     and turn the enemy to the north towards elevated, severely restricted terrain. In EA Raider, obstacles are
     planned to fix and turn the enemy to the north towards elevated, severely restricted terrain, and prevent the
     enemy from bypassing the battalion’s positions to the south. The battalion plans to employ FASCAM and
     Modular Pack Mine Systems (MOPMS) to close the passage lanes for A and C companies as part of their
     retrograde operations in support of the defense in depth.

     C-64. The battalion plans to employ artillery and mortar fires. In EA Pirate, fires will disrupt the enemy
     advance and delay the enemy to allow A and C companies to retrograde to subsequent positions. In EA Raider
     and EA Buccaneer, fires will fix the enemy to allow friendly forces to destroy them. Fires also will be
     employed in EA Raider to delay the enemy to allow the battalion to retrograde if necessary to EA Buccaneer.
     (See figure C-8.)

C-65. Much of the weapons troop commander’s planning for the defense occurs concurrently with the
planning for the execution of the guard mission. He plans for the operation by: task organizing with 1st platoon
composed of four ATGMs, 4th platoon composed of three MGS, and 5th and 6th platoons composed of one
ATGM and two MGS, coordinating with all three SBCT Infantry battalions to integrate into their tactical
plans within the SBCT commander’s intent, plans direct fires and direct fire control measures, coordinates
all plans with the respective supported SBCT Infantry battalion, coordinates weapons troop logistics with
SBCT Infantry battalions, selects the platoon battle positions at each engagement area, selects the location of

11 May 2017  ATP 3-21.91  C-17


---

Appendix C

     TAA Eagle in accordance with the SBCT commander’s intent and in coordination with the brigade staff,
     designates his high value and high payoff target lists, develops his attack guidance, and executes TLPs in
     accordance with the troop SOP.

PREPARE

     C-66. As the weapons troop begins preparations for the defense. The troop’s leaders continue to maintain
     situation awareness of friendly forces’ disposition along the defensive line. They also maintain awareness of
     the enemy’s composition, disposition, and actions as their attack begins.

     C-67. The weapons troop continues to conduct TLPs in accordance with the troop SOP. In addition to the
     standard tasks, the commander includes rehearsals of: conduct of the EAs, survivability moves in the event
     elements or the entire troop are compromised by enemy reconnaissance forces, massing the troop at an
     anticipated EA from dispersed hide sites, consolidation and reorganization after an engagement, displacement
     to subsequent positions, link ups with supported units, routes to and from battle positions, displacements and
     retrogrades from battle positions, and potential contingencies.

     C-68. The weapons troop commander also conducts a leader’s reconnaissance of his anticipated EAs to the
     extent possible to confirm DFCMs and fires targets, and adjusts them as necessary. He also familiarizes
     himself with each engagement area, paying particular attention to obstacles, dead space, and fires target
     locations. The weapons troop is not responsible for the construction or security of any obstacles or any
     engagement area beyond the requirements that facilitate or enable the weapons troop’s engagements, such as
     battle position marking. Finally, the weapons troop commander ensures his plan is coordinated and
     synchronized with the supported unit.

     C-69. The weapons troop conducts resupply, refuel, and rearm operations as necessary in TAA Eagle as the
     SBCT defense begins. This operation is conducted as rapidly as possible, and requires that the logistics assets
     supporting the weapons troop are in TAA Eagle no later than when the weapons troop arrives. Any last
     minute changes are made, disseminated, and rehearsed as necessary and to the limit of available resources
     with the necessary leaders and greatest level of leader participation possible given ongoing logistics
     operations. As soon as logistics operations are complete, the weapons troop immediately resumes the highest
     readiness condition in anticipation of a rapid commitment.

EXECUTE

     C-70. As the weapons troop completes its resupply, refueling, and rearming operations, the SBCT S-2
     assesses that the enemy identified in EA Pirate is the advance guard of the enemy’s main attack. The SBCT
     Commander then commits the weapons troop to support the 1st SBCT Infantry battalion at EA Raider while
     the 1st SBCT Infantry battalion conducts its initial engagement at EA Pirate. Immediately, the weapons troop
     moves from TAA Eagle to the defilade positions behind the troop battle position at EA Raider.

     C-71. At EA Pirate, A and C companies’ attached weapons platoons assist their supported units in destroying
     the enemy. Both platoons are kept behind the company’s main battle positions, and are committed to their
     platoon battle positions only when required to actively engage the approaching enemy. The weapons platoon
     attached to A company occupies a position on the right (south) flank of the company position. The weapons
     platoon attached to C company also occupies the right (south) flank of the company position to prevent the
     enemy from bypassing C company’s position and EA Pirate to the south. Both platoons are afforded alternate
     firing positions, and are able to engage the enemy with two volleys. The first volley assists in the destruction
     of the approaching enemy, while the second provides suppresses the enemy. This allows the Infantry to mount
     their Strykers and displace to EA Raider. Once A and C companies have displaced, the battalion fires its final
     protective fire target (utilizing a high explosive and smoke mix), and the weapons troop platoons bound west
     towards EA Raider. Once within supporting range of A, B, and C companies, and the weapons troop, they
     travel expeditiously to their positions supporting actions at EA Raider.

     C-72. At EA Raider, the weapons troop arrays its forces in the troop battle position on line with 1st platoon
     on the left (north), 5th platoon, 6th platoon, and then 4th platoon on the right (south) of the troop’s position.
     When the enemy reaches the engagement area initiation trigger point, the weapons troop commander initiates
     the troop’s fires in accordance with the battalion’s plan, requiring the weapons troop to conduct multiple
     volleys from the same troop battle position. The weapons troop commander chooses to engage the enemy

C-18        ATP 3-21.91  11 May 2017


---

                                                                                               Weapons Troop Fictional Scenario

with one platoon volley at a time, and platoons transitioning between primary and alternate vehicle battle
positions. The weapons troop commander controls his fires so that a platoon is always engaging the enemy
to reduce the enemy’s ability to scan and identify friendly vehicles and positions, and return fire.

C-73. The weapons troop employs its weapons systems against legitimate targets in accordance with the
weapons troop commander’s attack guidance. The MGS engage engineer and breaching vehicles first,
followed by BMPs. The ATGMs focus on enemy heavy armor vehicles with breaching equipment, then
heavy armor vehicles, and then engineer and breaching vehicles that are outside the range of the MGS 105mm
cannon. The SBCT Infantry battalion and weapons troop’s engagement at EA Raider destroys the enemy’s
fixing force, inflicting an estimated 70% casualties. While the remaining enemy conduct an orderly
withdrawal, they are soon passed by additional enemy elements.

C-74. Although the enemy’s fixing force has been defeated in EA Raider, the enemy commander commits
his assault force to follow and assume the fixing force in an effort to break through the 1st SBCT Infantry
battalion’s position. Once the SBCT commander determines that the enemy will successfully pass through
EA Raider, he instructs the 1st SBCT Infantry battalion commander to direct the battalion to occupy EA
Buccaneer. The battalion initiates its final protective fires and begins displacing to its battle positions at EA
Buccaneer. B company moves to EA Buccaneer, followed by C company, A company, and then the weapons
troop.

C-75. Logistics operations in EA Raider focus primarily on casualty evacuation and vehicle recovery. Platoon
sergeants collect, consolidate, and facilitate platoon medic treatment of casualties to the extent possible. They
are evacuated to the troop CCP to the extent possible between platoon volleys. However, the evacuation of
casualties from the platoon to troop ceases as the troop prepares to retrograde from EA Raider to EA
Buccaneer to prevent the troop’s retrograde from being delayed or compromised because of casualty transfer
operations from platoon to troop.

C-76. Vehicle recovery is mainly conducted by like-vehicles within each platoon. Catastrophically destroyed
vehicles are not recovered until after the engagement is concluded and the area secured. Vehicles that are
damaged but recoverable, but cannot be recovered within the METT-TC considerations of the existing
tactical situation are destroyed to prevent their exploitation by enemy forces. Recovered vehicles are
consolidated at the troop maintenance collection point and transferred to higher levels of maintenance in
accordance with the battalion or squadron plan, and are evacuated by higher level maintenance recovery
vehicles to minimize the number of combat vehicles committed to recovery instead of combat operations.

C-77. The weapons troop occupies its troop battle position at EA Buccaneer on line with 4th platoon on the
left (north), 5th platoon, 6th platoon, and then 4th platoon on the right (south) of the troop’s position. The troop
engages the enemy assault force at EA Buccaneer in accordance with the 1st SBCT Infantry battalion’s plan.
The attack guidance is for MGS to engage engineer and breaching vehicles first, followed by BMPs. The
ATGMs again focus on the enemy’s heavy armor vehicles with breaching equipment, then heavy armor
vehicles, and then engineer and breaching vehicles that are outside the range of the MGS 105mm cannon.
The effects of these synchronized fires is to destroy the enemy assault force in EA Buccaneer.

C-78. The weapons troop conducts its engagements by firing continuous sequential platoon volleys to ensure
constant enemy engagement. At the weapons troop commander’s discretion, he utilizes troop or multi-
platoon volleys to inflict psychological effect on the enemy in addition to inflicting significant casualties.
The weapons troop commander uses the technique if platoon volleys are insufficient to prevent the enemy
from accurately returning fire, or to destroy company-sized units attempting coordinated actions to adapt and
overcome conditions in EA Buccaneer. The seemingly random order of weapons troop platoon engagements
enables the weapons troop to conceal its actual composition and disposition. The rapidly conduct volleys also
reduce the enemy’s ability to gain the initiative at the vehicle level to effectively fight through the engagement
area. The result of the engagement is the destruction of 80 percent of the enemy’s assault force, with the
remainder withdrawing eastward.

C-79. Logistics operations conducted during this phase of the operation are similar to those conducted at EA
Raider. However, there is a possibility that during the engagement at EA Buccaneer that units will expend
much of their class III and V. The weapons troop employs three methods to address resupply: first, the troop
positions caches are each platoon battle position for their use. Second, the troop 1SG or XO conducts an
emergency resupply operation to the affected platoon and resupplies them at the rear of their platoon battle

11 May 2017  ATP 3-21.91  C-19


---

Appendix C

     position to reduce the time and number of vehicles disengaged from combat operations. Finally, the weapons
     troop conducts resupply to platoons based on consumption reported during the retrograde from the previous
     battle position, and meets the platoon at its battle position when it arrives. This is least preferred because it
     has the greatest risk to the weapons troop’s logistics assets, but may be necessary in certain tactical situations.

     C-80. At the conclusion of actions in EA Buccaneer and when directed, the SBCT Infantry battalion and
     weapons troop begin to consolidate and reorganize. The casualties the weapons Troop sustained during the
     engagement are evacuated to the CCP, where the First Sergeant oversees the triage, treatment, and further
     evacuation of personnel. The troop XO brings the troop’s logistics forward, and refuels and rearms the troop
     using the service station method in a covered and concealed position behind the troop’s battle positions.
     Platoons are rotated through the service station logistics one at a time. The weapons troop commander
     immediately transitions to TLPs for the next mission: offense.

  SECTION V- OFFENSE

     C-81. The SBCT defeated the enemy’s attacks and transitions to the offense. The SBCT will seize objectives
     in its sector to allow follow-on friendly forces to pass through and secure the international border. The SBCT
     S-2 assesses that they will be opposed by an enemy battalion task force comprised primarily of Armored
     fighting vehicles, reconnaissance vehicles, and Infantry. The SBCT anticipates that the enemy will defend
     three choke points: the town of Ujen, the crossroads of highways 1 and 5, and a high bridge over the Low
     river.

     C-82. The terrain in the northern sector of the SBCT’s area of operations is mountainous and rocky, but
     transitions to dense forest with multiple secondary roads and trails. To the south, the terrain is generally flat
     and open desert with the Low river running from generally north to south near the SBCT’s limit of advance.
     On the east side of the Low river to the south, the dense forest resumes, reducing north-south movement into
     the SBCT’s area of operations on the far side of the river.

PLAN

     C-83. The SBCT plans to seize three battalion objectives: OBJs Mouse, Cat, and Dog. First, the Cavalry
     Squadron will conduct a zone reconnaissance to PL Dodgeball to defeat enemy reconnaissance forces and
     prevent them from gaining and maintaining contact with friendly assault forces. Then, 1st SBCT Infantry
     battalion will seize OBJ Mouse to allow 3rd SBCT Infantry battalion to pass PL Baseball and PL Dodgeball,
     and seize OBJ Cat. 3rd SBCT Infantry battalion’s seizure of OBJ Cat will allow 2nd SBCT Infantry battalion—
     the SBCT’s main effort—to seize OBJ Dog after it has conducted a forward passage of lines with 1st SBCT
     Infantry battalion. Decisive to the SBCT’s operation is the seizure of OBJ Dog. Once the objectives are
     seized, the SBCT will then secure highways 5 and 8 to allow follow-on forces to pass through the SBCT and
     secure the international border to the east.

     C-84. 3rd weapons platoon will be attached to 3rd SBCT Infantry battalion. 1st platoon (3x ATGM) and 4th
     platoon (4x MGS) will be attached to 1st SBCT Infantry battalion. The Weapons Troop (with 2nd, 5th, and 6th
     platoons) and C company from 3rd SBCT Infantry battalion will comprise the SBCT reserve. The SBCT
     Commander anticipates that he will commit all or part of the reserve to support 2nd SBCT Infantry battalion’s
     attack on OBJ Dog. (See figure C-9.)

C-20  ATP 3-21.91  11 May 2017

                             Figure C-9. SBCT task organization for offense

C-85. Upon the SBCT’s publication of the task organization, the weapons troop immediately sends 1st, 3rd
and 4th platoons to their respective supported SBCT Infantry battalions. The weapons troop moves to the
SBCT main, and links up with C company of 3rd SBCT Infantry battalion. There, the SBCT Commander
informs the weapons troop and C company commanders that he will move with 2nd SBCT Infantry battalion
with the SBCT TAC. He instructs the weapons troop to follow 2nd SBCT Infantry battalion and remain in a
position to rapidly support the battalion’s attack on OBJ Dog. The SBCT Commander also instructs C
company to follow 3rd SBCT Infantry battalion until PL Baseball, where it will move to checkpoint R1 and
stand by to support 2nd or 3rd SBCT Infantry battalion’s attacks, or respond to any contingencies at his
discretion. The SBCT Commander’s planning priorities for the reserve are to defeat an enemy armored
counterattack, support 2nd SBCT Infantry battalion’s attack on OBJ Dog, and destroy enemy armored forces
that can delay the SBCT’s attack. (See figure C-10, page C-22.)

                                           Figure C-10. Operational plan

 C-86. The weapons troop commander begins his TLPs in accordance with the troop SOP. He determines his
 task organization and movement order of march as: 6th platoon, troop commander and FSO, 2nd platoon, troop
 headquarters, and 5th platoon. The weapons troop commander then plans the route to OBJ Dog in accordance
 with the SBCT plan, troop hide sites along the planned route, and coordinates both with 1st and 2nd SBCT
 Infantry battalions. He coordinates with 1st SBCT Infantry battalion for a FPOL around OBJ Mouse and with
 the Cavalry squadron at PL Dodgeball.

 C-87. The weapons troop commander then plans his execution of the SBCT Commander’s priorities. The
 weapons troop commander plans the engagement priorities and methods for destroying an enemy
 counterattack. If the counterattack is predominantly conducted by heavy armored vehicles, 6th platoon will
 establish an attack by fire position (ABF 61) to allow 2nd and 5th platoons to maneuver into firing positions.

C-22        ATP 3-21.91  11 May 2017

                                                                                                     Weapons Troop Fictional Scenario

     2nd platoon will occupy a firing position on one side of 6th platoon (directed by the weapons troop commander
     based on METT-TC considerations) (ABF 21 and 21A; ABF 21A is an alternate position) to allow maximum
     weapon system effect on enemy armored vehicles. 5th platoon will occupy according to METT-TC
     considerations either the remaining unoccupied side of 6th platoon, or the far side of 2nd platoon (ABF 51 and
     51A; ABF 51A is an alternate position). 5th platoon’s purpose is to facilitate 2nd platoon’s engagement and
     protect the weapons troop’s flank. If the counterattack is predominantly composed of light armor vehicles,
     6th platoon will set in an attack by fire position that allows 5th platoon to maneuver to a position of advantage
     to engage the enemy in an attack by fire. 2nd platoon will only be committed if the enemy’s composition
     exceeds the MGS’ capacity to destroy them in two volleys or fewer. ABF 61 is planned for the east side of
     OBJ Mouse, but can be adjusted to react to the specific enemy counterattack.

     C-88. The engagement priorities for the ATGMs are armored fighting vehicles and then targets beyond the
     MGS’ 105mm main gun range. The engagement priorities for the MGS are enemy armored personnel
     carriers, dismounted ATGM systems, and on order close with and destroy enemy armored vehicles, some of
     whom may initially be beyond 105mm main gun range and require additional movement and maneuver.

     C-89. The weapons troop commander then plans the execution of the next reserve priority. He plans to
     conduct a support by fire in support of 2nd SBCT Infantry battalion’s attack. He chooses his primary and
     alternate support by fire positions and plans his direct fire control measures. The weapons troop commander
     then coordinates and synchronizes his plan with the 2nd SBCT Infantry battalion to ensure rapid and seamless
     integration into their operation. He arrays his forces within his support by fire positions with 6th platoon on
     one flank (SBF 62 and SBF 62A), 2nd platoon in the center of the troop position (SBF 22 and 22A), and 5th
     platoon on the opposite troop flank (SBF 52 and 52A). The weapons troop commander will occupy troop
     SBF E2 or SBF E2A based on METT-TC considerations and the 2nd SBCT Infantry battalion’s tactical
     situation at execution of the attack.

     C-90. The weapons troop commander plans for the final reserve priority by integrating rehearsals of the react
     to contact and react to ambush battle drills into the TLPs, and ensuring the supported Infantry battalions have
     the weapons troop’s battle handover report format to streamline engagements. The weapons troop
     commander also ensures that his positioning within the SBCT’s order of movement reduces to the maximum
     extent possible the risks associated with the SDZs of his primary weapon systems in the event they must be
     employed when conducting a react to contact or ambush battle drill. He coordinates with the SBCT operations
     staff to ensure DFCMs are distributed and the weapons troop understand which directions at which times it
     may engage with each weapon system.

     C-91. The weapons troop commander, 1SG, and executive officer plan the weapons troop logistics for the
     operation. They plan for casualty evacuation, planned and emergency resupply of class III and V, vehicle and
     equipment maintenance, and vehicle recovery and evacuation. The weapons troop plans to utilize brigade
     helicopter landing zones (HLZ) along the weapons troop’s axis of advance if available or permitted, but also
     plans ambulance exchange points in accordance with the troop SOP and the SBCT plan.

PREPARE

     C-92. The weapons troop prepares for the mission in accordance with its troop leading procedure SOP. In
     addition to the standard tasks, the commander includes rehearsals of: battle handover of identified enemy
     targets, movement techniques and formations, troop and platoon battle drills, forward passages of lines,
     occupation of troop and platoon battle positions, engagement techniques and fire patterns supporting the
     attacks by fire and supports by fire, link ups with supported units, movements along the axis of advance, and
     potential contingencies.

     C-93. The weapons troop commander maintains situational awareness of the SBCT’s plan as it continues to
     be refined, and the supported SBCT Infantry battalions’ plans. The weapons troop 1SG and executive officer
     oversee resupply and rearmament operations as necessary. The weapons troop executive officer also oversees
     the planning and execution of all vehicle and equipment maintenance. The weapons troop 1SG checks on the
     platoons attached to other units as able to ensure maximum combat readiness. When all preparations are
     complete, the troop continues to execute priorities of work to include additional mission rehearsals as able.

11 May 2017  ATP 3-21.91  C-23


---

Appendix C

EXECUTE

     C-94. The SBCT places its units into their formations to conduct the attack, and moves them to the line of
     departure. 1st SBCT Infantry battalion crosses the line of departure and begins its attack on OBJ Mouse.

ATTACK ON OBJ MOUSE

     C-95. 1st SBCT Infantry battalion requests indirect fires to obscure 1st weapons platoon’s movement into
     SBF E11. The SBCT field artillery battalion fires a smoke target that that allows obscure 1st weapons platoon
     to get into position. Once 1st weapons platoon is in position, 1st SBCT Infantry battalion shifts its indirect
     fires to suppress identified enemy positions on OBJ Mouse; 1st ATGM platoon (attached to A company)
     supports the attack to gain a foothold on OBJ Mouse with TOW missile fires from a range of approximately
     3000m. 1st SBCT Infantry battalion attacks OBJ Mouse from the west, while 1st ATGM platoon and indirect
     fires suppress the enemy on OBJ Mouse. 1st SBCT Infantry battalion then establishes local support by fire
     with A company and 4th MGS platoon. B company attacks to seize a foothold, occupying key buildings along
     the perimeter of OBJ Mouse. C Company moves from its assault position into Ujen, receives 4th MGS platoon
     from A company, seizing key structures along the axis of advance. C company’s attacks enable A company
     to seize the fortified buildings in the center of the town. Once C company has reached its limit of advance,
     A company attaches 1st ATGM platoon to B company, then receives 4th MGS platoon, and passes C company.
     A company seizes the fortified buildings in the center of Ujen. Meanwhile, B company maintains security on
     the west side of the town to ensure routes into the town remain secure.

Movement to Town Center

     C-96. C company advances with 1st and 2nd platoons on line, and 3rd platoon following in support. 1st and 2nd
     platoons each have one section of MGS from 4th MGS platoon. The MGSs move between squads and
     successive bound towards the company objective. The MGS destroys light armored vehicles, fortified enemy
     positions, breach structures, and provide near side security when crossing of linear danger areas to facilitate
     the advance. Once the company objective is secured, 4th MGS platoon transitions from C company to A
     company for the battalion’s main attack. (See figure C-11.)

C-24  ATP 3-21.91  11 May 2017


---

                          Weapons Troop Fictional Scenario

                 Legend: MGS = mobile gun system

                                       Figure C-11. Bounding and intersection

Main Attack

     C-97. 4th MGS platoon occupies a support by fire position with first rifle platoon (-) from A company, and
     supports the 2nd Infantry rifle platoon’s attack on the fortified buildings. The 3rd Infantry rifle platoon provides
     flank security for the company. The A company commander holds one SBCT Infantry rifle squad in reserve.
     C company continues to advance past A company, receives 4th MGS platoon, and continues to clear the town
     of Ujen to the city limits to the east.

ATTACK ON OBJ CAT

     C-98. 3rd SBCT Infantry battalion’s scout platoon conducts route reconnaissance toward PL Dodgeball. A
     and B companies attack towards OBJ Cat on line. A section of 3rd weapons platoon is attached to A company,
     and B Section of 3rd weapons platoon is attached to B company. The battalion scouts conduct a battle
     handover to A and B companies. The SBCT Infantry rifle companies destroy enemy Infantry encountered
     along the axis of advance. 3rd weapons platoon supports the companies’ advance by engaging and destroying
     armored vehicles encountered along the axis of advance. Each time the Infantry company commanders
     encounter enemy elements, they must decide whether to engage using indirect fires, Infantry, ICVs, Infantry
     with ICVs, or their attached weapons platoon. In the case of the weapons troop platoon, the MGS within each
     MGS-ATGM pair identifies the target identified by the battalion scouts or Infantry companies. The PL
     verifies the battle handover report with MGS. The PL or PSG determines engagement positions and assigns
     targets to his vehicles. The PL or PSG leading the section initiates the engagement to destroy the enemy.
     Upon destroying the enemy, the weapons troop platoon retrogrades to its hide site, and A and B companies
     continue their attack. (See figure C-12, page C-26.)

11 May 2017  ATP 3-21.91  C-25

Appendix C

    Legend: HWY = highway, MGS = mobile gun system, N = north, PL = phase line, TRP = target
    reference point

                                               Figure C-12. OBJ Cat

C-99. While advancing, A company encounters an enemy ambush utilizing BMPs firing from hasty
covered and concealed positions overwatching an area of generally flat and open terrain. The 3rd SBCT
Infantry battalion commander requests support from the weapons troop. The weapons troop moves to conduct
a linkup with the 3rd SBCT Infantry battalion and then with the A company commander. The A company
commander directs the weapons troop to support the 1st SBCT Infantry rifle platoon. The weapons troop
commander conducts a linkup with the first rifle platoon leader and receives a battle handover report. Using
the information from the battle handover report, the weapons troop commander selects his firing positions
and moves his platoons to occupy them. The weapons troop commander assigns sectors of fire to the platoons.
The platoon leaders conduct a hasty reconnaissance of their positions and the suspected enemy locations.
They assign primary and alternate battle positions to their vehicles, and identify five enemy BMPs. The PLs
then assign targets within their sectors to each vehicle.

C-100. Once all platoon leaders have reported to the weapons troop commander that they are ready to
engage, the weapons troop commander initiates fires with a troop fire command. The weapons troop engages
with single, simultaneous MGS volley and destroys three BMPs. The enemy returns fire, forcing the five

C-26        ATP 3-21.91  11 May 2017


---

                                                                                               Weapons Troop Fictional Scenario

MGS to shift to their alternate positions, and revealing the location of an additional two enemy BMPs. The
weapons troop commander conducts a hasty battle damage assessment, and orders the platoons to reengage
using the five MGS from their alternate positions and one uncompromised MGS from its primary position,
destroying three additional BMPs. The losses cause the remaining four BMPs to withdraw to the east. To
ensure the security and speed of the advancing 3rd SBCT Infantry battalion, the weapons troop maintains
overwatch position while A company exits the mountainous area and enters forested area, establishing far
side security for the battalion. Once A company is set, the weapons troop conducts a battle handover with B
company of the 3rd SBCT Infantry battalion, and moves to CP R1 where it resumes its role as the SBCT
reserve. (See figure C-13.)

Legend: Co = company CP = command post, ENY = enemy, HWY = highway, TRP = target reference
point

                                     Figure C-13. Engagement on OBJ Cat

 C-101. The 3rd SBCT Infantry battalion continues the attack towards OBJ Cat. A company, moves
 dismounted through the restricted terrain supported by their ICVVs as the lead SBCT Infantry rifle company.
 The 3rd weapons platoon follows and supports A company in mixed ATGM/MGS sections. When A company
 identifies a small concrete building housing two machine guns, the company commander decides to engage

11 May 2017  ATP 3-21.91  C-27


---

Appendix C

     with an MGS. B section of 3rd weapons platoon moves into position, engages the bunker with a 105mm HEP
     round and destroys the enemy position. A company then continues its mission and advances eastward.

     C-102. At 5km from OBJ Cat, 3rd SBCT Infantry battalion moves into its assault position. 3rd weapons
     platoon’s MGS consolidates at A company’s assault position, and prepares to conduct support by fire for
     A company. 3rd weapons platoon ATGM consolidates at B company’s assault position, and prepares to follow
     and support the company. Once OBJ Cat has been taken, the 2nd SBCT Infantry battalion will transition to a
     hasty defense with B company to destroy any enemy counterattack. 3rd SBCT Infantry battalion conducts its
     deliberate attack on OBJ Cat with A company supporting by fire, and B company conducting the assault. 3rd
     weapons platoon, as a part of A company’s support by fire, focuses on engaging enemy bunkers, vehicles,
     and machine gun positions. The weapons troop platoon shifts and lifts fire in coordination with A company,
     and utilizing TRPs that account for the SDZs of their munitions while still enabling B company’s maneuver
     across the objective. Once A company’s support by fire has suppressed the enemy on OBJ Cat, B company
     begins its assault.

     C-103. Once assault begins, the enemy commits its tank platoon reserve to support OBJ Cat. B company
     rapidly bypasses the enemy’s obstacles, and clears OBJ Cat. As B company destroys the enemy positions
     along highway 1 from South to North, it seizes the crossroads at the center of OBJ Cat. Once the crossroads
     has been seized, the surviving enemy elements from OBJ Cat and the enemy elements in the northern-most
     battle position withdraw northward, breaking contact. Once B company clears OBJ Cat, the company
     transitions to a hasty defense in anticipation of an enemy counterattack. The B company commander moves
     his supporting ATGM section forward, incorporating them into his defense. He gives the ATGMs sectors of
     fire that are allow them to maximize the effective range of their TOW weapon systems along the enemy’s
     likely avenue of approach for heavy armored vehicles.

     C-104. Soon after B company sets its hasty defense of OBJ Cat, the enemy begins its counterattack. The
     enemy commits its reserve and maneuvers mechanized Infantry platoon on line towards B company’s
     positions using Highway 5 and the immediately surrounding terrain. As soon as the ATGM section identifies
     two enemy BMPs, they engage simultaneously from concealed positions on either side of Highway 5. The
     ATGM section destroys two enemy BMPs, causing the enemy’s remaining two BMPs to withdraw to the
     east. Once the counterattack is defeated, the 3rd SBCT Infantry battalion conducts reconsolidation and
     reorganization actions on the objective.

ATTACK ON OBJ DOG

     C-105. Once the 3rd SBCT Infantry battalion has breached the obstacles on OBJ Cat, the SBCT commander
     orders the commencement of the attack on OBJ Dog. 2nd SBCT Infantry battalion and C company, 3rd SBCT
     Infantry battalion, passes PL Baseball and conducts a FPOL with the 1st SBCT Infantry battalion using
     passage lane 21. The weapons troop and the 2nd SBCT Infantry battalion conduct a linkup at CP R1, and
     follows and supports the battalion along Axis Pitbull to attack position Terrier. Once consolidated at attack
     position Terrier, 2nd SBCT Infantry battalion companies move to their assault positions. The 2nd SBCT
     Infantry battalion utilizes the SBCT’s UAVs to determine that the enemy is not west of the Low River, and
     identifies several battle positions on the east side of the river. (See figure C-14.)

C-28  ATP 3-21.91  11 May 2017

  TGT = target, TRP = target reference point

                                       Figure C-14. Attack on OBJ Dog

C-106. The 2nd SBCT Infantry battalion commander directs the battalion to begin its attack once all
subordinate elements are set in their assault positions. The weapons troop moves from Hide Site E1 to Battle
Position E2 and begins suppressing the enemy on the east side of the Low River. The weapons troop has
assigned sectors of fire where 6th platoon is oriented on the enemy position near TRP 1, 2nd platoon is oriented
on TRPs 1 and 2, and 5th platoon is oriented on TRP 3. The weapons troop engages in accordance with the
commander’s engagement priorities: ATGM to tanks and MGS to BMPs and machine gun positions. The
weapons troop suppresses the enemy tank and mechanized Infantry platoon, enabling C company to occupy
its support by fire position.

C-107. Once C company has initiated its support by fire, the 2nd SBCT Infantry battalion begins breaching
operations by committing B company to breach the obstacles on the west end of high bridge. As B company
reaches the weapons troop’s support by fire positions, the weapons troop and C company shift their fires

11 May 2017  ATP 3-21.91  C-29


---

Appendix C

     away from B company and its positions. B company then sets its local support by fire positions, and executes
     its breaching operation in accordance with the breaching fundamentals. Once B company successfully creates
     a lane through the obstacle, A company conducts its assault through the breach lane, still supported by the
     weapons troop and C company, and B company from their security positions at the breach site. C company
     clears OBJ Dog from west to east. The remaining enemy forces on the OBJ to withdraw towards the east.
     Once C company has seized OBJ Dog, the weapons troop and A company pass through the breach lane to
     occupy positions on the east side of OBJ Dog and prepares for an enemy counterattack. The weapons troop
     places 6th platoon on the north side of the defensive perimeter, 2nd platoon in the center, and 5th platoon on
     the south side of the perimeter, with the weapons troop oriented eastward.

     C-108. The enemy counterattacks with a mechanized Infantry platoon moving in a wedge formation. The
     weapons troop commander intends to take advantage of the open terrain to destroy the enemy with TOW
     missiles. He issues his troop fire command to his platoons to engage with his ATGMs in a single frontal
     volley as soon as the enemy has deployed from its wedge movement formation into its line maneuver
     formation. The engagement destroys the enemy platoon, allowing the 2nd SBCT Infantry to consolidate and
     reorganize, and prepare for follow-on missions.

Glossary

  SECTION II – TERMS

adversary
     A party acknowledged as potentially hostile to a friendly party and against which the use of force may be
     envisaged (JP 3-0).

ambush
     An attack by fire, or other destructive means, from concealed positions on a moving or temporarily halted
     enemy (ADRP 3-90).

approach march
     The advance of a combat unit when direct contact with the enemy is intended (ADRP 3-90).

area reconnaissance
     A form of reconnaissance that focuses on obtained detailed information about the terrain or enemy activity
     within a prescribed area (ADRP 3-90).

area security
     A security task conducted to protect friendly forces, installations, routes, and actions within a specific area
     (ADRP 3-90).

attack
     An offensive task that destroys or defeats enemy forces, seizes and secures terrain, or both (ADRP 3-90).

class I
     Rations and gratuitous issue of health, morale, and welfare items. (DOD Dictionary)

class II
     Clothing, individual equipment, tentage, tool sets, and administrative and housekeeping supplies and
     equipment. (DOD Dictionary)

class III
     Petroleum, oils, and lubricants. (DOD Dictionary)

class IV
     Construction materials. (DOD Dictionary)

class V
     Ammunition. (DOD Dictionary)

class VI
     Personal demand items. (DOD Dictionary)

class VII
     Major end items, including tanks, helicopters, and radios. (DOD Dictionary)

class VIII
     Medical. (DOD Dictionary)

class IX
     Repair parts and components for equipment maintenance. (DOD Dictionary)

class X
     Nonstandard items to support nonmilitary programs such as agriculture and economic development. See also
     petroleum, oils, and lubricants. (DOD Dictonary)

combat power
     The total means of destructive, constructive, and information capabilities that a military unit or formation
     can apply at a given time (ADP 3-0).

Glossary-4  ATP 3-21.91  11 May 2017


---

                                                                                                                                          Glossary

conflict transformation
     The process of reducing the means and motivations for violent conflict while developing viable, peaceful
     alternatives for the competitive pursuit of political and socioeconomic aspirations. (ADRP 3-07).

cover
     A security task to protect the main body by fighting to gain time while also observing and reporting
     information and preventing enemy ground observation of and direct fire against the main body (ADRP 3-90).

defensive task
     A task conducted to defeat an enemy attack, gain time, economize forces, and develop conditions favorable
     for offensive or stability tasks (ADRP 3-0).

disengage
     A tactical mission task where the commander has the unit break contact with the enemy to allow the conduct
     of another mission or to avoid decisive engagement (FM 3-90-1).

enemy
     A party identified as hostile against which the use of force is authorized (ADRP 3-0).

engagement area
     An area where the commander intends to contain and destroy an enemy force with the massed effects of all
     available weapons and supporting systems (FM 3-90-1).

envelopment
     A form of maneuver in which an attacking force seeks to avoid the principal enemy defenses by seizing
     objectives behind those defenses that allow the targeted enemy force to be destroyed in their current positions
     (ADP 3-90).

fire support
     Fires that directly support land, maritime, amphibious, and special operations forces to engage enemy forces,
     combat formations, and facilities in pursuit of tactical and operational objectives (JP 3-09).

flank attack
     A form of offensive maneuver directed at the flank of an enemy force (FM 3-90-1).

forward observer
     An observer operating with front line troops trained to adjust ground or naval gunfire and pass back battlefield
     information (JP 3-09).

frontal attack
     A form of maneuver in which the attacking force seeks to destroy a weaker enemy force or fix a larger enemy
     force in place over a broad front (FM 3-90-1).

guard
     A security task to protect the main body by fighting to gain time while also observing and reporting
     information and to prevent enemy ground observation of and direct fire against the main body. Units
     conducting a guard mission cannot operate independently because they rely upon fires and functional and
     multifunctional support assets of the main body (ADRP 3-90).

hybrid threat
     The diverse and dynamic combination of regular forces, irregular forces, terrorist forces, or criminal elements
     unified to achieve mutually benefitting effects (ADRP 3-0).

infiltration
     A form of maneuver in which an attacking force conducts undetected movement through or into an area
     occupied by enemy forces to occupy a position of advantage in the enemy rear while exposing only small
     elements to enemy defensive fires (FM 3-90-1).

11 May 2017  ATP 3-21.91  Glossary-5


---

Glossary

intelligence warfighting function
     The related tasks and systems that facilitate understanding the enemy, terrain, weather, civil considerations,
     and other significant aspects of the operational environment. (ADRP 3-0).

joint fires observer
     A trained Service member who can request, adjust, and control surface-to-surface fires (ATP 3-09.32/
     MCRP 3-16.6A/NTTP3-09.2/AFTTP(I) 3-2.6).

leadership
     The process of influencing people by providing purpose, direction, and motivation, to accomplish the mission
     and improve the organization (ADP 6-22).

local security
     A security task that includes low-level security activities conducted near a unit to prevent surprise by the
     enemy (ADRP 3-90).

logistics
     Planning and executing the movement and support of forces (JP 4-0).

mission command warfighting function
     The related tasks and systems that develop and integrate those activities enabling a commander to balance
     the art of command and the science of control in order to integrate the other warfighting functions
     (ADRP 3-0).

movement and maneuver warfighting function
     The related tasks and systems that move and employ forces to achieve a position of relative advantage over
     the enemy and other threats (ADRP 3-0).

movement to contact
     An offensive task designed to develop the situation and establish or regain contact (ADRP 3-90).

neutral
     A party identified as neither supporting nor opposing friendly or enemy forces (JP 3-0).

operation
     A sequence of tactical actions with a common purpose or unifying theme (JP 1).

operational environment
     A composite of the conditions, circumstances, and influences that affect the employment of capabilities and
     bear on the decisions of the commander (JP 3-0).

passage of lines
     An operation in which a force moves forward or rearward through another force’s positions with the intention
     of moving into or out of contact with the enemy. (JP 3-18).

penetration
     A form of maneuver in which an attacking force seeks to rupture enemy defenses on a narrow front to disrupt
     the defensive system (FM 3-90-1).

protection warfighting function
     The related tasks and systems that preserve the force so the commander can apply maximum combat power
     to accomplish the mission (ADRP 3-0).

reconnaissance
     A mission undertaken to obtain, by visual observation or other detection methods, information about the
     activities and resources of an enemy or adversary, or to secure data concerning the meteorological,
     hydrographical, or geographical characteristics and the indigenous population of a particular area (JP 2-0).

Glossary-6  ATP 3-21.91  11 May 2017


---

                                                                                                                                          Glossary

reconnaissance in force
     A deliberate combat operation designed to discover or test the enemy’s strength, dispositions, and reactions
     or to obtain other information (ADRP 3-90).

reconnaissance pull
     Used when commanders are uncertain of the composition and disposition of threat forces in their areas of
     operation, information concerning terrain is vague, and time is limited (FM 3-98).

reconnaissance push
     Used when commanders have a relatively thorough understanding of the operational environment (FM 3-98).

retrograde
     A type of defense task that involves organized movement away from the enemy (ADRP 3-90).

route reconnaissance
     A directed effort to obtain detailed information of a specified route and all terrain from which the enemy
     could influence movement along that route (ADRP 3-90).

screen
     A security task that primarily provides early warning to the protected force (ADRP 3-90).

security operations
     Those operations undertaken by the commander to provide early and accurate warning of enemy operations,
     to provide the force being protected with time and maneuver space within which to react to the enemy, and
     to develop the situation to allow the commander to effectively use the protected force (ADRP 3-90).

Soldier and leader engagement
     Interpersonal interactions by Soldiers and leaders with audiences in an area of operations (FM 3-13).

spoiling attack
     A tactical maneuver employed to seriously impair a hostile attack while the enemy is forming or assembling
     for an attack (FM 3-90-1).

stability operations
     An overarching term encompassing various military missions, tasks, and activities conducted outside the
     United States in coordination with other instruments of national power to maintain or reestablish a safe and
     secure environment, provide essential governmental services, emergency infrastructure reconstruction, and
     humanitarian relief (JP 3-0.)

stability tasks
     Tasks conducted as part of operations outside the United States in coordination with other instruments of
     national power to maintain or reestablish a safe and secure environment and provide essential governmental
     services, emergency infrastructure reconstruction, and humanitarian relief. (ADP 3-07).

sustainment
     The provision of the logistics, personnel services, and health service support necessary to maintain operations
     until successful mission completion (ADRP 4-0).

sustainment warfighting function
     The related tasks and systems that provide support and services to ensure freedom of action, extend
     operational reach, and prolong endurance (ADRP 3-0).

tactical road march
     A rapid movement used to relocate units within an area of operations to prepare for combat operations
     (ADRP 3-90).

threat
     Any combination of actors, entities, or forces that have the capability and intent to harm United States forces,
     United States national interests, or the homeland (ADRP 3-0).

11 May 2017  ATP 3-21.91  Glossary-7


---

Glossary

troop-leading procedures
     A dynamic process used by small-unit leaders to analyze a mission, develop a plan, and prepare for an
     operation (ADP 5-0).

troop movement
     The movement of troops from one place to another by any available means (ADRP 3-90).

turning movement
     A form of maneuver in which the attacking force seeks to avoid the enemy’s principle defensive positions by
     seizing objectives behind the enemy’s current positions thereby causing the enemy force to move out of their
     current positions or divert major forces to meet the threat (FM 3-90-1).

unified action
     The synchronization, coordination, and/or integration of the activities of governmental and nongovernmental
     entities with military operations to achieve unity of effort (JP 1).

unified action partners
     Those military forces, governmental and nongovernmental organizations, and elements of the private sector
     with whom Army forces plan, coordinate, synchronize, and integrate during the conduct of operations.
     (ADRP 3-0).

unified land operations
     Are simultaneous offensive, defensive, and stability or defense support of civil authorities tasks to seize,
     retain, and exploit the initiative and consolidate gains to prevent conflict, shape the operational environment,
     and win our Nation’s wars as part of unified action (ADRP 3-0).

unity of effort
     The coordination and cooperation toward common objectives, even if the participants are not necessarily part
     of the same command or organization, which is the product of successful unified action (JP 1).

warfighting function
     A group of tasks and systems united by a common purpose that commanders use to accomplish missions and
     training objectives (ADRP 3-0).

zone reconnaissance
     A form of reconnaissance that involves a directed effort to obtain detailed information on all routes, obstacles,
     terrain, and enemy forces within a zone defined by boundaries (ADRP 3-90).

Glossary-8  ATP 3-21.91  11 May 2017
